## I / 一

===Original===
For reasons which many persons thought ridiculous, Mrs. Lightfoot Lee decided to pass the winter in Washington. She was in excellent health, but she said that the climate would do her good. In New York she had troops of friends, but she suddenly became eager to see again the very small number of those who lived on the Potomac. It was only to her closest intimates that she honestly acknowledged herself to be tortured by ennui. Since her husband’s death, five years before, she had lost her taste for New York society; she had felt no interest in the price of stocks, and very little in the men who dealt in them; she had become serious. What was it all worth, this wilderness of men and women as monotonous as the brownstone houses they lived in? In her despair she had resorted to desperate measures. She had read philosophy in the original German, and the more she read, the more she was disheartened that so much culture should lead to nothing﻿—nothing. After talking of Herbert Spencer for an entire evening with a very literary transcendental commission-merchant, she could not see that her time had been better employed than when in former days she had passed it in flirting with a very agreeable young stockbroker; indeed, there was an evident proof to the contrary, for the flirtation might lead to something﻿—had, in fact, led to marriage; while the philosophy could lead to nothing, unless it were perhaps to another evening of the same kind, because transcendental philosophers are mostly elderly men, usually married, and, when engaged in business, somewhat apt to be sleepy towards evening. Nevertheless Mrs. Lee did her best to turn her study to practical use. She plunged into philanthropy, visited prisons, inspected hospitals, read the literature of pauperism and crime, saturated herself with the statistics of vice, until her mind had nearly lost sight of virtue. At last it rose in rebellion against her, and she came to the limit of her strength. This path, too, seemed to lead nowhere. She declared that she had lost the sense of duty, and that, so far as concerned her, all the paupers and criminals in New York might henceforward rise in their majesty and manage every railway on the continent. Why should she care? What was the city to her? She could find nothing in it that seemed to demand salvation. What gave peculiar sanctity to numbers? Why were a million people, who all resembled each other, any way more interesting than one person? What aspiration could she help to put into the mind of this great million-armed monster that would make it worth her love or respect? Religion? A thousand powerful churches were doing their best, and she could see no chance for a new faith of which she was to be the inspired prophet. Ambition? High popular ideals? Passion for whatever is lofty and pure? The very words irritated her. Was she not herself devoured by ambition, and was she not now eating her heart out because she could find no one object worth a sacrifice?
===Chinese===
出于不少人都觉得可笑的缘故，莱特富特·李夫人（Mrs. Lightfoot Lee）决定去华盛顿过冬。她身子骨好得很，却说那边的气候于她有益。在纽约，她朋友成群，如今却忽然热切起来，想再见一见住在波托马克河畔的那寥寥几位故人。唯有对最贴心的密友，她才坦然承认自己正饱受倦怠（ennui）的折磨。自五年前丈夫去世，她对纽约社交界便再也提不起兴致；股票行情引不起她半点兴趣，做股票生意的人更引不起多少；她变得严肃了。这一切到底有什么意思——这一片男男女女的荒原，单调得一如他们栖身的褐石住宅？绝望之下，她使出了孤注一掷的手段。她啃起哲学著作的德文原著，谁知越读越灰心：这么多的文化教养，到头来竟通向虚无——虚无。同一位满身文学气的超验论经纪商畅谈了一整晚赫伯特·斯宾塞（Herbert Spencer）之后，她看不出这个晚上，比当年同一个极讨人喜欢的年轻股票经纪人调情厮混的时光用得更值；非但如此，反倒有现成的反证：调情好歹还能通向点什么——事实上就通向过一桩婚姻；哲学却什么也通向不了，顶多再通向一个如出一辙的夜晚，因为超验论哲学家多半是上了年纪的男人，通常早成了家，经商之余，一到傍晚便难免瞌睡阵阵。话虽如此，李夫人还是竭力想叫自己的学问派上实际用场。她一头扎进慈善事业：探访监狱，视察医院，研读济贫与罪案的文献，把形形色色罪恶的统计数字灌满头脑，直到那里面几乎再也见不着美德。到头来，头脑起而造反，她也熬到了气力的尽头。这条路，看来同样走不到哪里去。她宣称自己丧失了责任感，还说：就她而论，纽约全城的贫民与罪犯尽可以从此庄严崛起，接管大陆上的每一条铁路。关她什么事？这座城于她算得了什么？她在这里面找不到一样东西，看起来是非拯救不可的。数目究竟有什么特别的神圣？一百万个彼此一个模样的凡人，凭什么就比一个凡人更有趣味？她能往这头百万臂膀的巨怪头脑里灌进什么高远的志向，叫它当得起她的爱、她的敬？宗教吗？上千座财雄势大的教堂正在各显神通，她看不出还留得出什么空位，好由她来做一门新信仰的受神启的先知。雄心吗？风行一时的高尚理想吗？对一切崇高纯洁之物的热忱吗？这些字眼本身就把她惹恼了。她自己何尝不正在被雄心啃噬，此刻又何尝不因为寻不出一件值得牺牲的东西而忧心如焚？

===Original===
Was it ambition﻿—real ambition﻿—or was it mere restlessness that made Mrs. Lightfoot Lee so bitter against New York and Philadelphia, Baltimore and Boston, American life in general and all life in particular? What did she want? Not social position, for she herself was an eminently respectable Philadelphian by birth; her father a famous clergyman; and her husband had been equally irreproachable, a descendant of one branch of the Virginia Lees, which had drifted to New York in search of fortune, and had found it, or enough of it to keep the young man there. His widow had her own place in society which no one disputed. Though not brighter than her neighbours, the world persisted in classing her among clever women; she had wealth, or at least enough of it to give her all that money can give by way of pleasure to a sensible woman in an American city; she had her house and her carriage; she dressed well; her table was good, and her furniture was never allowed to fall behind the latest standard of decorative art. She had travelled in Europe, and after several visits, covering some years of time, had returned home, carrying in one hand, as it were, a green-grey landscape, a remarkably pleasing specimen of Corot, and in the other some bales of Persian and Syrian rugs and embroideries, Japanese bronzes and porcelain. With this she declared Europe to be exhausted, and she frankly avowed that she was American to the tips of her fingers; she neither knew nor greatly cared whether America or Europe were best to live in; she had no violent love for either, and she had no objection to abusing both; but she meant to get all that American life had to offer, good or bad, and to drink it down to the dregs, fully determined that whatever there was in it she would have, and that whatever could be made out of it she would manufacture. “I know,” said she, “that America produces petroleum and pigs; I have seen both on the steamers; and I am told it produces silver and gold. There is choice enough for any woman.”

Yet, as has been already said, Mrs. Lee’s first experience was not a success. She soon declared that New York might represent the petroleum or the pigs, but the gold of life was not to be discovered there by her eyes. Not but that there was variety enough; a variety of people, occupations, aims, and thoughts; but that all these, after growing to a certain height, stopped short. They found nothing to hold them up. She knew, more or less intimately, a dozen men whose fortunes ranged between one million and forty millions. What did they do with their money? What could they do with it that was different from what other men did? After all, it is absurd to spend more money than is enough to satisfy all one’s wants; it is vulgar to live in two houses in the same street, and to drive six horses abreast. Yet, after setting aside a certain income sufficient for all one’s wants, what was to be done with the rest? To let it accumulate was to own one’s failure; Mrs. Lee’s great grievance was that it did accumulate, without changing or improving the quality of its owners. To spend it in charity and public works was doubtless praiseworthy, but was it wise? Mrs. Lee had read enough political economy and pauper reports to be nearly convinced that public work should be public duty, and that great benefactions do harm as well as good. And even supposing it spent on these objects, how could it do more than increase and perpetuate that same kind of human nature which was her great grievance? Her New York friends could not meet this question except by falling back upon their native commonplaces, which she recklessly trampled upon, averring that, much as she admired the genius of the famous traveller, Mr. Gulliver, she never had been able, since she became a widow, to accept the Brobdingnagian doctrine that he who made two blades of grass grow where only one grew before deserved better of mankind than the whole race of politicians. She would not find fault with the philosopher had he required that the grass should be of an improved quality; “but,” said she, “I cannot honestly pretend that I should be pleased to see two New York men where I now see one; the idea is too ridiculous; more than one and a half would be fatal to me.”
===Chinese===
究竟是雄心——真正的雄心——还是单纯的心神不宁，叫莱特富特·李夫人对纽约这般怨毒，对费城、巴尔的摩、波士顿，对泛泛的美国生活、对具体而微的全部人生这般愤懑不平？她究竟要什么？不是社会地位，因为她自己就出身极其体面的费城世家：父亲是位有名的牧师；丈夫同样无可指摘，是弗吉尼亚李氏家族一支的后裔——那一支当年为谋财路漂到纽约，财也真谋到了，至少多到足以把这位青年留在那里。遗孀自有社交地位，无人置疑。她并不比左邻右舍更聪明，世人却偏要把她归入聪明女人之列；她有钱，至少够叫一个明白事理的女人在美国的城市里享尽金钱所能买到的种种欢愉；她有自己的宅邸和马车；衣着考究；餐桌上乘；家具也从不容落伍于装饰艺术的最新标准。她游历过欧洲，几度前往，前后历时数年，回到家里，一手仿佛拎着一幅青灰色的风景——柯罗（Corot）一幅分外悦目的佳构——一手挽着几大包波斯与叙利亚的地毯、绣品，日本的铜器与瓷器。置办下这些，她便宜布欧洲已被她用罄，并坦然自认是彻头彻尾、连指尖都是美国化的女人；美国与欧洲究竟哪里更宜于居住，她既不知道，也不大在乎；她对哪一边都没有炽烈的爱，也不反对把两边都痛骂一顿；但她打定主意，美国生活所能供奉的一切，无论好坏，她都要领受，都要连酒带渣喝个滴酒不剩；这里面无论有什么，她都要弄到手，无论能从中造出什么，她都要亲手造出来。“我知道，”她说，“美国出产石油和猪，这两样我在轮船上都见过；还听说出产金银。对随便哪个女人，这份选择尽够了。”

然而，如前所说，李夫人的头一番阅历并不成功。她不久便宜布，纽约尽可以去代表石油或者猪，可生活的黄金，凭她这双眼睛在那里是发现不了的。倒不是花样不够多；人物、职业、志向、思想，样样有花样；只是这一切长到某个高度便齐齐打住，再也找不着什么能把它们撑住。她认识一打男子，交情深浅不一，家产从一百万到四千万不等。他们拿钱做什么？又能做出什么与旁人不同的名堂？说到底，花钱超过满足一切所需的本属荒唐；同一条街上住两处宅子、驾六匹马并辔而行，也未免俗气。然而，留出一份足以应付一切所需的进项之后，其余的又当如何处置？听任它堆积，无异于自认失败；李夫人最大的苦楚，正在于那钱一味堆积，而主人的品性却分毫不曾改变、不曾长进。把钱散在慈善与公共事业上固然可敬，可是明智吗？李夫人读过的政治经济学与济贫报告，已足以叫她差不多信服：公共事务本当出于公共义务，而巨额捐助既行善也作恶。退一步，就算钱都花在这些名目上，它又能多做出什么？无非把那种最令她痛心的同样的人性，加倍繁殖下去、延续下去罢了。她的纽约朋友接不住这一问题，只会退回各自的老生常谈，而她便不管不顾地一脚踏过去，扬言：那位大名鼎鼎的旅行家格列佛先生（Mr. Gulliver）的天才，她纵然钦佩之至，可自打守寡以来，她始终没能接受大人国（Brobdingnag）的那条教义——说是叫两茎草在原先只生一茎的地方长出来的人，于人类的功劳竟胜过全天下的政客。倘若那位哲学家还要求那草的品种务须改良，她倒不至于怪他；“可是，”她说，“要我在如今只见一个纽约人的地方看见两个，还要我由衷欢喜，这话我说不出口；这念头太可笑了；超过一个半，就够要我的命了。”

===Original===
Then came her Boston friends, who suggested that higher education was precisely what she wanted; she should throw herself into a crusade for universities and art-schools. Mrs. Lee turned upon them with a sweet smile; “Do you know,” said she, “that we have in New York already the richest university in America, and that its only trouble has always been that it can get no scholars even by paying for them? Do you want me to go out into the streets and waylay boys? If the heathen refuse to be converted, can you give me power over the stake and the sword to compel them to come in? And suppose you can? Suppose I march all the boys in Fifth Avenue down to the university and have them all properly taught Greek and Latin, English literature, ethics, and German philosophy. What then? You do it in Boston. Now tell me honestly what comes of it. I suppose you have there a brilliant society; numbers of poets, scholars, philosophers, statesmen, all up and down Beacon Street. Your evenings must be sparkling. Your press must scintillate. How is it that we New Yorkers never hear of it? We don’t go much into your society; but when we do, it doesn’t seem so very much better than our own. You are just like the rest of us. You grow six inches high, and then you stop. Why will not somebody grow to be a tree and cast a shadow?”

The average member of New York society, although not unused to this contemptuous kind of treatment from his leaders, retaliated in his blind, commonsense way. “What does the woman want?” he said. “Is her head turned with the Tuileries and Marlborough House? Does she think herself made for a throne? Why does she not lecture for women’s rights? Why not go on the stage? If she cannot be contented like other people, what need is there for abusing us just because she feels herself no taller than we are? What does she expect to get from her sharp tongue? What does she know, anyway?”
===Chinese===
接着登场的是她那班波士顿朋友，他们进言说，高等教育正是她所要的；她该投身一场兴办大学与艺术学校的十字军运动。李夫人报以甜甜的一笑，迎头反击：“你们可知道，”她说，“我们纽约已经有了全美国最有钱的大学，而它独一无二的老大难，恰恰是出钱都招不来学生？你们是要我上街去半路拦截男孩子吗？异教徒不肯皈依，你们能授我火刑柱与刀剑之权，逼他们进来吗？就算能吧？就算我把第五大道的男孩子统统列队押进大学，叫他们规规矩矩受教希腊文、拉丁文、英国文学、伦理学和德国哲学，然后呢？这些事你们在波士顿样样都做了。如今老实告诉我，做出了什么名堂。想必你们那儿有一派煜煜生辉的社会；成群的诗人、学者、哲学家、政治家，比肯街（Beacon Street）上上下下站满了。你们的夜晚想必妙语如珠，你们的报章想必光焰逼人。可怎么我们纽约人竟从没听见过一点风声？我们不大进你们的圈子；偶尔进去一回，也并不觉得比我们自己的高出多少。你们同我们这些人一模一样：长到六英寸高，就不肯再长了。怎么就没有人肯长成一棵树，好投下一片荫凉呢？”

纽约社交界的寻常一员，对领袖人物们这等居高临下的白眼虽说见惯不怪，仍以他自己那种盲目的、凭常识的路数回敬几句。“这女人到底想要什么？”他说。“是不是被杜伊勒里宫和马尔堡府（Marlborough House）迷昏了头？自以为生来该坐宝座？她怎么不去为女权作演讲？怎么不登台演戏？倘若她不能像旁人一样安分知足，只因为她自觉并不比我们高明，又何苦把我们糟蹋个没完？她指望她那张利嘴讨着什么便宜？再说，她到底懂得点什么？”

===Original===
Mrs. Lee certainly knew very little. She had read voraciously and promiscuously one subject after another. Ruskin and Taine had danced merrily through her mind, hand in hand with Darwin and Stuart Mill, Gustave Droz and Algernon Swinburne. She had even laboured over the literature of her own country. She was perhaps, the only woman in New York who knew something of American history. Certainly she could not have repeated the list of Presidents in their order, but she knew that the Constitution divided the government into Executive, Legislative, and Judiciary; she was aware that the President, the Speaker, and the Chief Justice were important personages, and instinctively she wondered whether they might not solve her problem; whether they were the shade trees which she saw in her dreams.

Here, then, was the explanation of her restlessness, discontent, ambition﻿—call it what you will. It was the feeling of a passenger on an ocean steamer whose mind will not give him rest until he has been in the engine-room and talked with the engineer. She wanted to see with her own eyes the action of primary forces; to touch with her own hand the massive machinery of society; to measure with her own mind the capacity of the motive power. She was bent upon getting to the heart of the great American mystery of democracy and government. She cared little where her pursuit might lead her, for she put no extravagant value upon life, having already, as she said, exhausted at least two lives, and being fairly hardened to insensibility in the process. “To lose a husband and a baby,” said she, “and keep one’s courage and reason, one must become very hard or very soft. I am now pure steel. You may beat my heart with a trip-hammer and it will beat the trip-hammer back again.”
===Chinese===
李夫人懂得的实在有限。她博览成癖、不辨门类，一门科目接一门科目地读了下去。罗斯金（Ruskin）与泰纳（Taine）曾在她脑海里翩然起舞，同达尔文（Darwin）、斯图尔特·密尔（Stuart Mill）、古斯塔夫·德罗（Gustave Droz）和阿尔杰农·斯温伯恩（Algernon Swinburne）手挽着手。她甚至下苦功读过自己国家的文学。她多半是纽约城里唯一对美国历史略知一二的女人。当然，历届总统按次序背出来，她是办不到的；但她知道宪法把政府分作行政、立法、司法三权；她晓得总统、议长、首席大法官都是了不得的人物；于是本能地暗自纳闷：他们会不会恰好解得开她的难题；会不会正是她梦中所见的那几棵亭亭如盖的大树。

于是，她的躁动、不满、雄心——随你怎么称呼——便都有了着落。这好比远洋轮船上一位乘客的心绪：不亲自下到机舱、同工程师谈上一谈，那颗心便不得安生。她要亲眼看看原初之力怎样施展；亲手摸一摸社会这部庞大的机器；用自己的头脑掂一掂那动力究竟有多大的容量。她一门心思，要探到民主与政府这个美国莫大奥秘的底里。这条追求之路会把人引向何处，她并不放在心上，因为她对生命素来不作过高的估价：用她自己的话说，她至少已经耗尽了两世性命，在这过程里也炼得心硬如铁、麻木不仁了。“没了丈夫，又没了婴孩，”她说，“还要守住勇气和理智，人不变得极硬，就得变得极软。我如今是纯钢打的。你们尽可以拿汽锤去砸我的心，它会把汽锤砸回来。”

===Original===
Perhaps after exhausting the political world she might try again elsewhere; she did not pretend to say where she might then go, or what she should do; but at present she meant to see what amusement there might be in politics. Her friends asked what kind of amusement she expected to find among the illiterate swarm of ordinary people who in Washington represented constituencies so dreary that in comparison New York was a New Jerusalem, and Broad Street a grove of Academe. She replied that if Washington society were so bad as this, she should have gained all she wanted, for it would be a pleasure to return﻿—precisely the feeling she longed for. In her own mind, however, she frowned on the idea of seeking for men. What she wished to see, she thought, was the clash of interests, the interests of forty millions of people and a whole continent, centering at Washington; guided, restrained, controlled, or unrestrained and uncontrollable, by men of ordinary mould; the tremendous forces of government, and the machinery of society, at work. What she wanted, was **Power**.

Perhaps the force of the engine was a little confused in her mind with that of the engineer, the power with the men who wielded it. Perhaps the human interest of politics was after all what really attracted her, and, however strongly she might deny it, the passion for exercising power, for its own sake, might dazzle and mislead a woman who had exhausted all the ordinary feminine resources. But why speculate about her motives? The stage was before her, the curtain was rising, the actors were ready to enter; she had only to go quietly on among the supernumeraries and see how the play was acted and the stage effects were produced; how the great tragedians mouthed, and the stage-manager swore.
===Chinese===
也许把政界榨干之后，她还会到别处再试运气；那时会上哪里、会做些什么，她并不佯称知道；但眼下，她只想看看政治里面究竟有几分乐趣可寻。朋友们问，华盛顿那群代表各选区的目不识丁的平民百姓，沉闷乏味得相形之下纽约竟成了新耶路撒冷、宽街（Broad Street）竟成了学园的树林，她指望在这样一群人里寻到怎样的乐趣。她答道，倘若华盛顿社交界真坏到这个地步，她想要的可就全数到手了，因为回家本身便是一桩乐事——这正是她渴求已久的感觉。然而在她自己心里，她并不想去找什么男人。她想看的，她想，是利益的交锋：四千万人与整个大陆的利益汇于华盛顿一点，由一班寻常材料铸成的男人们去引导、钳制、驾驭——或者放任自流、无从驾驭；是政府的雷霆万钧之力、社会这部机器，开足马力运转。她想要的，是**权力（Power）**。

或许在她心里，引擎的力量与工程师的力量，权力与操掌权力的人，已有点纠缠难分。或许政治里面人的兴味，到头来才是真正吸引她的东西；而任凭她抵死不认，那种为权力而权力的行使之欲，也尽够叫一个把寻常女性的看家本领耗尽了的女人眼花缭乱、误入歧途。可是，何必再去推敲她的动机呢？舞台就在眼前，大幕正在升起，演员们整装待发；她只消悄悄混在龙套的行列里上场，看这出戏怎样演、舞台效果怎样造出来；看那些伟大的悲剧演员怎样拿腔作势，舞台监督怎样破口大骂。

## II / 二

===Original===
On the first of December, Mrs. Lee took the train for Washington, and before five o’clock that evening she was entering her newly hired house on Lafayette Square. She shrugged her shoulders with a mingled expression of contempt and grief at the curious barbarism of the curtains and the wallpapers, and her next two days were occupied with a life-and-death struggle to get the mastery over her surroundings. In this awful contest the interior of the doomed house suffered as though a demon were in it; not a chair, not a mirror, not a carpet, was left untouched, and in the midst of the worst confusion the new mistress sat, calm as the statue of Andrew Jackson in the square under her eyes, and issued her orders with as much decision as that hero had ever shown. Towards the close of the second day, victory crowned her forehead. A new era, a nobler conception of duty and existence, had dawned upon that benighted and heathen residence. The wealth of Syria and Persia was poured out upon the melancholy Wilton carpets; embroidered comets and woven gold from Japan and Teheran depended from and covered over every sad stuff-curtain; a strange medley of sketches, paintings, fans, embroideries, and porcelain was hung, nailed, pinned, or stuck against the wall; finally the domestic altarpiece, the mystical Corot landscape, was hoisted to its place over the parlour fire, and then all was over. The setting sun streamed softly in at the windows, and peace reigned in that redeemed house and in the heart of its mistress.
===Chinese===
十二月一日，李夫人乘上开往华盛顿的火车；当天傍晚五点不到，她已踏进拉斐特广场（Lafayette Square）上自己新赁的宅子。望着窗帘与墙纸那股古怪的野蛮气，她耸了耸肩，一脸又轻蔑又沉痛的神色；随后两天，她便投入一场生死攸关的苦斗，务求降服周遭的一切。在这场可怕的交战里，那座在劫难逃的宅子的内室饱受荼毒，仿佛有恶魔住进了体内；没有一把椅子、一面镜子、一块地毯免于翻动。而在一片极度的狼藉中央，新女主人端坐不动，沉静得一如她眼底广场上那尊安德鲁·杰克逊（Andrew Jackson）的雕像，发号施令，决断之果决不让那位英雄半分。第二天日暮，胜利加上了她的额头。一个新的纪元、一种对义务与生存更高贵的观念，已在那座蒙昧异教的宅邸里破晓。叙利亚与波斯的财富，倾泻在愁惨的威尔顿地毯上；日本与德黑兰来的绣彗星与织金，从每一幅寒素的布帘上垂挂下来，又将其覆掩；素描、油画、折扇、绣品、瓷器，五花八门地聚作一处，或挂、或钉、或别、或支在墙上；末了，那件家宅的祭坛画——那幅神秘的柯罗风景——被高高吊上客厅壁炉上方属于它的位置，于是一切都宣告完结。夕阳柔柔地从窗口流泻进来，在这座得了救赎的宅子里、在女主人的心上，一片安宁君临一切。

===Original===
“I think it will do now, Sybil,” said she, surveying the scene.

“It must,” replied Sybil. “You haven’t a plate or a fan or coloured scarf left. You must send out and buy some of these old negro-women’s bandanas if you are going to cover anything else. What *is* the use? Do you suppose any human being in Washington will like it? They will think you demented.”

“There is such a thing as self-respect,” replied her sister, calmly.
===Chinese===
“我看这回总算是行了，西比尔（Sybil）。”她环视着这满屋气象，说道。

“不行也得行了，”西比尔回答。“你手边的盘子、扇子、彩披巾，一件也不剩了。还想再遮盖什么的话，只好打发人出去，买几块此地老黑人女人扎的花头巾来。这究竟图个什么？你以为华盛顿会有一个活人喜欢这套布置？人家只当你疯了。”

“自尊这东西，终究是有的。”做姐姐的平心静气地答道。

===Original===
Sybil﻿—Miss Sybil Ross﻿—was Madeleine Lee’s sister. The keenest psychologist could not have detected a single feature quality which they had in common, and for that reason they were devoted friends. Madeleine was thirty, Sybil twenty-four. Madeleine was indescribable; Sybil was transparent. Madeleine was of medium height with a graceful figure, a well-set head, and enough golden-brown hair to frame a face full of varying expression. Her eyes were never for two consecutive hours of the same shade, but were more often blue than grey. People who envied her smile said that she cultivated a sense of humour in order to show her teeth. Perhaps they were right; but there was no doubt that her habit of talking with gesticulation would never have grown upon her unless she had known that her hands were not only beautiful but expressive. She dressed as skilfully as New York women do, but in growing older she began to show symptoms of dangerous unconventionality. She had been heard to express a low opinion of her countrywomen who blindly fell down before the golden calf of Mr. Worth, and she had even fought a battle of great severity, while it lasted, with one of her best-dressed friends who had been invited﻿—and had gone﻿—to Mr. Worth’s afternoon tea-parties. The secret was that Mrs. Lee had artistic tendencies, and unless they were checked in time, there was no knowing what might be the consequence. But as yet they had done no harm; indeed, they rather helped to give her that sort of atmosphere which belongs only to certain women; as indescribable as the afterglow; as impalpable as an Indian summer mist; and nonexistent except to people who feel rather than reason. Sybil had none of it. The imagination gave up all attempts to soar where she came. A more straightforward, downright, gay, sympathetic, shallow, warmhearted, sternly practical young woman has rarely touched this planet. Her mind had room for neither gravestones nor guidebooks; she could not have lived in the past or the future if she had spent her days in churches and her nights in tombs. “She was not clever, like Madeleine, thank Heaven.” Madeleine was not an orthodox member of the church; sermons bored her, and clergymen never failed to irritate every nerve in her excitable system. Sybil was a simple and devout worshipper at the ritualistic altar; she bent humbly before the Paulist fathers. When she went to a ball she always had the best partner in the room, and took it as a matter of course; but then, she always prayed for one; somehow it strengthened her faith. Her sister took care never to laugh at her on this score, or to shock her religious opinions. “Time enough,” said she, “for her to forget religion when religion fails her.” As for regular attendance at church, Madeleine was able to reconcile their habits without trouble. She herself had not entered a church for years; she said it gave her unchristian feelings; but Sybil had a voice of excellent quality, well trained and cultivated: Madeleine insisted that she should sing in the choir, and by this little manoeuvre, the divergence of their paths was made less evident. Madeleine did not sing, and therefore could not go to church with Sybil. This outrageous fallacy seemed perfectly to answer its purpose, and Sybil accepted it, in good faith, as a fair working principle which explained itself.
===Chinese===
西比尔——西比尔·罗斯小姐（Miss Sybil Ross）——是马德琳·李（Madeleine Lee）的妹妹。再敏锐的心理学家，也捉不出姐妹俩共有的半点品貌；也正因如此，她们是彼此倾心的挚友。马德琳三十岁，西比尔二十四。马德琳难以名状，西比尔一览无余。马德琳中等身量，体态婀娜，头型端正，一头金褐色的头发，浓淡恰好框住那张神情百变的脸。她的眼睛从没有连着两个钟头是同一种色泽，只是蓝的时候多，灰的时候少。嫉妒她笑容的人说，她培养幽默感，为的是露一嘴好牙。这些人也许没说错；不过她那说话必打手势的习惯，若不是自知这双手不但美丽、而且富于表情，断然不会养成。她穿衣的功夫不输纽约女子，只是年岁渐长，开始显出几分危险的离经叛道。有人听她贬责过本国那些盲目拜倒在沃思先生（Mr. Worth）金牛犊脚下的女同胞；她甚至同一位挚友——那位受了邀请、也当真去了沃思先生午后茶会的衣冠楚楚的朋友——打过一场当时颇为吃紧的硬仗。症结在于：李夫人天生有艺术倾向，若不及时弹压，天晓得会酿出什么后果。不过迄今尚未成害；反倒给她平添了一种唯有某类女人才有的气韵：像落日余晖一样难以名状，像小阳春的薄雾一样无从把捉，只有凭感受而不凭推理的人，才知觉它的存在。西比尔全然没有这份气韵。有她在场，想象力便放弃了一切高飞的尝试。比她更直截、更痛快、更快活、更热诚、更浅薄、更热心肠、更死心塌地讲求实际的年轻女子，这颗星球上罕曾有过。她的头脑里既容不下墓碑，也容不下旅行指南；纵然白日里泡在教堂、黑夜里睡在坟茔，她也活不进过去或未来里去。“她不像马德琳那样聪明，谢天谢地。”马德琳并非教会的正统信徒；布道叫她昏昏欲睡，牧师们则没有一次不把她那套易激动的神经一一惹怒。西比尔却是仪轨祭坛前一个单纯而虔敬的礼拜者；她在保禄会神父（Paulist fathers）面前谦卑俯首。她去赴舞会，全场最好的舞伴总落在她手里，而她视作理所当然；不过呢，她回回都为此祈祷；不知怎么，这倒加固了她的信心。做姐姐的在这一点上从不取笑她，也从不惊犯她的宗教见解。“等宗教帮不上她的时候，”她说，“她有的是工夫忘掉宗教。”至于按时上教堂，马德琳不费吹灰之力便调停了两人的习惯。她自己多年不进教堂，说是教堂叫她生出非基督教的感情；而西比尔天生一副上好的嗓音，训练有素、教养得法：马德琳执意要她进唱诗班，靠着这一小小的权谋，两人路径的分歧便不那样显眼了。马德琳不唱诗，因此便不能陪西比尔上教堂。这个骇人听闻的谬见居然恰到好处地尽到了本分，西比尔也诚心诚意，把它当作一条不证自明、行之有效的好原则接受下来。

===Original===
Madeleine was sober in her tastes. She wasted no money. She made no display. She walked rather than drove, and wore neither diamonds nor brocades. But the general impression she made was nevertheless one of luxury. On the other hand, her sister had her dresses from Paris, and wore them and her ornaments according to all the formulas; she was good-naturedly correct, and bent her round white shoulders to whatever burden the Parisian autocrat chose to put upon them. Madeleine never interfered, and always paid the bills.
===Chinese===
马德琳趣味素净。她不糟蹋钱，不事张扬。出门宁可步行，不坐马车；身上既无钻石，也无锦缎。然而她给人的总印象，却依然是奢华。妹妹则不然：衣裙一律出自巴黎，穿着衣裙、佩戴饰物，处处恪守成法；她好脾气地循规蹈矩，把那双圆润白皙的肩膀，俯就巴黎的专制君主高兴压上去的任何重负。马德琳从不干涉，账单却总是照付。

===Original===
Before they had been ten days in Washington, they fell gently into their place and were carried along without an effort on the stream of social life. Society was kind; there was no reason for its being otherwise. Mrs. Lee and her sister had no enemies, held no offices, and did their best to make themselves popular. Sybil had not passed summers at Newport and winters in New York in vain; and neither her face nor her figure, her voice nor her dancing, needed apology. Politics were not her strong point. She was induced to go once to the Capitol and to sit ten minutes in the gallery of the Senate. No one ever knew what her impressions were; with feminine tact she managed not to betray herself. But, in truth, her notion of legislative bodies was vague, floating between her experience at church and at the opera, so that the idea of a performance of some kind was never out of her head. To her mind the Senate was a place where people went to recite speeches, and she naively assumed that the speeches were useful and had a purpose, but as they did not interest her she never went again. This is a very common conception of Congress; many Congressmen share it.

Her sister was more patient and bolder. She went to the Capitol nearly every day for at least two weeks. At the end of that time her interest began to flag, and she thought it better to read the debates every morning in the *Congressional Record*. Finding this a laborious and not always an instructive task, she began to skip the dull parts; and in the absence of any exciting question, she at last resigned herself to skipping the whole. Nevertheless she still had energy to visit the Senate gallery occasionally when she was told that a splendid orator was about to speak on a question of deep interest to his country. She listened with a little disposition to admire, if she could; and, whenever she could, she did admire. She said nothing, but she listened sharply. She wanted to learn how the machinery of government worked, and what was the quality of the men who controlled it. One by one, she passed them through her crucibles, and tested them by acids and by fire. A few survived her tests and came out alive, though more or less disfigured, where she had found impurities. Of the whole number, only one retained under this process enough character to interest her.
===Chinese===
来华盛顿不满十天，姐妹俩便悄然各就其位，被社交生活的水流毫不费力地载着前行。社交界待她们和善；也没有理由不如此。李夫人姐妹没有仇家，不谋官职，只管尽力讨人喜欢。西比尔的夏天在纽波特（Newport）、冬天在纽约，都不曾虚度；她的容貌身段、嗓音舞技，一概无须道歉。政治不是她的强项。她被人劝着去了一回国会大厦（Capitol），在参议院旁听席上坐了十分钟。她的观感如何，无人得知；凭着女性的圆熟，她做到滴水不漏。但说实在的，她对立法机构的观念本就含糊，在教堂与歌剧院两段经验之间飘来荡去，于是“某种演出”的念头总在她脑中盘桓不去。在她心里，参议院是人们去朗诵演说的地方；她还天真地认定那些演说有用场、有目的，只是引不起她的兴趣，所以再没去过。这是对国会极常见的一种理解；许多国会议员亦有同感。

姐姐更有耐性，也更大胆。足足两个星期，她几乎天天去国会大厦。到了末了，兴致开始低落，她觉得不如每天早晨读《国会议事录》（Congressional Record）里的辩论。发觉这桩差事费力、又不总有教益，她开始跳过沉闷的部分；又因为没有一件激动人心的问题，末了索性认命，整份整份地跳了过去。尽管如此，每逢听说某位出色的演说家即将就一件他深念祖国的问题登台，她仍有精神偶尔亲赴参议院旁听席。她带着一分肯钦佩的意思去听——只要钦佩得起来，她就钦佩。她一言不发，耳朵却尖得很。她想知道政府这部机器怎样运转，驾驭机器的又是何等成色的男人。她把他们一个个放进自己的坩埚，用酸、用火加以检验。少数几个经住了她的检验，活着出来了，只是在她查出杂质的地方，或多或少损了原形。全部人数当中，只有一个经此一番，还保得住足以引她兴味的品格。

===Original===
In these early visits to Congress, Mrs. Lee sometimes had the company of John Carrington, a Washington lawyer about forty years old, who, by virtue of being a Virginian and a distant connection of her husband, called himself a cousin, and took a tone of semi-intimacy, which Mrs. Lee accepted because Carrington was a man whom she liked, and because he was one whom life had treated hardly. He was of that unfortunate generation in the south which began existence with civil war, and he was perhaps the more unfortunate because, like most educated Virginians of the old Washington school, he had seen from the first that, whatever issue the war took, Virginia and he must be ruined. At twenty-two he had gone into the rebel army as a private and carried his musket modestly through a campaign or two, after which he slowly rose to the rank of senior captain in his regiment, and closed his services on the staff of a major-general, always doing scrupulously enough what he conceived to be his duty, and never doing it with enthusiasm. When the rebel armies surrendered, he rode away to his family plantation﻿—not a difficult thing to do, for it was only a few miles from Appomattox﻿—and at once began to study law; then, leaving his mother and sisters to do what they could with the worn-out plantation, he began the practice of law in Washington, hoping thus to support himself and them. He had succeeded after a fashion, and for the first time the future seemed not absolutely dark. Mrs. Lee’s house was an oasis to him, and he found himself, to his surprise, almost gay in her company. The gaiety was of a very quiet kind, and Sybil, while friendly with him, averred that he was certainly dull; but this dullness had a fascination for Madeleine, who, having tasted many more kinds of the wine of life than Sybil, had learned to value certain delicacies of age and flavour that were lost upon younger and coarser palates. He talked rather slowly and almost with effort, but he had something of the dignity﻿—others call it stiffness﻿—of the old Virginia school, and twenty years of constant responsibility and deferred hope had added a touch of care that bordered closely on sadness. His great attraction was that he never talked or seemed to think of himself. Mrs. Lee trusted in him by instinct. “He is a type!” said she; “he is my idea of George Washington at thirty.”
===Chinese===
在这几番早访国会的路上，李夫人有时有约翰·卡林顿（John Carrington）作陪。他是华盛顿一位年约四十的律师，凭着弗吉尼亚人的身份，又沾着她丈夫一门远亲，便自居为表亲，言谈间带上三分亲昵；李夫人听之任之，一来因为卡林顿这人她喜欢，二来因为他是被生活苛待了的人。他属于南方那不幸的一代：人生刚一开始，便撞上了内战；而他或许格外不幸，因为同老华盛顿学派的大多数弗吉尼亚读书人一样，他从头一天起就看得分明：不论战事如何了局，弗吉尼亚和他自己都非毁不可。二十二岁上，他以列兵的身份投了叛军，谦逊地扛着一杆滑膛枪，打完一两场战役；此后慢慢升到团里的资深上尉，最终在一位置少将的参谋任上结束了军旅生涯。凡他认定是本分的事，无一不兢兢业业办到，却从不曾怀着热忱去办。叛军投降之后，他骑马回了自家的种植园——这事不难，因为那儿离阿波马托克斯（Appomattox）不过几英里——随即动手攻读法律；随后，他把母亲和姐妹们留给那座耗竭的种植园，听她们各自设法，自己到华盛顿挂牌做律师，指望以此养活自己，也养活她们。他算是混出了个模样，平生头一回，前程看起来不至于全然漆黑。李夫人的宅子于他是一片绿洲；他发觉，同她做伴，自己竟几乎快活，连他也觉意外。那是一种极安静的快活；西比尔同他友好，却一口咬定他肯定乏味；可这份乏味对马德琳自有一种蛊惑力——她比西比尔尝过的生命之酒，种类多得多，早已学会赏识某些年份与风味上的精微，而更年轻、更粗糙的舌头，是无从领略的。他说话慢吞吞，几乎像是费劲；但带着几分旧弗吉尼亚学派的威仪——旁人管它叫古板——而二十年不断的责任与延宕的希望，又给他添上一抹紧挨着哀愁的心事。他最大的动人之处，是从来不谈自己，也仿佛从不想自己。李夫人凭本能信得过他。“他是个典型！”她说；“三十岁的乔治·华盛顿（George Washington），在我心里就是这个样子。”

===Original===
One morning in December, Carrington entered Mrs. Lee’s parlour towards noon, and asked if she cared to visit the Capitol.

“You will have a chance of hearing today what may be the last great speech of our greatest statesman,” said he; “you should come.”

“A splendid sample of our native raw material, sir?” asked she, fresh from a reading of Dickens, and his famous picture of American statesmanship.

“Precisely so,” said Carrington; “the Prairie Giant of Peonia, the Favourite Son of Illinois; the man who came within three votes of getting the party nomination for the Presidency last spring, and was only defeated because ten small intriguers are sharper than one big one. The Honourable Silas P. Ratcliffe, Senator from Illinois; he will be run for the Presidency yet.”

“What does the P. stand for?” asked Sybil.

“I don’t remember ever to have heard his middle name,” said Carrington. “Perhaps it is Peonia or Prairie; I can’t say.”
===Chinese===
十二月的一个早晨，将近正午，卡林顿走进李夫人的客厅，问她可有兴致去一趟国会大厦。

“今天您有机会听一场演说，也许是我们最伟大的政治家的最后一场伟大演说，”他说；“您该来。”

“是我们本土原材料里一件出色的标本么，先生？”她问，狄更斯（Dickens）刚读完，他笔下那幅美国政治家的名画还历历在目。

“正是如此，”卡林顿说；“皮奥尼亚（Peonia）的草原巨人，伊利诺伊（Illinois）的骄子；此人今年春天只差三票便可摘得党内总统候选人提名，落败只因为十个搞阴谋的小角色，比一个大角色来得更尖利。尊敬的赛拉斯·P. 拉特克利夫（Silas P. Ratcliffe）阁下，伊利诺伊州参议员；有朝一日，还会有人推他出来竞选总统呢。”

“那个P.是哪个字的头？”西比尔问。

“我不记得几时听过他的中间名，”卡林顿说。“没准儿是皮奥尼亚，要不就是草原；我说不准。”

===Original===
“He is the man whose appearance struck me so much when we were in the Senate last week, is he not? A great, ponderous man, over six feet high, very senatorial and dignified, with a large head and rather good features?” inquired Mrs. Lee.

“The same,” replied Carrington. “By all means hear him speak. He is the stumbling-block of the new President, who is to be allowed no peace unless he makes terms with Ratcliffe; and so everyone thinks that the Prairie Giant of Peonia will have the choice of the State or Treasury Department. If he takes either it will be the Treasury, for he is a desperate political manager, and will want the patronage for the next national convention.”

Mrs. Lee was delighted to hear the debate, and Carrington was delighted to sit through it by her side, and to exchange running comments with her on the speeches and the speakers.

“Have you ever met the Senator?” asked she.

“I have acted several times as counsel before his committees. He is an excellent chairman, always attentive and generally civil.”
===Chinese===
“他就是上礼拜我们在参议院时，那个相貌叫我印象极深的人吧？”李夫人问道。“高大魁伟，六英尺开外，一派十足的参议员气派，庄重得很，大脑门，五官也相当周正？”

“正是他，”卡林顿答道。“无论如何要去听他演说。他是新总统的绊脚石：不同拉特克利夫讲好条件，新总统就休想安生；所以人人都料定，皮奥尼亚草原巨人将在国务院与财政部之间任拣其一。真要挑，他挑的准是财政部，因为此人是个狠辣的政治操盘手，要攥住官职恩赏，为下一届全国代表大会布下人马。”

李夫人听得满心欢喜；卡林顿也欢喜坐在她身旁听完全场，同她就演说与演说家随时交换着低声的评点。

“您见过这位参议员么？”她问。

“我好几回在他的委员会里当过出庭律师。他是位顶好的主席，人永远专注，多半也客气。”

===Original===
“Where was he born?”

“The family is a New England one, and I believe respectable. He came, I think, from some place in the Connecticut Valley, but whether Vermont, New Hampshire, or Massachusetts, I don’t know.”

“Is he an educated man?”

“He got a kind of classical education at one of the country colleges there. I suspect he has as much education as is good for him. But he went West very soon after leaving college, and being then young and fresh from that hotbed of abolition, he threw himself into the anti-slavery movement in Illinois, and after a long struggle he rose with the wave. He would not do the same thing now.”
===Chinese===
“他生在哪里？”

“他家是新英格兰（New England）人家，想必体面。他好像是康涅狄格河谷什么地方的出身，究竟是佛蒙特、新罕布什尔还是马萨诸塞，我就不知道了。”

“他受过教育么？”

“他在那边乡间的一所学院里，受过一种古典教育。我疑心他的学问，已经多得正好够他消受。不过他离校不久便西去；那时人年轻，又刚从废奴主义那座温床里出来，便一头扎进伊利诺伊的反蓄奴运动，苦斗多年，随着浪潮一同兴起。如今他可不会再干这种事了。”

===Original===
“Why not?”

“He is older, more experienced, and not so wise. Besides, he has no longer the time to wait. Can you see his eyes from here? I call them Yankee eyes.”

“Don’t abuse the Yankees,” said Mrs. Lee; “I am half Yankee myself.”
===Chinese===
“为什么不会？”

“他老了些，阅历深了些，智慧少了些。再说，他再也没工夫等了。您从这儿看得见他的眼睛么？我管那双眼睛叫扬基（Yankee）眼。”

“别作践扬基人，”李夫人说；“我自己就有半个扬基血统。”

===Original===
“Is that abuse? Do you mean to deny that they have eyes?”

“I concede that there may be eyes among them; but Virginians are not fair judges of their expression.”

“Cold eyes,” he continued; “steel grey, rather small, not unpleasant in good-humour, diabolic in a passion, but worst when a little suspicious; then they watch you as though you were a young rattlesnake, to be killed when convenient.”
===Chinese===
“这算作践么？您难道想否认他们长着眼睛？”

“我承认他们中间兴许也有眼睛；不过说到眼神，弗吉尼亚人可不是公正的裁判。”

“冷眼睛，”他接着说；“钢灰色的，偏小，脾气好时不讨嫌，发起怒来如恶魔，最糟是起疑心的时候；那时它们盯住你，仿佛你是一条小响尾蛇，等方便的时候就打死。”

===Original===
“Does he not look you in the face?”

“Yes; but not as though he liked you. His eyes only seem to ask the possible uses you might be put to. Ah, the vice-president has given him the floor; now we shall have it. Hard voice, is it not? like his eyes. Hard manner, like his voice. Hard all through.”

“What a pity he is so dreadfully senatorial!” said Mrs. Lee; “otherwise I rather admire him.”
===Chinese===
“他不正眼看人么？”

“看；可不像是喜欢你的样子。他那双眼睛只像在问：你有可能派上什么用场。啊，副总统把发言权给他了；这就来了。嗓音生硬，是不是？跟眼睛一样。做派生硬，跟嗓音一样。从头到脚，硬到了底。”

“多可惜，他这一身吓人的参议员气派！”李夫人说；“不然我倒挺佩服他。”

===Original===
“Now he is settling down to his work,” continued Carrington. “See how he dodges all the sharp issues. What a thing it is to be a Yankee! What a genius the fellow has for leading a party! Do you see how well it is all done? The new President flattered and conciliated, the party united and given a strong lead. And now we shall see how the President will deal with him. Ten to one on Ratcliffe. Come, there is that stupid ass from Missouri getting up. Let us go.”

As they passed down the steps and out into the Avenue, Mrs. Lee turned to Carrington as though she had been reflecting deeply and had at length reached a decision.

“Mr. Carrington,” said she, “I want to know Senator Ratcliffe.”

“You will meet him tomorrow evening,” replied Carrington, “at your senatorial dinner.”
===Chinese===
“他这就沉下心干活了，”卡林顿接着说。“瞧他怎样把锋利的议题一一闪开。做个扬基人是多么了得的一件事！这家伙领导一个党派的天才！您看这一切办得多漂亮：新总统受了抚慰笼络，全党归于统一，还得了一个强有力的引领。且看总统接下来怎样对付他。十赌九，押拉特克利夫。来，密苏里（Missouri）来的那头蠢驴又站起来了。咱们走吧。”

两人走下台阶、出到大道上时，李夫人转向卡林顿，仿佛沉思已久，终于拿定了主意。

“卡林顿先生，”她说，“我想结识拉特克利夫参议员。”

“明天晚上您就会见到他，”卡林顿答道，“在您那场满座参议员的晚宴上。”

===Original===
The Senator from New York, the Honourable Schuyler Clinton, was an old admirer of Mrs. Lee, and his wife was a cousin of hers, more or less distant. They had lost no time in honouring the letter of credit she thus had upon them, and invited her and her sister to a solemn dinner, as imposing as political dignity could make it. Mr. Carrington, as a connection of hers, was one of the party, and almost the only one among the twenty persons at table who had neither an office, nor a title, nor a constituency. Senator Clinton received Mrs. Lee and her sister with tender enthusiasm, for they were attractive specimens of his constituents. He pressed their hands and evidently restrained himself only by an effort from embracing them, for the Senator had a marked regard for pretty women, and had made love to every girl with any pretensions to beauty that had appeared in the State of New York for fully half a century. At the same time he whispered an apology in her ear; he regretted so much that he was obliged to forego the pleasure of taking her to dinner; Washington was the only city in America where this could have happened, but it was a fact that ladies here were very great stickiers for etiquette; on the other hand he had the sad consolation that she would be the gainer, for he had allotted to her Lord Skye, the British Minister, “a most agreeable man and not married, as I have the misfortune to be;” and on the other side “I have ventured to place Senator Ratcliffe, of Illinois, whose admirable speech I saw you listening to with such rapt attention yesterday. I thought you might like to know him. Did I do right?” Madeleine assured him that he had divined her inmost wishes, and he turned with even more warmth of affection to her sister: “As for you, my dear﻿—dear Sybil, what can I do to make your dinner agreeable? If I give your sister a coronet, I am only sorry not to have a diadem for you. But I have done everything in my power. The first Secretary of the Russian Legation, Count Popoff, will take you in; a charming young man, my dear Sybil; and on your other side I have placed the Assistant Secretary of State, whom you know.” And so, after the due delay, the party settled themselves at the dinner-table, and Mrs. Lee found Senator Ratcliffe’s grey eyes resting on her face for a moment as they sat down.
===Chinese===
纽约州参议员、尊敬的斯凯勒·克林顿（Schuyler Clinton）阁下，是李夫人多年的爱慕者，他的夫人又是她或远或近的一位表亲。两位一接到她凭这层关系握有的信用证，便不失时机地予以兑付，邀她姐妹俩赴一场庄严的晚宴，其煊赫隆重，政治威仪所能铺排的莫过于此。卡林顿先生以她家亲戚的资格忝与席间，且几乎是满桌二十位宾客中，唯一既无官职、又无头衔、亦无选区的一位。克林顿参议员以脉脉的热忱接待李夫人姐妹，因为她们是他选民中的动人样本。他紧握她们的双手，看得出全凭竭力克制，才没有上前拥抱；这位参议员对漂亮女人向另眼相看，整整半个世纪以来，纽约州里凡自命有几分姿色的姑娘，无一不曾承过他的殷勤。同时，他在她耳边低声致歉：不得已放弃挽她入席之乐，深以为憾；这种事在全美国也只有华盛顿会发生，可此地的小姐太太们委实是礼数上分毫不让的人；不过另一面，他也有一个可悲的安慰——得利的将是她，因为他把斯凯勋爵（Lord Skye）——英国公使——“一位极可亲的人，而且不像我这样不幸娶了太太”——安排在她身侧；另一边，“我斗胆安置了伊利诺伊的拉特克利夫参议员；昨天他发表那篇令人赞佩的演说时，我看见您听得那样入神。我想您也许愿意认识他。我做得对么？”马德琳向他担保，他猜中了她心底最深的愿望；他又带着加倍的情意转向她妹妹：“至于您，亲爱的——亲爱的西比尔，我还能做点什么，叫您这顿晚饭吃得惬意呢？我给您姐姐安排了一顶小冠冕，只恨不能给您备下一顶王冕。可我能做的都做了。俄国公使馆一等秘书波波夫伯爵（Count Popoff）将挽您入席；一位迷人的青年，我亲爱的西比尔；您另一侧，我安置了助理国务卿，您认识他。”于是，经过应有的周章，宾主在餐桌旁各就各位；落座之际，李夫人发觉拉特克利夫参议员那双灰眼睛，在她的脸上停留了片刻。

===Original===
Lord Skye was very agreeable, and, at almost any other moment of her life, Mrs. Lee would have liked nothing better than to talk with him from the beginning to the end of her dinner. Tall, slender, bald-headed, awkward, and stammering with his elaborate British stammer whenever it suited his convenience to do so; a sharp observer who had wit which he commonly concealed; a humourist who was satisfied to laugh silently at his own humour; a diplomatist who used the mask of frankness with great effect; Lord Skye was one of the most popular men in Washington. Everyone knew that he was a ruthless critic of American manners, but he had the art to combine ridicule with good-humour, and he was all the more popular accordingly. He was an outspoken admirer of American women in everything except their voices, and he did not even shrink from occasionally quizzing a little the national peculiarities of his own countrywomen; a sure piece of flattery to their American cousins. He would gladly have devoted himself to Mrs. Lee, but decent civility required that he should pay some attention to his hostess, and he was too good a diplomatist not to be attentive to a hostess who was the wife of a Senator, and that Senator the chairman of the committee of foreign relations.
===Chinese===
斯凯勋爵十分可亲；在她一生几乎任何别的时刻，李夫人都会求之不得，巴不得从晚饭开头到结尾同他谈个尽兴。他高挑清瘦，头顶光秃，举止笨拙，那口精心锤炼的英国式口吃，随时随地发作得恰到好处；他是锐利的观察家，满腹的机锋惯于藏而不露；他是幽默家，甘于对自己的幽默默然一哂；他是外交家，把坦率这张面具使得出神入化；斯凯勋爵是华盛顿最得人望的男子之一。人人都知道他是美国风俗毫不留情的批评者，可他有本事把讥诮同好脾气熔于一炉，人望反而愈盛。他是美国女人直言不讳的倾慕者——只是对她们的嗓音不在此列——他甚至不惮于偶尔把本国女同胞的民族特性打趣几分；对她们的美国表亲，这是一记万无一失的奉承。他本乐得专心侍奉李夫人，但体面的礼数要求他对女主人也稍效殷勤，何况他又是太高明的外交家，决不会怠慢一位参议员的夫人——而那位参议员，正是外交关系委员会的主席。

===Original===
The moment his head was turned, Mrs. Lee dashed at her Peonia Giant, who was then consuming his fish, and wishing he understood why the British Minister had worn no gloves, while he himself had sacrificed his convictions by wearing the largest and whitest pair of French kids that could be bought for money on Pennsylvania Avenue. There was a little touch of mortification in the idea that he was not quite at home among fashionable people, and at this instant he felt that true happiness was only to be found among the simple and honest sons and daughters of toil. A certain secret jealousy of the British Minister is always lurking in the breast of every American Senator, if he is truly democratic; for democracy, rightly understood, is the government of the people, by the people, for the benefit of Senators, and there is always a danger that the British Minister may not understand this political principle as he should. Lord Skye had run the risk of making two blunders; of offending the Senator from New York by neglecting his wife, and the Senator from Illinois by engrossing the attention of Mrs. Lee. A young Englishman would have done both, but Lord Skye had studied the American constitution. The wife of the Senator from New York now thought him most agreeable, and at the same moment the Senator from Illinois awoke to the conviction that after all, even in frivolous and fashionable circles, true dignity is in no danger of neglect; an American Senator represents a sovereign state; the great state of Illinois is as big as England﻿—with the convenient omission of Wales, Scotland, Ireland, Canada, India, Australia, and a few other continents and islands; and in short, it was perfectly clear that Lord Skye was not formidable to him, even in light society; had not Mrs. Lee herself as good as said that no position equalled that of an American Senator?
===Chinese===
他头一转，李夫人便直扑她的皮奥尼亚巨人。那位正忙着吃鱼，一面暗自纳闷，英国公使何以不戴手套，而他本人却牺牲了信念，戴上了花得动钱在宾夕法尼亚大道（Pennsylvania Avenue）上买得到的最大最白的一双法国小山羊皮手套。想到自己置身时髦人物中间未免格格不入，他心头掠过一丝懊丧；此时此刻，他只觉得真正的幸福，唯有望于质朴而诚实的劳动儿女中间。每一位真正民主派的美国参议员胸中，对英国公使总潜伏着几分隐秘的醋意；因为照正当的理解，民主本是民有、民治、为参议员谋利的政府；而英国公使万一不能恰如其分地领会这条政治原理，危险便随时都在。斯凯勋爵方才险些连犯两桩过失：一桩是冷落纽约州参议员的夫人，一桩是独占了李夫人的注意。换一个英国青年，两桩都会犯下，斯凯勋爵却是研习过美国宪法的。纽约州参议员的夫人此刻只觉得他再可亲不过；与此同时，伊利诺伊州参议员也幡然醒悟：说到底，即便在轻浮的时髦圈子里，真正的尊严也绝无遭冷落之虞；一位美国参议员代表的是一个主权州；伟大的伊利诺伊州，抵得上整个英格兰——只消顺手把威尔士、苏格兰、爱尔兰、加拿大、印度、澳大利亚，连同其他几片大洲与岛屿略去不计；总而言之，事情一清二楚：即便在轻社交场合，斯凯勋爵于他也全然不足畏；李夫人自己不是差不多亲口说过，世上没有什么地位，抵得上一位美国参议员么？

===Original===
In ten minutes Mrs. Lee had this devoted statesman at her feet. She had not studied the Senate without a purpose. She had read with unerring instinct one general characteristic of all Senators, a boundless and guileless thirst for flattery, engendered by daily draughts from political friends or dependents, then becoming a necessity like a dram, and swallowed with a heavy smile of ineffable content. A single glance at Mr. Ratcliffe’s face showed Madeleine that she need not be afraid of flattering too grossly; her own self-respect, not his, was the only restraint upon her use of this feminine bait.

She opened upon him with an apparent simplicity and gravity, a quiet repose of manner, and an evident consciousness of her own strength, which meant that she was most dangerous.
===Chinese===
不出十分钟，李夫人便叫这位忠心的政治家拜倒在了自己脚边。她研读参议院，从来不是无的放矢。凭着百不失一的直觉，她读出了全体参议员的一个通性：一种无边而无欺诈之心的谄渴，由政界朋友或依附者每日的斟饮酿成，继而像一口烧酒成了必需，喝下去时，脸上便堆起重重的、妙不可言的餍足。只消朝拉特克利夫先生的脸看上一眼，马德琳便明白，恭维得再露骨也无须担心；约束她使用这枚女性钓饵的，唯有她自己的自尊，与他毫不相干。

她向他开了腔，带着表面的单纯与庄重，举止安恬沉静，又显然对自己的力量深有自觉——这副模样意味着，她最是危险。

===Original===
“I heard your speech yesterday, Mr. Ratcliffe. I am glad to have a chance of telling you how much I was impressed by it. It seemed to me masterly. Do you not find that it has had a great effect?”

“I thank you, madam. I hope it will help to unite the party, but as yet we have had no time to measure its results. That will require several days more.” The Senator spoke in his senatorial manner, elaborate, condescending, and a little on his guard.
===Chinese===
“昨天我听了您的演说，拉特克利夫先生。难得有个机会告诉您，它给我留下了多深的印象。依我看，那是大师手笔。您不觉得它已经起了很大的作用么？”

“谢谢您，夫人。我盼望它有助于全党的团结，不过成效还来不及掂量。那还需要几天工夫。”参议员拿出了他参议员式的腔调：周详，屈尊，还带着几分提防。

===Original===
“Do you know,” said Mrs. Lee, turning towards him as though he were a valued friend, and looking deep into his eyes, “Do you know that everyone told me I should be shocked by the falling off in political ability at Washington? I did not believe them, and since hearing your speech I am sure they are mistaken. Do you yourself think there is less ability in Congress than there used to be?”

“Well, madam, it is difficult to answer that question. Government is not so easy now as it was formerly. There are different customs. There are many men of fair abilities in public life; many more than there used to be; and there is sharper criticism and more of it.”

“Was I right in thinking that you have a strong resemblance to Daniel Webster in your way of speaking? You come from the same neighbourhood, do you not?”
===Chinese===
“您可知道，”李夫人说着转向他，仿佛他是一位交情深厚的朋友，一面深深望进他的眼睛，“您可知道，人人都对我说，华盛顿政治才干之凋敝，会叫我大吃一惊。我不信他们的话；听了您的演说之后，我拿得准他们错了。您自己觉得，如今国会里的才干真比从前少了么？”

“唔，夫人，这个问题不好回答。如今治国，不比从前容易。时下的风气不同了。公共生活中能力过得去的人很多，比从前多得多；而批评也更尖刻，为数也更多。”

“我想得对不对——您演讲的路数，同丹尼尔·韦伯斯特（Daniel Webster）像得很？你们是同一带地方的人，是不是？”

===Original===
Mrs. Lee here hit on Ratcliffe’s weak point; the outline of his head had, in fact, a certain resemblance to that of Webster, and he prided himself upon it, and on a distant relationship to the Expounder of the Constitution; he began to think that Mrs. Lee was a very intelligent person. His modest admission of the resemblance gave her the opportunity to talk of Webster’s oratory, and the conversation soon spread to a discussion of the merits of Clay and Calhoun. The Senator found that his neighbour﻿—a fashionable New York woman, exquisitely dressed, and with a voice and manner seductively soft and gentle﻿—had read the speeches of Webster and Calhoun. She did not think it necessary to tell him that she had persuaded the honest Carrington to bring her the volumes and to mark such passages as were worth her reading; but she took care to lead the conversation, and she criticised with some skill and more humour the weak points in Websterian oratory, saying with a little laugh and a glance into his delighted eyes:
===Chinese===
李夫人这一下，正打在拉特克利夫的软处。他的头型轮廓，同韦伯斯特确有几分相像，他一向以此自负，还夸说自己是那位宪法阐释者（the Expounder of the Constitution）的远亲；他开始觉得，李夫人是位极有头脑的人物。他谦逊地承认了这份相像，这就给了她机会去谈韦伯斯特的辩才；话头很快铺开，变成一场对克莱（Clay）与卡尔霍恩（Calhoun）高下的议论。参议员发觉，邻座这位时髦的纽约女子——衣饰精美入时，嗓音与举止柔婉得诱人——竟读过韦伯斯特与卡尔霍恩的演说。她认为不必告诉他，是她劝动了老实人卡林顿替她搬来那几卷书，还标出值得她一读的篇章；但她步步留心引着话头走，凭着几分功力、更多几分诙谐，挑剔韦伯斯特式演说术的破绽，末了一笑，朝他那双喜不自胜的眼睛里望了一望，说道：

===Original===
“My judgment may not be worth much, Mr. Senator, but it does seem to me that our fathers thought too much of themselves, and till you teach me better I shall continue to think that the passage in your speech of yesterday which began with, ‘Our strength lies in this twisted and tangled mass of isolated principles, the hair of the half-sleeping giant of Party,’ is both for language and imagery quite equal to anything of Webster’s.”
===Chinese===
“我的见识也许值不得什么，参议员先生，可我实在觉得，我们的父辈把自己看得太重了；在您把我教得明白些之前，我要一直认为，您昨天的演说里，以‘我们的力量，正在于这团纠结缠绕、彼此孤立的原则，在于政党这头半睡巨人的发丝’一句开头的那一段——无论辞采还是意象，都全然抵得上韦伯斯特的任何文字。”

===Original===
The Senator from Illinois rose to this gaudy fly like a huge, two-hundred-pound salmon; his white waistcoat gave out a mild silver reflection as he slowly came to the surface and gorged the hook. He made not even a plunge, not one perceptible effort to tear out the barbed weapon, but, floating gently to her feet, allowed himself to be landed as though it were a pleasure. Only miserable casuists will ask whether this was fair play on Madeleine’s part; whether flattery so gross cost her conscience no twinge, and whether any woman can without self-abasement be guilty of such shameless falsehood. She, however, scorned the idea of falsehood. She would have defended herself by saying that she had not so much praised Ratcliffe as depreciated Webster, and that she was honest in her opinion of the old-fashioned American oratory. But she could not deny that she had wilfully allowed the Senator to draw conclusions very different from any she actually held. She could not deny that she had intended to flatter him to the extent necessary for her purpose, and that she was pleased at her success. Before they rose from table the Senator had quite unbent himself; he was talking naturally, shrewdly, and with some humour; he had told her Illinois stories; spoken with extraordinary freedom about his political situation; and expressed the wish to call upon Mrs. Lee, if he could ever hope to find her at home.
===Chinese===
伊利诺伊州参议员扑向这只艳丽的假蝇，活像一尾两百磅重的大鲑鱼；他那件白背心泛出淡淡的银光，仿佛缓缓浮上水面，把钓钩一口吞了下去。他连一个翻腾也不打，不见半分挣脱那枚倒刺钓钩的气力，只轻轻浮到她的脚边，听任自己被拉上岸来，仿佛这是一桩赏心乐事。只有寒碜的诡辩家才会追问：马德琳这一手算不算光明正大？这样露骨的奉承可曾刺痛她的良心？一个女人做出这样无耻的假话，可还能不自贬身份？然而她对“假话”这项指控嗤之以鼻。她会替自己辩护说，她与其说是抬高了拉特克利夫，不如说是压低了韦伯斯特；她对旧式美国演说术的见解，句句出自真心。可她无法否认，她是存心听任参议员去得出与她实际所持大相径庭的结论；无法否认，她有意把他奉承到尽够她用的地步，而且对自己的得手满心欢喜。离席之前，参议员已经全然松了架子；他谈得自然、精到，还不乏幽默；他给她讲了几个伊利诺伊的故事；以异乎寻常的坦率谈了自己的政治处境；并表示，倘若能有幸撞上她在家的时辰，他盼望着登门拜望李夫人。

===Original===
“I am always at home on Sunday evenings,” said she.

To her eyes he was the high-priest of American politics; he was charged with the meaning of the mysteries, the clue to political hieroglyphics. Through him she hoped to sound the depths of statesmanship and to bring up from its oozy bed that pearl of which she was in search; the mysterious gem which must lie hidden somewhere in politics. She wanted to understand this man; to turn him inside out; to experiment on him and use him as young physiologists use frogs and kittens. If there was good or bad in him, she meant to find its meaning.

And he was a western widower of fifty; his quarters in Washington were in gaunt boardinghouse rooms, furnished only with public documents and enlivened by western politicians and office-seekers. In the summer he retired to a solitary, white framehouse with green blinds, surrounded by a few feet of uncared-for grass and a white fence; its interior more dreary still, with iron stoves, oilcloth carpets, cold white walls, and one large engraving of Abraham Lincoln in the parlour; all in Peonia, Illinois! What equality was there between these two combatants? what hope for him? what risk for her? And yet Madeleine Lee had fully her match in Mr. Silas P. Ratcliffe.
===Chinese===
“星期天晚上我总在家。”她说。

在她眼里，他是美国政治的大祭司，一身担当着种种奥秘的义谛，掌着政治象形文字的线索。她指望凭他探到治国之术的深底，从那泥泞的河床上，捞起她寻而未获的那颗珍珠——那颗必定藏匿在政治中某一处的神秘宝珠。她想弄懂这个男人；把他翻个底朝天；拿他做实验、拿他当用具，就像年轻的生理学家摆布青蛙和小猫。他身上无论是善是恶，她都要找出其中的含义。

而他呢，是西部一个五十岁的鳏夫；在华盛顿的寓所，是寄宿公寓里几间冷清空落的屋子，陈设唯有公文，给屋里添点人气的，唯有西部的政客和钻营官职之徒。夏天，他退居到一幢孤零零的白木板房，绿色百叶窗，四周围着几英尺无人经管的草地和一道白栅栏；屋内更加凄凉：铁炉子，油布地毯，冷白的墙，客厅里一幅大幅的亚伯拉罕·林肯（Abraham Lincoln）雕版像——而这一切，都在伊利诺伊的皮奥尼亚！这样两名决斗者之间，还有什么均势可言？他有什么指望？她有什么风险？然而马德琳·李在这位赛拉斯·P. 拉特克利夫先生面前，可算遇上了旗鼓相当的对手。

## III / 三

===Original===
Mrs. Lee soon became popular. Her parlour was a favourite haunt of certain men and women who had the art of finding its mistress at home; an art which seemed not to be within the powers of everybody. Carrington was apt to be there more often than anyone else, so that he was looked on as almost a part of the family, and if Madeleine wanted a book from the library, or an extra man at her dinner-table, Carrington was pretty certain to help her to the one or the other. Old Baron Jacobi, the Bulgarian minister, fell madly in love with both sisters, as he commonly did with every pretty face and neat figure. He was a witty, cynical, broken-down Parisian roué, kept in Washington for years past by his debts and his salary; always grumbling because there was no opera, and mysteriously disappearing on visits to New York; a voracious devourer of French and German literature, especially of novels; a man who seemed to have met every noted or notorious personage of the century, and whose mind was a magazine of amusing information; an excellent musical critic, who was not afraid to criticise Sybil’s singing; a connoisseur in bric-a-brac, who laughed at Madeleine’s display of odds and ends, and occasionally brought her a Persian plate or a bit of embroidery, which he said was good and would do her credit. This old sinner believed in everything that was perverse and wicked, but he accepted the prejudices of Anglo-Saxon society, and was too clever to obtrude his opinions upon others. He would have married both sisters at once more willingly than either alone, but as he feelingly said, “If I were forty years younger, mademoiselle, you should not sing to me so calmly.” His friend Popoff, an intelligent, vivacious Russian, with very Calmuck features, susceptible as a girl, and passionately fond of music, hung over Sybil’s piano by the hour; he brought Russian airs which he taught her to sing, and, if the truth were known, he bored Madeleine desperately, for she undertook to act the part of duenna to her younger sister.
===Chinese===
李夫人很快便红了起来。她的客厅成了某类男女流连不去的胜地——这类人精通一门艺术：上门时总能撞见女主人恰好在家；这门艺术似乎并非人人所能。卡林顿来的次数比谁都多，以致被看作几乎是这个家庭的一部分；马德琳若要从图书馆借一本书，或宴席上需要添一位男客，卡林顿十有八九能帮她办到这一样或那一样。老雅各比男爵（Baron Jacobi）是保加利亚公使，对姐妹俩一齐疯狂地动了情——凡是漂亮脸蛋、利落身段，他惯常如此。他是个机智、玩世不恭、垮掉了的巴黎老浪子，多年来被债务与薪俸一并扣在华盛顿；永远抱怨此地没有歌剧，又不时神秘消失，说是去纽约走访；法德两国文学——尤其是小说——的饕餮之徒；一个仿佛与本世纪每一位赫赫有名或臭名昭著的人物都有过一面之缘的人，满腹皆是有趣的见闻；一位出色的音乐评论家，敢于批评西比尔的歌唱；一位古玩行家，笑话马德琳陈列的杂碎摆设，偶尔却带给她一只波斯盘子或一方绣片，说这才是好东西，给她添光彩。这个老罪人信奉一切乖张邪僻的东西，但他接受盎格鲁-撒克逊社会的成见，又聪明得不至把自己的见解强加于人。姐妹两个让他挑，他宁可一齐娶下，也不愿单娶其一；不过正如他动情时说的：“我要是年轻四十岁，小姐，您就不能这样心平气和地给我唱歌了。”他的朋友波波夫是个聪明活泼的俄国人，长着一张十足的卡尔梅克人的脸，多情善感一如少女，又酷嗜音乐，一坐到西比尔的钢琴边便是几个钟头；他带来俄国曲调教她唱，而若说句实话，他简直把马德琳烦透了，因为她自愿充当起妹妹的伴媪来。

===Original===
A very different visitor was Mr. C. C. French, a young member of Congress from Connecticut, who aspired to act the part of the educated gentleman in politics, and to purify the public tone. He had reform principles and an unfortunately conceited manner; he was rather wealthy, rather clever, rather well-educated, rather honest, and rather vulgar. His allegiance was divided between Mrs. Lee and her sister, whom he infuriated by addressing as “Miss Sybil” with patronising familiarity. He was particularly strong in what he called “badinaige,” and his playful but ungainly attempts at wit drove Mrs. Lee beyond the bounds of patience. When in a solemn mood, he talked as though he were practising for the ear of a college debating society, and with a still worse effect on the patience; but with all this he was useful, always bubbling with the latest political gossip, and deeply interested in the fate of party stakes.

Quite another sort of person was Mr. Hartbeest Schneidekoupon, a citizen of Philadelphia, though commonly resident in New York, where he had fallen a victim to Sybil’s charms, and made efforts to win her young affections by instructing her in the mysteries of currency and protection, to both which subjects he was devoted. To forward these two interests and to watch over Miss Ross’s welfare, he made periodical visits to Washington, where he closeted himself with committeemen and gave expensive dinners to members of Congress. Mr. Schneidekoupon was rich, and about thirty years old, tall and thin, with bright eyes and smooth face, elaborate manners and much loquacity. He had the reputation of turning rapid intellectual somersaults, partly to amuse himself and partly to startle society. At one moment he was artistic, and discoursed scientifically about his own paintings; at another he was literary, and wrote a book on “Noble Living,” with a humanitarian purpose; at another he was devoted to sport, rode a steeplechase, played polo, and set up a four-in-hand; his last occupation was to establish in Philadelphia the *Protective Review*, a periodical in the interests of American industry, which he edited himself, as a stepping-stone to Congress, the Cabinet, and the Presidency. At about the same time he bought a yacht, and heavy bets were pending among his sporting friends whether he would manage to sink first his Review or his yacht. But he was an amiable and excellent fellow through all his eccentricities, and he brought to Mrs. Lee the simple outpourings of the amateur politician.

A much higher type of character was Mr. Nathan Gore, of Massachusetts, a handsome man with a grey beard, a straight, sharply cut nose, and a fine, penetrating eye; in his youth a successful poet whose satires made a noise in their day, and are still remembered for the pungency and wit of a few verses; then a deep student in Europe for many years, until his famous *History of Spain in America* placed him instantly at the head of American historians, and made him minister at Madrid, where he remained four years to his entire satisfaction, this being the nearest approach to a patent of nobility and a government pension which the American citizen can attain. A change of administration had reduced him to private life again, and after some years of retirement he was now in Washington, willing to be restored to his old mission. Every President thinks it respectable to have at least one literary man in his pay, and Mr. Gore’s prospects were fair for obtaining his object, as he had the active support of a majority of the Massachusetts delegation. He was abominably selfish, colossally egoistic, and not a little vain; but he was shrewd; he knew how to hold his tongue; he could flatter dexterously, and he had learned to eschew satire. Only in confidence and among friends he would still talk freely, but Mrs. Lee was not yet on those terms with him.
===Chinese===
一位截然不同的客人是 C. C. 弗伦奇先生（Mr. C. C. French），康涅狄格（Connecticut）来的年轻国会议员，立志在政界扮演有教养绅士的角色，涤荡公共言论的风气。他怀有改革原则，可惜做派自负；他相当有钱、相当聪明、相当有学问、相当诚实，也相当俗气。他的忠心在李夫人和她妹妹之间一分为二；他一口一个“西比尔小姐”，那副屈尊俯就的熟络劲儿惹得她火冒三丈。他尤其擅长他所谓的“打趣妙语”（badinaige），那些打诨而笨拙的诙谐尝试，屡屡把李夫人逼到耐心的边界之外。情绪庄重时，他讲话俨然在给大学辩论社的耳朵做演习，对人的耐心为害更烈；但尽管如此他有他的用处，永远翻涌着最新的政治小道消息，对党派赌局的输赢关切入微。

性情迥然不同的另一位，是哈特比斯特·施奈德库彭先生（Mr. Hartbeest Schneidekoupon），费城公民，平日常住纽约；在那儿他做了西比尔魅力的牺牲品，努力以讲授货币与保护关税两门玄奥来赢得她的少女芳心——这两门学问他都醉心。为推进这两桩利益，也为看护罗斯小姐的福祉，他定期来华盛顿，与委员们闭门密谈，给国会议员们操办昂贵的宴席。施奈德库彭先生有钱，年约三十，瘦高个儿，眼睛明亮，脸面光洁，礼数繁缛，饶舌多话。他素有翻思想筋斗的名声，翻得又快又急，一半自娱，一半惊世。一时他是艺术家，科学地谈论自己的画作；一时他是文人，怀着人道主义的目的写了一部《高尚生活》（Noble Living）；一时他又献身体育，骑越野障碍赛马，打马球，置办一辆四驾马车；他最近的一桩事业，是在费城创办《保护评论》（Protective Review）——一份为美国实业利益鼓吹的期刊，由他亲任主编，当作进军国会、内阁与总统宝座的垫脚石。差不多与此同时他买了一艘游艇，他那些运动界的朋友重注悬而未决：赌他先弄沉的是那份《评论》，还是那艘游艇。但千般怪癖之下，他是个和蔼可亲的好人，他带给李夫人的，是业余政治家淳朴的心声倾吐。

格调高出许多的，是马萨诸塞（Massachusetts）的内森·戈尔先生（Mr. Nathan Gore）：相貌堂堂，灰白胡须，鼻梁笔直如削，一双眼睛精明摄人；年轻时是位成功的诗人，讽刺诗名噪一时，其中几行的辛辣与机智至今为人记诵；尔后在欧洲潜心问学多年，直到他著名的《西班牙在美洲史》（History of Spain in America）一举将他推上美国史家的头把交椅，并为他挣得驻马德里（Madrid）公使一职——他在任四年，称心满意之至，因为这是美国公民所能企及的、最接近贵族特许状与政府年金的位子。政府一换届，他又被贬回平民生活；闲居数年后，如今来到华盛顿，愿意官复原职。每位总统都觉得豢养至少一位文人脸上体面；戈尔先生达到目的的前景颇为看好，因为他有马萨诸塞州代表团多数人的积极支持。他自私得可憎，自我大得骇人，虚荣亦复不少；但他精明；他知道如何管住舌头；他奉承起来手法娴熟，而且学会了戒绝讽刺。只有推心置腹、朋友之间，他才肯畅所欲言，而李夫人与他的交情尚未至此。

===Original===
These were all men, and there was no want of women in Mrs. Lee’s parlour; but, after all, they are able to describe themselves better than any poor novelist can describe them.

Generally two currents of conversation ran on together—one round Sybil, the other about Madeleine.
===Chinese===
这些都是男人；李夫人客厅里的女客也不匮乏；不过说到底，她们自己刻画自己，总比可怜的小说家来刻画她们高明。

通常两股谈话的水流并行不悖——一股绕着西比尔，一股围着马德琳。

===Original===
“Mees Ross,” said Count Popoff, leading in a handsome young foreigner, “I have your permission to present to you my friend Count Orsini, Secretary of the Italian Legation. Are you at home this afternoon? Count Orsini sings also.”

“We are charmed to see Count Orsini. It is well you came so late, for I have this moment come in from making Cabinet calls. They were so queer! I have been crying with laughter for an hour past.”

“Do you find these calls amusing?” asked Popoff, gravely and diplomatically.

“Indeed I do! I went with Julia Schneidekoupon, you know, Madeleine; the Schneidekoupons are descended from all the Kings of Israel, and are prouder than Solomon in his glory. And when we got into the house of some dreadful woman from Heaven knows where, imagine my feelings at overhearing this conversation: ‘What may be your family name, ma’am?’ ‘Schneidekoupon is my name,’ replies Julia, very tall and straight. ‘Have you any friends whom I should likely know?’ ‘I think not,’ says Julia, severely. ‘Wal! I don’t seem to remember of ever having heerd the name. But I s’pose it’s all right. I like to know who calls.’ I almost had hysterics when we got into the street, but Julia could not see the joke at all.”
===Chinese===
“罗斯小姐，”波波夫伯爵领着一位英俊的外国青年进来，说道，“我征得您的许可，向您介绍我的朋友奥西尼伯爵（Count Orsini），意大利公使馆秘书。您今天下午见客么？奥西尼伯爵也会唱歌。”

“见到奥西尼伯爵，我们太高兴了。幸亏你们来得晚，我方才出门拜会内阁各家才回来。那些人家可真古怪！我笑得眼泪直流，笑了整整一个钟头。”

“您觉得这些拜会有趣么？”波波夫庄重而外交腔调地问。

“岂止有趣！我是同朱莉娅·施奈德库彭（Julia Schneidekoupon）一起去的，你知道的，马德琳；施奈德库彭家是以色列列王的后裔，比荣耀鼎盛的所罗门还骄傲。我们走进某位可怕女人的家里——天晓得她是哪里来的——你想想，我无意间听到下面这段对话，是什么心情：‘请问太太您府上姓什么？’‘施奈德库彭就是我的姓，’朱莉娅答道，人高高的，腰板笔直。‘您有我兴许认识的朋友么？’‘我想没有，’朱莉娅凛然道。‘哟！我可不记得几时听说过这个姓儿。不过想来总归没错儿。我喜欢弄清楚上门的是谁。’回到街上，我几乎犯了歇斯底里，朱莉娅却全然看不出笑料何在。”

===Original===
Count Orsini was not quite sure that he himself saw the joke, so he only smiled becomingly and showed his teeth. For simple, childlike vanity and self-consciousness nothing equals an Italian Secretary of Legation at twenty-five. Yet conscious that the effect of his personal beauty would perhaps be diminished by permanent silence, he ventured to murmur presently: “Do you not find it very strange, this society in America?”

“Society!” laughed Sybil with gay contempt. “There are no snakes in America, any more than in Norway.”

“Snakes, mademoiselle!” repeated Orsini, with the doubtful expression of one who is not quite certain whether he shall risk walking on thin ice, and decides to go softly: “Snakes! Indeed they would rather be doves I would call them.”

A kind laugh from Sybil strengthened into conviction his hope that he had made a joke in this unknown tongue. His face brightened, his confidence returned; once or twice he softly repeated to himself: “Not snakes; they would be doves!”
===Chinese===
奥西尼伯爵自己也不大有把握是否看懂了这个笑话，于是只得得体地微笑，露出牙齿。论单纯、孩子气的虚荣与自我意识，二十五岁的意大利公使馆秘书天下无双。然而他也意识到，长久的沉默或许会折损他个人美貌的效力，便过了一会儿冒险嗫嚅道：“您不觉得非常奇怪么，美国的这个社交界？”

“社交界！”西比尔带着轻快的不屑笑道。“美国没有蛇，跟挪威一样没有。”

“蛇，小姐！”奥西尼重复道，脸上带着那种疑疑惑惑的神情，好比一个人拿不准要不要冒险踏上薄冰，决定还是放轻脚步：“蛇！说真的，它们毋宁是鸽子呢，依我看。”

西比尔善意的一笑，使他相信自己已用这门陌生的语言说成了一句笑话。他脸上放光，信心回来了；有一两回，他轻声向自己念叨：“不是蛇；它们是鸽子！”

===Original===
But Mrs. Lee’s sensitive ear had caught Sybil’s remark, and detected in it a certain tone of condescension which was not to her taste. The impassive countenances of these bland young Secretaries of Legation seemed to acquiesce far too much as a matter of course in the idea that there was no society except in the old world. She broke into the conversation with an emphasis that fluttered the dovecote:

“Society in America? Indeed there is society in America, and very good society too; but it has a code of its own, and newcomers seldom understand it. I will tell you what it is, Mr. Orsini, and you will never be in danger of making any mistake. ‘Society’ in America means all the honest, kindly-mannered, pleasant-voiced women, and all the good, brave, unassuming men, between the Atlantic and the Pacific. Each of these has a free pass in every city and village, ‘good for this generation only,’ and it depends on each to make use of this pass or not as it may happen to suit his or her fancy. To this rule there are *no* exceptions, and those who say ‘Abraham is our father’ will surely furnish food for that humour which is the staple product of our country.”

The alarmed youths, who did not in the least understand the meaning of this demonstration, looked on with a feeble attempt at acquiescence, while Mrs. Lee brandished her sugar-tongs in the act of transferring a lump of sugar to her cup, quite unconscious of the slight absurdity of the gesture, while Sybil stared in amazement, for it was not often that her sister waved the stars and stripes so energetically. Whatever their silent criticisms might be, however, Mrs. Lee was too much in earnest to be conscious of them, or, indeed, to care for anything but what she was saying. There was a moment’s pause when she came to the end of her speech, and then the thread of talk was quietly taken up again where Sybil’s incipient sneer had broken it.
===Chinese===
但李夫人敏感的耳朵捕捉到了西比尔的话，并从中听出一种居高临下的口气，不合她的脾胃。这些温文尔雅的年轻公使馆秘书们，一张张不动声色的脸，似乎把“旧世界之外无社交界”的想法视作理所当然，默认得未免太过。她斩钉截铁地闯进谈话，惊得这一窝鸽子扑腾乱飞：

“美国的社交界？美国当然有社交界，而且是顶好的社交界；只是它有自己的一套法典，新来的人少有能懂的。我这就告诉您它是怎么回事，奥西尼先生，往后您就决无弄错的危险。在美国，‘社交界’指的是大西洋与太平洋之间所有诚实的女人——她们举止亲切、嗓音悦耳——以及所有善良、勇敢、不自大的男人。这些人在每一座城市、每一个乡村都持有一张免费通行证，‘限本代使用’；用不用这张证，全凭各人高兴。这条规则绝无例外；至于那些口称‘亚伯拉罕是我们的先祖’的人，注定要给我国的大宗物产——幽默——供应口粮。”

那几个受惊的青年全然不明白这番宣言是什么意思，面面相觑，勉强作出唯唯称是的神色；李夫人则挥舞着方糖夹子，正把一块糖夹进自己的茶杯，全然不觉这姿态微微有些可笑；西比尔惊愕地瞪着眼——姐姐如此起劲地挥动星条旗，可不是常有的事。然而无论他们无声的腹诽是什么，李夫人过分认真，既觉察不到，也实在顾不上理会别的，只顾说她的话。话音落下，片刻停顿，随后谈话的线头又悄悄接续起来，恰在西比尔那声将起未起的冷笑把它扯断的地方。

===Original===
Carrington came in. “What have you been doing at the Capitol?” asked Madeleine.

“Lobbying!” was the reply, given in the semi-serious tone of Carrington’s humour.

“So soon, and Congress only two days old?” exclaimed Mrs. Lee.

“Madam,” rejoined Carrington, with his quietest malice, “Congressmen are like birds of the air, which are caught only by the early worm.”
===Chinese===
卡林顿走了进来。“你在国会大厦干什么来着？”马德琳问。

“游说！”对方答道，用的是卡林顿式幽默的半认真口吻。

“这么快？国会才开了两天哪！”李夫人失声道。

“夫人，”卡林顿以他最安详的恶意应道，“国会议员好比空中的飞鸟，唯有早起的虫子才捉得着它们。”

===Original===
“Good afternoon, Mrs. Lee. Miss Sybil, how do you do again? Which of these gentlemen’s hearts are you feeding upon now?” This was the refined style of Mr. French, indulging in what he was pleased to term “badinaige.” He, too, was on his way from the Capitol, and had come in for a cup of tea and a little human society. Sybil made a face which plainly expressed a longing to inflict on Mr. French some grievous personal wrong, but she pretended not to hear. He sat down by Madeleine, and asked, “Did you see Ratcliffe yesterday?”

“Yes,” said Madeleine; “he was here last evening with Mr. Carrington and one or two others.”

“Did he say anything about politics?”

“Not a word. We talked mostly about books.”

“Books! What does he know about books?”

“You must ask him.”
===Chinese===
“午后安好，李夫人。西比尔小姐，您又好？这会儿您正拿哪位先生的心当点心吃呢？”这便是弗伦奇先生风雅的谈吐，正在纵情施展他美其名曰“打趣妙语”的功夫。他也是打国会大厦来的，顺路进来喝杯茶，沾一点人间的陪伴。西比尔做了个鬼脸，明白表示极想对弗伦奇先生下一点惨痛的毒手，但她装作没听见。他在马德琳身边坐下，问道：“您昨天见到拉特克利夫了么？”

“见到了，”马德琳说；“昨晚他同卡林顿先生还有一两位别人一起在这儿。”

“他谈政治了么？”

“一个字也没谈。我们多半谈的是书。”

“书！他懂什么书？”

“那您得去问他。”

===Original===
“Well, this is the most ridiculous situation we are all in. No one knows anything about the new President. You could take your oath that everybody is in the dark. Ratcliffe says he knows as little as the rest of us, but it can’t be true; he is too old a politician not to have wires in his hand; and only today one of the pages of the Senate told my colleague Cutter that a letter sent off by him yesterday was directed to Sam Grimes, of North Bend, who, as everyone knows, belongs to the President’s particular crowd.—Why, Mr. Schneidekoupon! How do you do? When did you come on?”

“Thank you; this morning,” replied Mr. Schneidekoupon, just entering the room. “So glad to see you again, Mrs. Lee. How do you and your sister like Washington? Do you know I have brought Julia on for a visit? I thought I should find her here.”

“She has just gone. She has been all the afternoon with Sybil, making calls. She says you want her here to lobby for you, Mr. Schneidekoupon. Is it true?”
===Chinese===
“唉，咱们大家所处的，真是有史以来最可笑的局面。谁也不知道新总统的底细。您可以起誓，人人都蒙在鼓里。拉特克利夫说他跟咱们大家一样一无所知，这不可能是真话；他是太老到的政客，手里不可能没有提线的；就在今天，参议院的一个听差还告诉我的同僚卡特（Cutter），说他昨天寄出的一封信，收信人是北本德（North Bend）的萨姆·格莱姆斯（Sam Grimes）——谁都知道，此人是总统心腹圈子里的人。——哟，施奈德库彭先生！您可好？几时到的？”

“谢谢；今天早晨，”施奈德库彭先生答道，人刚好进门。“又见到您真高兴，李夫人。您和您妹妹喜欢华盛顿么？您可知道我把朱莉娅接来小住了？我料想该在这儿找着她。”

“她刚走。一下午都陪西比尔出门拜客来着。她说您要她来华盛顿替您游说，施奈德库彭先生。是真的么？”

===Original===
“So I did,” replied he, with a laugh, “but she is precious little use. So I’ve come to draft you into the service.”

“Me!”

“Yes; you know we all expect Senator Ratcliffe to be Secretary of the Treasury, and it is very important for us to keep him straight on the currency and the tariff. So I have come on to establish more intimate relations with him, as they say in diplomacy. I want to get him to dine with me at Welckley’s, but as I know he keeps very shy of politics I thought my only chance was to make it a ladies’ dinner, so I brought on Julia. I shall try and get Mrs. Schuyler Clinton, and I depend upon you and your sister to help Julia out.”

“Me! at a lobby dinner! Is that proper?”

“Why not? You shall choose the guests.”

“I never heard of such a thing; but it would certainly be amusing. Sybil must not go, but I might.”

“Excuse me; Julia depends upon Miss Ross, and will not go to table without her.”
===Chinese===
“倒是真的，”他笑着答道，“可她顶不了什么用。所以我来把您征募入伍了。”

“我！”

“正是；您知道我们都料定拉特克利夫参议员要当财政部长，务必让他在货币和关税问题上站稳立场，这对我们关系重大。所以我赶了来，用外交上的话说，同他建立更亲密的关系。我想请他到韦尔克利餐馆（Welckley’s）与我共进晚餐，但我知道他对政治应酬避之唯恐不及，便想唯一的指望是办成一场有女眷作陪的宴席，于是把朱莉娅也接了来。我还要设法请斯凯勒·克林顿夫人；至于帮朱莉娅撑住场面，我就全指望您和您妹妹了。”

“我！去赴游说的饭局！这成体统么？”

“怎么不成？客人由您来挑。”

“这种事我闻所未闻；不过一定很有趣。西比尔不能去，我倒可以去。”

“对不起；朱莉娅全指着罗斯小姐，没有她是不肯入席的。”

===Original===
“Well,” assented Mrs. Lee, hesitatingly, “perhaps if you get Mrs. Clinton, and if your sister is there—And who else?”

“Choose your own company.”

“I know no one.”

“Oh yes; here is French, not quite sound on the tariff, but good for what we want just now. Then we can get Mr. Gore; he has his little hatchet to grind too, and will be glad to help grind ours. We only want two or three more, and I will have an extra man or so to fill up.”

“Do ask the Speaker. I want to know him.”

“I will, and Carrington, and my Pennsylvania Senator. That will do nobly. Remember, Welckley’s, Saturday at seven.”
===Chinese===
“那么，”李夫人迟疑着应道，“要是您请到了克林顿夫人，您妹妹又在场的话，兴许——还有谁？”

“宾容您自个儿定。”

“我谁也不认识。”

“哦，认识；这不有弗伦奇——在关税问题上立场不大牢靠，不过眼下正合我们的用场。然后我们还能请到戈尔先生；他自己也有把小斧头要磨，会乐意帮着磨我们的。只差两三位了，我再添上一两个补空的男客。”

“务必请上议长。我想认识认识他。”

“一定，还有卡林顿，还有我那位宾夕法尼亚州参议员。那就齐全了，妙极。记住，韦尔克利餐馆，礼拜六七点。”

===Original===
Meanwhile Sybil had been at the piano, and when she had sung for a time, Orsini was induced to take her place, and show that it was possible to sing without injury to one’s beauty. Baron Jacobi came in and found fault with them both. Little Miss Dare—commonly known among her male friends as little Daredevil—who was always absorbed in some flirtation with a Secretary of Legation, came in, quite unaware that Popoff was present, and retired with him into a corner, while Orsini and Jacobi bullied poor Sybil, and fought with each other at the piano; everybody was talking with very little reference to any reply, when at last Mrs. Lee drove them all out of the room: “We are quiet people,” said she, “and we dine at half-past six.”
===Chinese===
这厢西比尔一直在钢琴前，唱了一阵之后，众人劝奥西尼接替她的位置，证明唱歌也可以不损容貌。雅各比男爵进来，把两人都数落了一通。小戴尔小姐（Little Miss Dare）——男性朋友圈里惯常唤她作“小亡命徒”（little Daredevil）——正一门心思同哪位公使馆秘书调情，走进来时全然不知波波夫在场，便同他退到一个角落里去；这厢奥西尼和雅各比一面欺负可怜的西比尔，一面在琴边互相较劲；人人都在说话，很少理会任何回音；末了李夫人把他们统统轰出房间：“我们是清静人家，”她说，“六点半开饭。”

===Original===
Senator Ratcliffe had not failed to make his Sunday evening call upon Mrs. Lee. Perhaps it was not strictly correct to say that they had talked books all the evening, but whatever the conversation was, it had only confirmed Mr. Ratcliffe’s admiration for Mrs. Lee, who, without intending to do so, had acted a more dangerous part than if she had been the most accomplished of coquettes. Nothing could be more fascinating to the weary politician in his solitude than the repose of Mrs. Lee’s parlour, and when Sybil sang for him one or two simple airs—she said they were foreign hymns, the Senator being, or being considered, orthodox—Mr. Ratcliffe’s heart yearned toward the charming girl quite with the sensations of a father, or even of an elder brother.
===Chinese===
拉特克利夫参议员没有失约，礼拜天晚上照来李夫人府上拜访。说他们整晚谈的都是书，或许不完全确切；但无论谈的是什么，都只会加深拉特克利夫先生对李夫人的倾慕——她无意之间所扮演的角色，比最老练的卖弄风情者还要危险。对这位孤身疲惫的政客来说，再没有什么比李夫人客厅里的安宁更迷人的了；当西比尔为他唱了一两支朴素的曲子——她说那是外国的圣歌，因为参议员是、或被认为是正统信徒——拉特克利夫先生的心向着这位可爱的姑娘生出眷恋，那情怀俨然一位父亲，甚至一位兄长。

===Original===
His brother senators very soon began to remark that the Prairie Giant had acquired a trick of looking up to the ladies’ gallery. One day Mr. Jonathan Andrews, the special correspondent of the New York *Sidereal System*, a very friendly organ, approached Senator Schuyler Clinton with a puzzled look on his face.

“Can you tell me,” said he, “what has happened to Silas P. Ratcliffe? Only a moment ago I was talking with him at his seat on a very important subject, about which I must send his opinions off to New York tonight, when, in the middle of a sentence, he stopped short, got up without looking at me, and left the Senate Chamber, and now I see him in the gallery talking with a lady whose face I don’t know.”

Senator Clinton slowly adjusted his gold eyeglasses and looked up at the place indicated: “Ah! Mrs. Lightfoot Lee! I think I will say a word to her myself;” and turning his back on the special correspondent, he skipped away with youthful agility after the Senator from Illinois.

“Devil!” muttered Mr. Andrews; “what has got into the old fools?” and in a still less audible murmur as he looked up to Mrs. Lee, then in close conversation with Ratcliffe: “Had I better make an item of that?”
===Chinese===
参议院的同僚们很快便察觉，草原巨人养成了抬眼张望女宾旁听席的习惯。一天，纽约《恒星系报》（Sidereal System——一份立场极友善的机关报）的特派记者乔纳森·安德鲁斯先生（Mr. Jonathan Andrews），一脸困惑地走近斯凯勒·克林顿参议员。

“您能告诉我，”他说，“赛拉斯·P. 拉特克利夫这是怎么了？方才我还在他座位上同他谈一个顶重要的题目——今晚我必须把他的意见发往纽约——话说到一半，他突然打住，看也不看我一眼就站起身，出了参议院议事厅；现在我一抬眼，却见他在旁听席上同一位女士谈话，那面孔我不认得。”

克林顿参议员慢慢架好他的金丝夹鼻眼镜，朝所指的方向望去：“啊！莱特富特·李夫人！我想我自己也该去同她说句话；”说罢便把特派记者丢在背后，以年轻人的轻捷，一溜烟追赶那位伊利诺伊州参议员去了。

“见鬼！”安德鲁斯先生咕哝道；“这些老傻瓜中了什么邪？”接着，他仰望正与拉特克利夫窃窃私语的李夫人，用更低的咕哝声自语：“这一条，要不要写成新闻？”

===Original===
When young Mr. Schneidekoupon called upon Senator Ratcliffe to invite him to the dinner at Welckley’s, he found that gentleman overwhelmed with work, as he averred, and very little disposed to converse. No! he did not now go out to dinner. In the present condition of the public business he found it impossible to spare the time for such amusements. He regretted to decline Mr. Schneidekoupon’s civility, but there were imperative reasons why he should abstain for the present from social entertainments; he had made but one exception to his rule, and only at the pressing request of his old friend Senator Clinton, and on a very special occasion.
===Chinese===
年轻的施奈德库彭先生登门邀请拉特克利夫参议员赴韦尔克利餐馆的宴会时，发现那位先生据他自己说公务缠身，很不情愿交谈。不去了！他如今不赴宴。就目前公务的状况而言，他挤不出工夫来消遣。他很遗憾不得不辞谢施奈德库彭先生的好意，但有迫不得已的理由，他目前必须戒绝社交宴饮；他的规矩只破过一次例，还是经老友克林顿参议员再三敦请，且在一场极特殊的场合。

===Original===
Mr. Schneidekoupon was deeply vexed—the more, he said, because he had meant to beg Mr. and Mrs. Clinton to be of the party, as well as a very charming lady who rarely went into society, but who had almost consented to come.

“Who is that?” inquired the Senator.

“A Mrs. Lightfoot Lee, of New York. Probably you do not know her well enough to admire her as I do; but I think her quite the most intelligent woman I ever met.”

The Senator’s cold eyes rested for a moment on the young man’s open face with a peculiar expression of distrust. Then he solemnly said, in his deepest senatorial tones:

“My young friend, at my time of life men have other things to occupy them than women, however intelligent they may be. Who else is to be of your party?”
===Chinese===
施奈德库彭先生大为懊恼——他说，尤其因为他本打算恳请克林顿先生偕夫人一同赴宴，外加一位极少出入社交界、却已几乎答应赏光的非常可爱的女士。

“是哪一位？”参议员问道。

“一位纽约来的莱特富特·李夫人。您对她的了解也许还不及我，没能像我这样倾慕她；不过我认为，她实在是我平生所见最聪明的女人。”

参议员那双冷冰冰的眼睛，在年轻人坦率的脸上停了片刻，带着一种说不出的不信任的神情。随后，他用最深沉的参议员腔调，庄重地说：

“年轻的朋友，到了我这把年纪，男人们心里装的是别的事情，轮不到女人，不管她们多么聪明。您的宴席上还有谁？”

===Original===
Mr. Schneidekoupon named his list.

“And for Saturday evening at seven, did you say?”

“Saturday at seven.”

“I fear there is little chance of my attending, but I will not absolutely decline. Perhaps when the moment arrives, I may find myself able to be there. But do not count upon me—do not count upon me. Good day, Mr. Schneidekoupon.”
===Chinese===
施奈德库彭报了他的名单。

“是礼拜六晚上七点，您是说？”

“礼拜六七点。”

“我怕到场的机会渺茫，但也不绝对回绝。也许时辰一到，我会发现自己来得成。但别指望我——别指望我。日安，施奈德库彭先生。”

===Original===
Schneidekoupon was rather a simple-minded young man, who saw no deeper than his neighbours into the secrets of the universe, and he went off swearing roundly at “the infernal airs these senators give themselves.” He told Mrs. Lee all the conversation, as indeed he was compelled to do under penalty of bringing her to his party under false pretences.

“Just my luck,” said he; “here I am forced to ask no end of people to meet a man, who at the same time says he shall probably not come. Why, under the stars, couldn’t he say, like other people, whether he was coming or not? I’ve known dozens of senators, Mrs. Lee, and they’re all like that. They never think of anyone but themselves.”
===Chinese===
施奈德库彭是个心思相当单纯的年轻人，对宇宙奥秘的见识并不比邻居们更深；他一边走，一边狠狠咒骂“这些参议员摆的可恶架子”。他把全部谈话一五一十告诉了李夫人——他也实在不得不如此，否则便是存心诓骗她赴宴。

“我就是这号运气，”他说；“我被迫请来没完没了的一堆客人，就为陪一位老爷，这位老爷偏又说多半不来。哎呀，老天爷在上，他怎么就不能像常人一样，痛痛快快给个准话，到底来还是不来？我认得的参议员没有几十也有几打，李夫人，个个都是这副德行。他们从来不把任何人放在心上，只想着自己。”

===Original===
Mrs. Lee smiled rather a forced smile, and soothed his wounded feelings; she had no doubt the dinner would be very agreeable whether the Senator were there or not; at any rate she would do all she could to carry it off well, and Sybil should wear her newest dress. Still she was a little grave, and Mr. Schneidekoupon could only declare that she was a trump; that he had told Ratcliffe she was the cleverest woman he ever met, and he might have added the most obliging, and Ratcliffe had only looked at him as though he were a green ape. At all which Mrs. Lee laughed good-naturedly, and sent him away as soon as she could.
===Chinese===
李夫人挤出一个颇为勉强的微笑，抚慰他受伤的感情；她毫不怀疑，无论参议员到不到场，这顿晚餐都会十分惬意；无论如何她会竭力把它办得圆满，西比尔要穿上她最新的衣裙。不过她仍有一分凝重；施奈德库彭先生只会连声宣称她“真够意思”；说他告诉过拉特克利夫，她是他平生所见最聪明的女人——他满可以再添上一句，也是最热心助人的——而拉特克利夫只是瞧着他，仿佛瞧着一只绿毛猿。对这一切，李夫人好脾气地笑了，随即尽快打发他走。

===Original===
When he was gone, she walked up and down the room and thought. She saw the meaning of Ratcliffe’s sudden change in tone.
