You will see that I have got you down for the Treasury. Will you look at the list and say what you think of it?”

===Chinese===
洛德先生学着惟妙惟肖的腔调，描摹总统言语举止的种种特色，拉特克利夫听得阴阴一笑，却什么也不说，只静候事变。当天傍晚，总统的私人秘书送来一封便简，请他明日、即星期六上午十点，如蒙拨冗莅临。便简写得简慢而冷淡。拉特克利夫只回了句话，说他一定到；心里倒有几分惋惜，总统竟连这点礼数都不通，不明白这句口信的意思，是提点他放规矩些。他如约而至，只见总统的脸色比先前更加阴沉。这一回，犯忌讳的话题再也绕不开了。总统存心要用手起刀落的果断，向拉特克利夫表明：局面的主人是他。他一开口便单刀直入：“我请你来，”他说，“是要同你商量我的内阁。这是我打算延请入阁的各位先生的名单。你会看到，我把你定在了财政部。请你过过目，说说你的看法。”

===Original===
Ratcliffe took the paper, but laid it at once on the table without looking at it. “I can have no objection,” said he, “to any Cabinet you may appoint, provided I am not included in it. My wish is to remain where I am. There I can serve your administration better than in the Cabinet.”

“Then you refuse?” growled the President.

“By no means. I only decline to offer any advice or even to hear the names of my proposed colleagues until it is decided that my services are necessary. If they are, I shall accept without caring with whom I serve.”

===Chinese===
拉特克利夫接过纸来，却立刻往桌上一放，看也不看。“只要不把我本人算在内，”他说，“你任命什么样的内阁，我都无异议。我的愿望是留在现在的位子上。在那儿，我对你这届政府的用处，比进了内阁还要大。”

“那么你是拒绝了？”总统低吼道。

“决无此意。我不过是想说明，在确定非我效力不可之前，我拒绝提出任何建议，连拟议中同僚的名单都不想听。倘若果真用得着我，我便接受，同谁共事在所不计。”

===Original===
The President glared at him with an uneasy look. What was to be done next? He wanted time to think, but Ratcliffe was there and must be disposed of. He involuntarily became more civil: “Mr. Ratcliffe, your refusal would knock everything on the head. I thought that matter was all fixed. What more can I do?”

===Chinese===
总统瞪着他，眼里透着不安。下一步该怎么办？他需要时间想一想，可拉特克利夫就坐在眼前，总得打发走。他不由得客气了几分：“拉特克利夫先生，你若拒绝，一切就全砸了。我还以为那件事早已说定。还要我怎样呢？”

===Original===
But Ratcliffe had no mind to let the President out of his clutches so easily, and a long conversation followed, during which he forced his antagonist into the position of urging him to take the Treasury in order to prevent some undefined but portentous mischief in the Senate. All that could be agreed upon was that Ratcliffe should give a positive answer within two days, and on that agreement he took his leave.

===Chinese===
可拉特克利夫无意这么轻易地放总统脱出自己的掌心。接下来是一场长谈；谈来谈去，他竟迫使对手落到了这般田地：反过来敦促他出任财政部长，以求防止参议院里生出某种说不出名目、却凶险可怖的乱子。最后谈定的只有一条：拉特克利夫须在两天之内给出明确的答复。他便挟着这一协议告辞了。

===Original===
As he passed through the corridor, a number of gentlemen were waiting for interviews with the President, and among them was the whole Pennsylvania delegation, “ready for biz,” as Mr. Tom Lord remarked, with a wink. Ratcliffe drew Krebs aside and they exchanged a few words as he passed out. Ten minutes afterwards the delegation was admitted, and some of its members were a little surprised to hear their spokesman, Senator Krebs, press with extreme earnestness and in their names, the appointment of Josiah B. Carson to a place in the Cabinet, when they had been given to understand that they came to recommend Jared Caldwell as postmaster of Philadelphia. But Pennsylvania is a great and virtuous State, whose representatives have entire confidence in their chief. Not one of them so much as winked.

===Chinese===
他穿过走廊时，一群先生正候着总统召见，其中就有宾夕法尼亚代表团全体——用汤姆·洛德先生眨着眼的话说，“随时可谈买卖”。拉特克利夫把克雷布斯拉到一旁，出门时同他交换了几句。十分钟之后，代表团获准入内；其中几位不免有点纳罕：他们的发言人克雷布斯参议员，竟以极度的恳切、打着他们的旗号，力荐延揽乔赛亚·B. 卡森（Josiah B. Carson）入阁；而他们原先领会的任务，却是来保举贾里德·考德威尔（Jared Caldwell）出任费城邮政局长的。不过，宾夕法尼亚是个伟大而高尚的州，它的代表们对自家首领有着全盘的信任。连一个眨眼的都没有。

===Original===
The dance of democracy round the President now began again with wilder energy. Ratcliffe launched his last bolts. His two-days’ delay was a mere cover for bringing new influences to bear. He needed no delay. He wanted no time for reflection. The President had undertaken to put him on the horns of a dilemma; either to force him into a hostile and treacherous Cabinet, or to throw on him the blame of a refusal and a quarrel. He meant to embrace one of the horns and to impale the President on it, and he felt perfect confidence in his own success. He meant to accept the Treasury and he was ready to back himself with a heavy wager to get the government entirely into his own hands within six weeks. His contempt for the Hoosier Stonecutter was unbounded, and his confidence in himself more absolute than ever.

===Chinese===
围着总统的民主之舞，此刻又以更狂野的劲头跳将起来。拉特克利夫掷出了他最后的霹雳。他那两天的拖延，不过是道幌子，好调集新的影响来发生作用。他并不需要拖延。他不需要思考的时间。总统原打算把他架上两难困境的尖角：要么逼他进一个怀敌意、藏祸心的内阁，要么把拒不受命、挑起争端的罪责推到他头上。他却打算干脆抱住其中一只角，反把总统戳在这只角上；而且对于自己的成功，他有着十足的把握。他打定主意接受财政部，并且愿意下重注担保：不出六个星期，便把整个政府原原本本攥进自己手心。他对这位胡希尔石匠的轻蔑漫无止境，他对自己的信心，则比以往任何时候都更加绝对。

===Original===
Busy as he was, the Senator made his appearance the next evening at Mrs. Lee’s, and finding her alone with Sybil, who was occupied with her own little devices, Ratcliffe told Madeleine the story of his week’s experience. He did not dwell on his exploits. On the contrary he quite ignored those elaborate arrangements which had taken from the President his power of volition. His picture presented himself, solitary and unprotected, in the character of that honest beast who was invited to dine with the lion and saw that all the footmarks of his predecessors led into the lion’s cave, and none away from it. He described in humorous detail his interviews with the Indiana lion, and the particulars of the surfeit of lobster as given in the President’s dialect; he even repeated to her the story told him by Mr. Tom Lord, without omitting oaths or gestures; he told her how matters stood at the moment, and how the President had laid a trap for him which he could not escape; he must either enter a Cabinet constructed on purpose to thwart him and with the certainty of ignominious dismissal at the first opportunity, or he must refuse an offer of friendship which would throw on him the blame of a quarrel, and enable the President to charge all future difficulties to the account of Ratcliffe’s “insatiable ambition.” “And now, Mrs. Lee,” he continued, with increasing seriousness of tone; “I want your advice; what shall I do?”

===Chinese===
参议员纵然忙碌，第二天晚上仍旧到李夫人家露了面；见她正单独同西比尔在一处——西比尔自顾自忙着她那些小小的玩意儿——拉特克利夫便把他这一周的经历讲给马德琳听。他并不铺陈自己的战绩。恰恰相反，那些煞费经营、夺走了总统意志自由的布置，他一概略过不提。他给自己画出的形象，是孤零零、无遮无傍，正是那头应邀赴狮子宴的老实畜生，眼看着先前那些客人的脚印个个通向狮子洞，却没有一个走出来的。他妙趣横生地细述他同那头印第安纳狮子几番会面的经过，还用总统的方言土语，把龙虾吃出祸来的前前后后复述了一遍；他甚至把汤姆·洛德先生讲给他听的那段故事，原原本本转述给她，赌咒和手势一概不删；他告诉她眼下的局面如何，总统如何给他设下了一个逃不脱的圈套：他要么进一个专为掣肘他而拼凑、而且一到时机便注定要受辱被逐的内阁；要么回绝这一番示好，从而把失和的责任揽到自己头上，让总统日后得以把一切难关，都记在拉特克利夫“欲壑难填的野心”账上。“而现在，李夫人，”他接着说，语气越发郑重；“我要听听你的高见；我该怎么办？”

===Original===
Even this half revelation of the meanness which distorted politics; this one-sided view of human nature in its naked deformity playing pranks with the interests of forty million people, disgusted and depressed Madeleine’s mind. Ratclife spared her nothing except the exposure of his own moral sores. He carefully called her attention to every leprous taint upon his neighbours’ persons, to every rag in their foul clothing, to every slimy and fetid pool that lay beside their path. It was his way of bringing his own qualities into relief. He meant that she should go hand in hand with him through the brimstone lake, and the more repulsive it seemed to her, the more overwhelming would his superiority become. He meant to destroy those doubts of his character which Carrington was so carefully fostering, to rouse her sympathy, to stimulate her feminine sense of self-sacrifice.

===Chinese===
哪怕只揭开一半——政治竟已被这等卑污所扭曲；哪怕只是单面的一瞥——人性竟以它赤裸裸的畸形嘴相，拿四千万人的利益耍把戏：这已经让马德琳心里又厌恶，又消沉。拉特克利夫什么都没有饶过她，唯独不暴露自己道德上的脓疮。他细心地引她的目光，去看邻人们身上每一处麻风似的污痕，看他们秽衣上的每一条破缕，看他们路旁每一汪黏滑恶臭的死水。这是他衬托自身品色的手法。他要她同他手挽着手，蹚过这片硫磺火湖；这湖在她眼里越是可憎，他的优越便越是势不可当。他要摧毁卡林顿苦心浇灌的那些疑团，要唤起她的怜悯，要挑起她那女性的自我牺牲感。

===Original===
When he asked this question she looked up at him with an expression of indignant pride, as she spoke:

“I say again, Mr. Ratcliffe, what I said once before. Do whatever is most for the public good.”

“And what *is* most for the public good?”

===Chinese===
他问出这一句时，她抬起头来望着他，带着一副又愤激又骄傲的神情，开口说道：

“我再说一遍，拉特克利夫先生，把我从前说过的话再说一遍：怎么做最符合公共利益，就怎么做。”

“那么，究竟什么*才*最符合公共利益？”

===Original===
Madeleine half opened her mouth to reply, then hesitated, and stared silently into the fire before her. What was indeed most for the public good? Where did the public good enter at all into this maze of personal intrigue, this wilderness of stunted natures where no straight road was to be found, but only the tortuous and aimless tracks of beasts and things that crawl? Where was she to look for a principle to guide, an ideal to set up and to point at?

===Chinese===
马德琳嘴张了一半想要回答，随即又犹豫了，默默地盯着面前的炉火出神。究竟什么才最符合公共利益？在这座由私人阴谋织成的迷宫里，在这片由发育不全的天性构成的荒野里——这里寻不到一条直路，只有走兽和爬虫留下的曲折而漫无目的的踪迹——公共利益究竟从哪里插得进脚？她该到哪里去寻一条指引方向的原则、一个竖起来指点江山的理念？

===Original===
Ratcliffe resumed his appeal, and his manner was more serious than ever.

“I am hard pressed, Mrs. Lee. My enemies encompass me about. They mean to ruin me. I honestly wish to do my duty. You once said that personal considerations should have no weight. Very well! throw them away! And now tell me what I should do.”

===Chinese===
拉特克利夫重新恳求起来，态度比先前更加郑重。

“我处境艰难，李夫人。我的仇人四面团团围住。他们要毁掉我。我诚心诚意想尽我的本分。你从前说过，个人的利害得失不该有分量。很好！把它们抛开吧！现在告诉我，我该怎么办。”

===Original===
For the first time, Mrs. Lee began to feel his power. He was simple, straightforward, earnest. His words moved her. How should she imagine that he was playing upon her sensitive nature precisely as he played upon the President’s coarse one, and that this heavy western politician had the instincts of a wild Indian in their sharpness and quickness of perception; that he divined her character and read it as he read the faces and tones of thousands from day to day? She was uneasy under his eye. She began a sentence, hesitated in the middle, and broke down. She lost her command of thought, and sat dumbfounded. He had to draw her out of the confusion he had himself made.

===Chinese===
李夫人有生以来第一次，开始感到了他的力量。他朴实、直率、恳切。他的话打动了她。她哪里想得到，他此刻拨弄她这副敏感的天性，同他拨弄总统那副粗钝的天性，手法分毫不差；她哪里想得到，这个笨重的西部政客，在知觉的敏锐与迅捷上，怀有野印第安人的本能；他一眼看穿她的性格，就像他日复一日读熟了成千上万人的脸相和腔调一样？在他的目光下，她如坐针毡。她起了一个头，说到一半便迟疑起来，终于垮了下去。她失去了驾驭思绪的能力，呆呆地坐着。他只得亲手把她从他自己搅出来的迷乱里领出去。

===Original===
“I see your meaning in your face. You say that I should accept the duty and disregard the consequences.”

“I don’t know,” said Madeleine, hesitatingly; “Yes, I think that would be my feeling.”

===Chinese===
“我从你脸上看出你的意思了。你是说，我应当担起这份职责，后果一概不计。”

“我说不好，”马德琳迟疑地说；“是了，我想那会是我的心情。”

===Original===
“And when I fall a sacrifice to that man’s envy and intrigue, what will you think then, Mrs. Lee? Will you not join the rest of the world and say that I overreached myself, and walked into this trap with my eyes open, and for my own objects? Do you think I shall ever be thought better of, for getting caught here? I don’t parade high moral views like our friend French. I won’t cant about virtue. But I do claim that in my public life I have tried to do right. Will you do me the justice to think so?”

Madeleine still struggled to prevent herself from being drawn into indefinite promises of sympathy with this man. She would keep him at arm’s length whatever her sympathies might be. She would not pledge herself to espouse his cause. She turned upon him with an effort, and said that her thoughts, now or at any time, were folly and nonsense, and that the consciousness of right-doing was the only reward any public man had a right to expect.

===Chinese===
“等我成了那人的妒恨与阴谋的牺牲品，那时你又会怎么想，李夫人？你会不会也学世人那样，说我弄巧成拙，说我睁着眼睛、为着自家的图谋，走进了这一个圈套？你以为我栽在这里，人家日后倒会高看我一眼么？我不像我们的朋友弗伦奇那样，把高调的道德见解挂在旗杆上。我也不愿拿美德唱高调。但我确实要说明：在我的公众生涯里，我一直力求行得正。你可肯主持这个公道，也这样想？”

马德琳仍在挣扎，不肯让自己被卷进对这个人的无边无际的同情许诺里去。无论心里如何同情，她都要同他保持一臂之遥。她不愿担保自己投身于他的事业。她费了一把力气，回身向他说道：她的想法，如今也好，无论何时也好，都是愚蠢的胡话；一个公众人物有权指望的唯一报偿，唯有自知行善的那一点良心。

===Original===
“And yet you are a hard critic, Mrs. Lee. If your thoughts are what you say, your words are not. You judge with the judgment of abstract principles, and you wield the bolts of divine justice. You look on and condemn, but you refuse to acquit. When I come to you on the verge of what is likely to be the fatal plunge of my life, and ask you only for some clue to the moral principle that ought to guide me, you look on and say that virtue is its own reward. And you do not even say where virtue lies.”

===Chinese===
“然而你这个批评家是严酷的，李夫人。倘若你的想法果真如你所说，你的话可就不是了。你用抽象原则的尺度下判断，你挥的是上天雷霆的霹雳。你旁观着、定罪着，却不肯赦免。当我来到你面前，正走到这一生也许致命的一跳的边上，只求你给我一点线索，寻着那条本该指引我的道德原则，你却旁观着，说美德本身就是报偿。你甚至不肯说一说，美德究竟在哪里。”

===Original===
“I confess my sins,” said Madeleine, meekly and despondently; “life is more complicated than I thought.”

“I shall be guided by your advice,” said Ratcliffe; “I shall walk into that den of wild beasts, since you think I ought. But I shall hold you to your responsibility. You cannot refuse to see me through dangers you have helped to bring me into.”

“No, no!” cried Madeleine, earnestly; “no responsibility. You ask more than I can give.”

===Chinese===
“我认我的罪，”马德琳温顺而颓然地说；“生活比我原来想的复杂。”

“我会依你的忠告行事，”拉特克利夫说；“既然你认为我应当，我就走进那座兽窟去。但我要你守住你的责任。这些危难里既有你出的一份力，把我送进来的，你就不能拒绝看着我闯过去。”

“不，不！”马德琳恳切地叫道；“没有什么责任。你索要的，超过了我所能给的。”

===Original===
Ratcliffe looked at her a moment with a troubled and careworn face. His eyes seemed deep sunk in their dark circles, and his voice was pathetic in its intensity. “Duty is duty, for you as well as for me. I have a right to the help of all pure minds. You have no right to refuse it. How can you reject your own responsibility and hold me to mine?”

===Chinese===
拉特克利夫带着烦乱忧劳的神色，望了她片刻。他的眼睛仿佛深深陷进乌黑的眼眶里，他的嗓音激切得近乎哀恳。“职责就是职责，于你如此，于我也如此。一切纯洁的心灵，我都有权向之求援。你无权拒绝。你怎么能否认你自己那份责任，却偏要我守住我这份？”

===Original===
Almost as he spoke, he rose and took his departure, leaving her no time to do more than murmur again her ineffectual protest. After he was gone, Mrs. Lee sat long, with her eyes fixed on the fire, reflecting upon what he had said. Her mind was bewildered by the new suggestions which Ratcliffe had thrown out. What woman of thirty, with aspirations for the infinite, could resist an attack like this? What woman with a soul could see before her the most powerful public man of her time, appealing﻿—with a face furrowed by anxieties, and a voice vibrating with only half-suppressed affection﻿—to her for counsel and sympathy, without yielding some response? and what woman could have helped bowing her head to that rebuke of her overconfident judgment, coming as it did from one who in the same breath appealed to that judgment as final? Ratcliffe, too, had a curious instinct for human weaknesses. No magnetic needle was ever truer than his finger when he touched the vulnerable spot in an opponent’s mind. Mrs. Lee was not to be reached by an appeal to religious sentiment, to ambition, or to affection. Any such appeal would have fallen flat on her ears and destroyed its own hopes. But she was a woman to the very last drop of her blood. She could not be induced to love Ratcliffe, but she might be deluded into sacrificing herself for him. She atoned for want of devotion to God, by devotion to man. She had a woman’s natural tendency towards asceticism, self-extinction, self-abnegation. All through life she had made painful efforts to understand and follow out her duty. Ratcliffe knew her weak point when he attacked her from this side. Like all great orators and advocates, he was an actor; the more effective because of a certain dignified air that forbade familiarity. He had appealed to her sympathy, her sense of right and of duty, to her courage, her loyalty, her whole higher nature; and while he made this appeal he felt more than half convinced that he was all he pretended to be, and that he really had a right to her devotion. What wonder that she in her turn was more than half inclined to admit that right. She knew him now better than Carrington or Jacobi knew him. Surely a man who spoke as he spoke, had noble instincts and lofty aims? Was not his career a thousand times more important than hers? If he, in his isolation and his cares, needed her assistance, had she an excuse for refusing it? What was there in her aimless and useless life which made it so precious that she could not afford to fling it into the gutter, if need be, on the bare chance of enriching some fuller existence?


===Chinese===
话犹未了，他已起身离去，只留给她刚刚够把那番无效的抗议再低声咕哝一遍的工夫。他走之后，李夫人久久坐着，两眼凝望炉火，细味他方才的话。拉特克利夫抛出的这些新的暗示，搅得她心神迷乱。一个三十岁、胸怀无限之志的女人，怎能抵挡这样的进攻？一个有灵魂的女人，眼见她那个时代最有权势的政界人物立在面前——一张脸被忧虑犁出沟壑，一副嗓音因半遭压抑的爱慕而颤抖——向她恳求指点与怜惜，怎能不作出几分回应？而那一番对她过分自信的判断的训斥——训斥者同一口气里，又偏偏奉这判断为终裁——她又怎能不低下头去？拉特克利夫对于人的弱点，还有一种奇妙的直觉。当他的手指按在对手心中的痛处上时，世上没有一根磁针比它更准。李夫人不是那种用宗教感情、用野心、用情爱所能打动的人。任何这一类的进攻，都只会在她耳边落空，反倒毁了它自身的指望。可她是彻头彻尾、直到最后一滴血都是女人。要引得她爱上拉特克利夫是办不到的，但哄得她为他牺牲自己，却是可能的。她以对人的奉献，补赎了对上帝奉献的欠缺。她身上带着女人天然趋向苦行、自我消泯、自我舍弃的倾向。她一生都在痛苦地努力，去理解并践行自己的责任。拉特克利夫从这一侧进攻，正打中了她的软处。同一切伟大的演说家和辩护士一样，他是个演员；又因自有一种拒人千里的庄重气派，而愈发见效。他诉诸了她的怜悯，她的正义感与责任感，她的勇气，她的忠悃，她全部更高的天性；而在发出这一番呼吁的同时，他自己也多半信了：他当真就是他装出来的那个人，当真有权利获得她的奉献。无怪乎她这一边，也多半倾向于承认这一权利。她如今对他的了解，已胜过卡林顿或雅各比对他的了解。一个说话如他这般说话的人，天性必然高尚、志向必然高远吧？他的事业，难道不比她的重要一千倍？倘若他在他的孤寂与忧劳之中需要她的臂助，她还有什么推辞的借口？她这漫无目的、百无一用的一生里，究竟有什么东西金贵到这般地步，使她舍不得——倘若有此必要——把它抛进阴沟，只为那一线渺茫的机会，去充实另一段更为丰盈的生命？

## VIII / 八

===Original===
Of all titles ever assumed by prince or potentate, the proudest is that of the Roman pontiffs: *Servus servorum Dei*﻿—“Servant of the servants of God.” In former days it was not admitted that the devil’s servants could by right have any share in government. They were to be shut out, punished, exiled, maimed, and burned. The devil has no servants now; only the people have servants. There may be some mistake about a doctrine which makes the wicked, when a majority, the mouthpiece of God against the virtuous, but the hopes of mankind are staked on it; and if the weak in faith sometimes quail when they see humanity floating in a shoreless ocean, on this plank, which experience and religion long since condemned as rotten, mistake or not, men have thus far floated better by its aid, than the popes ever did with their prettier principle; so that it will be a long time yet before society repents.

Whether the new President and his chief rival, Mr. Silas P. Ratcliffe, were or were not servants of the servants of God, is not material here. Servants they were to someone. No doubt many of those who call themselves servants of the people are no better than wolves in sheep’s clothing, or asses in lions’ skins. One may see scores of them any day in the Capitol when Congress is in session, making noisy demonstrations, or more usefully doing nothing. A wiser generation will employ them in manual labour; as it is, they serve only themselves. But there are two officers, at least, whose service is real﻿—the President and his Secretary of the Treasury. The Hoosier Quarryman had not been a week in Washington before he was heartily homesick for Indiana. No maid-of-all-work in a cheap boardinghouse was ever more harassed. Everyone conspired against him. His enemies gave him no peace. All Washington was laughing at his blunders, and ribald sheets, published on a Sunday, took delight in printing the new Chief Magistrate’s sayings and doings, chronicled with outrageous humour, and placed by malicious hands where the President could not but see them. He was sensitive to ridicule, and it mortified him to the heart to find that remarks and acts, which to him seemed sensible enough, should be capable of such perversion. Then he was overwhelmed with public business. It came upon him in a deluge, and he now, in his despair, no longer tried to control it. He let it pass over him like a wave. His mind was muddied by the innumerable visitors to whom he had to listen. But his greatest anxiety was the Inaugural Address which, distracted as he was, he could not finish, although in another week it must be delivered. He was nervous about his Cabinet; it seemed to him that he could do nothing until he had disposed of Ratcliffe. Already, thanks to the President’s friends, Ratcliffe had become indispensable; still an enemy, of course, but one whose hands must be tied; a sort of Sampson, to be kept in bonds until the time came for putting him out of the way, but in the meanwhile, to be utilized. This point being settled, the President had in imagination begun to lean upon him; for the last few days he had postponed everything till next week, “when I get my Cabinet arranged;” which meant, when he got Ratcliffe’s assistance; and he fell into a panic whenever he thought of the chance that Ratcliffe might refuse.
===Chinese===
古往今来，君王霸主们用过的种种头衔里，最傲的莫过于罗马教皇的那一顶：*Servus servorum Dei*——“天主众仆之仆”。往昔的年代不承认魔鬼的仆役有权分沾政府分毫。他们是要被拒之门外、受惩罚、遭放逐、被致残、被烧死的。如今魔鬼没有仆役了；有仆役的只有人民。一条教义，使恶人一旦成了多数，便化作上帝对付善人的喉舌，其中未必没有差错；可人类的希望全都押在它上面；倘若信仰薄弱的人，眼见人类漂浮在一片无岸的海上，脚下只有这一条经验与宗教早判定为朽木的船板，有时不免胆寒——那么，不管是不是差错，人类靠它的扶助，至今漂浮得比历代教皇抱着他们那条更漂亮的原则所做到的还要妥帖；所以说，社会要想悔悟，还早着呢。

新总统同他的头号对手赛拉斯·P. 拉特克利夫先生，究竟算不算天主众仆之仆，此处无关宏旨。他们总归是什么人的仆役。毫无疑问，许多自称人民公仆的人，不过是披着羊皮的狼，或是披着狮子皮的驴。国会开会期间，在国会大厦里天天可以撞见他们几十成群，不是吵吵嚷嚷地表演，就是更有用处地无所事事。将来更聪明的一代人，会打发他们去卖力气；眼下呢，他们只伺候自己。但至少有两个官儿，仆役当得货真价实——总统和他的财政部长。这位胡希尔采石工到华盛顿不满一个星期，就实打实地害起了思乡病，一心惦记印第安纳。廉价寄宿公寓里的打杂女佣，也没受过比这更狠的作践。人人都在同他作对。他的敌人不给他一刻安生。整个华盛顿都在笑话他的岔子；礼拜天出的那些泼辣小报，更津津有味地登载新任最高行政长官的言行，用无法无天的谐笔一一记下，再由心怀恶意的手，摆到总统不看也得看见的地方。他对嘲笑最是敏感；在他自己看来够得体的言行，竟会被人歪曲成这副模样，真叫他羞恼彻骨。再说，公务又劈头盖脑压了下来。它像山洪一样当头浇下，他在绝望之余索性不再设法驾驭，任它像一道浪头从身上滚过去。他不得不听的来访者多得不计其数，把他的脑子搅成了一潭浑水。而他最大的焦虑，还是那篇就职演说：他心烦意乱，怎么也写不完，可再过一个星期就得登台去讲。他为他的内阁揪着心；在他看来，不先把拉特克利夫处置妥当，他就什么事也办不成。如今，托总统的朋友们的诸般运作之福，拉特克利夫已经成了须臾离不得的人物；当然还是个敌人，却是个必须捆住双手的敌人；一尊参孙式的人物，先拿绳索捆着，等时机一到再除掉，但在那之前，还得派用场。这一点既经议定，总统便在想象中开始倚靠起他来；这几天他把一切统统推到下星期，“等我把内阁安排停当”；这就是说，等他得到拉特克利夫的臂助；而每当想到拉特克利夫可能拒绝，他便一阵恐慌。

===Original===
He was pacing his room impatiently on Monday morning, an hour before the time fixed for Ratcliffe’s visit. His feelings still fluctuated violently, and if he recognized the necessity of using Ratcliffe, he was not the less determined to tie Ratcliffe’s hands. He must be made to come into a Cabinet where every other voice would be against him. He must be prevented from having any patronage to dispose of. He must be induced to accept these conditions at the start. How present this to him in such a way as not to repel him at once? All this was needless, if the President had only known it, but he thought himself a profound statesman, and that his hand was guiding the destinies of America to his own reelection. When at length, on the stroke of ten o’clock, Ratcliffe entered the room, the President turned to him with nervous eagerness, and almost before offering his hand, said that he hoped Mr. Ratcliffe had come prepared to begin work at once. The Senator replied that, if such was the President’s decided wish, he would offer no further opposition. Then the President drew himself up in the attitude of an American Cato, and delivered a prepared address, in which he said that he had chosen the members of his Cabinet with a careful regard to the public interests; that Mr. Ratcliffe was essential to the combination; that he expected no disagreement on principles, for there was but one principle which he should consider fundamental, namely, that there should be no removals from office except for cause; and that under these circumstances he counted upon Mr. Ratcliffe’s assistance as a matter of patriotic duty.

To all this Ratcliffe assented without a word of objection, and the President, more convinced than ever of his own masterly statesmanship, breathed more freely than for a week past. Within ten minutes they were actively at work together, clearing away the mass of accumulated business. The relief of the Quarryman surprised himself. Ratcliffe lifted the weight of affairs from his shoulders with hardly an effort. He knew everybody and everything. He took most of the President’s visitors at once into his own hands and dismissed them with great rapidity. He knew what they wanted; he knew what recommendations were strong and what were weak; who was to be treated with deference and who was to be sent away abruptly; where a blunt refusal was safe, and where a pledge was allowable. The President even trusted him with the unfinished manuscript of the Inaugural Address, which Ratcliffe returned to him the next day with such notes and suggestions as left nothing to be done beyond copying them out in a fair hand. With all this, he proved himself a very agreeable companion. He talked well and enlivened the work; he was not a hard taskmaster, and when he saw that the President was tired, he boldly asserted that there was no more business that could not as well wait a day, and so took the weary Stonecutter out to drive for a couple of hours, and let him go peacefully to sleep in the carriage. They dined together and Ratcliffe took care to send for Tom Lord to amuse them, for Tom was a wit and a humourist, and kept the President in a laugh. Mr. Lord ordered the dinner and chose the wines. He could be coarse enough to suit even the President’s palate, and Ratcliffe was not behindhand. When the new Secretary went away at ten o’clock that night, his chief, who was in high good humour with his dinner, his champagne, and his conversation, swore with some unnecessary granite oaths, that Ratcliffe was “a clever fellow anyhow,” and he was glad “that job was fixed.”
===Chinese===
星期一上午，离约定拉特克利夫来访的钟点还有一个钟头，他已在房里焦躁地踱来踱去。他的情绪仍在剧烈起落：他既然认定非用拉特克利夫不可，也就同样打定了主意要捆住拉特克利夫的双手。必须让他进一个旁人个个同他唱反调的内阁。必须不让他手上握有半点官缺可分。必须叫他从一开头便把这些条件认下来。可这番话要怎么说出口，才不至于当场把他吓跑呢？倘若总统知道底细，这一切原属多余；无奈他自命为深谋远虑的政治家，以为自己那只手正掌着美国的命运之舵，一路驶向他自己的连任。十点钟声敲响，拉特克利夫终于走进房来，总统带着紧张的急切迎上前去，手还几乎没伸出去，便说，他希望拉特克利夫先生这次来，已经准备好立即开工。参议员答道，倘若这是总统的定见，他不再作梗。于是总统挺起身子，摆出一副美国加图的架势，发表了一篇预备好的演说，其中说道：他遴选阁员，曾以公众利益为审慎的依归；拉特克利夫先生于这个组合必不可少；他预计在原则上不生龃龉，因为在他看来堪称根本的原则只有一条，即除非事出有因，不得撤除任何人的官职；在这种情况下，他把拉特克利夫先生的襄助，当作一桩爱国义务来指望。

对这一切，拉特克利夫一概应承，没有半个不字；总统呢，比先前任何时候都更信服自己那炉火纯青的政治手腕，一个星期以来头一回呼吸得这般畅快。不出十分钟，两人已经埋首合力干起活来，把积压的一大堆公事清理出去。采石工身上那股轻松劲儿，连他自己都觉得意外。拉特克利夫几乎不费什么力气，就把政务的重担从他的肩头卸了下来。他谁都认得，什么都门儿清。总统的来访者，多半被他一把接过手去，三下五除二便打发走了。他知道来人要什么；他知道哪份荐举有分量、哪份没分量；谁该客气相待，谁该三言两语轰走了事；何处不妨直截了当拒死，何处许个愿也无伤大雅。总统甚至把那篇没写完的就职演说手稿也托付给了他；第二天拉特克利夫把手稿送还，上面的批注和建议写得那样妥帖，剩下要做的，不过用工楷誊清一遍而已。在这一切之外，他还证明了自己是个极好相处的伴儿。他谈吐不俗，给公务添了生趣；他也不是个苛厉的监工，见总统乏了，便大着胆子断言，再没有哪件公事不能索性搁上一天，于是带这位困乏的石匠出去兜了两个钟头的风，让他安安稳稳地在马车里睡了一觉。两人一同进餐，拉特克利夫特意打发人把汤姆·洛德请来凑趣，因为汤姆是个妙人，一个插科打诨的角色，逗得总统笑个不住。点菜选酒，都归洛德先生张罗。他粗俗得恰到好处，连总统的口味都伺候得熨帖，拉特克利夫也不落人后。当晚十点钟新部长告辞而去，他的长官正为那顿晚饭、那几杯香槟、那一场清谈而兴致高涨，赌了几句多余的花岗岩式咒誓，说拉特克利夫“到底是个伶俐人”，又说“那桩差事总算办妥了”，他很高兴。

===Original===
The truth was that Ratcliffe had now precisely ten days before the new Cabinet could be set in motion, and in these ten days he must establish his authority over the President so firmly that nothing could shake it. He was diligent in good works. Very soon the court began to feel his hand. If a business letter or a written memorial came in, the President found it easy to endorse: “Referred to the Secretary of the Treasury.” If a visitor wanted anything for himself or another, the invariable reply came to be: “Just mention it to Mr. Ratcliffe;” or, “I guess Ratcliffe will see to that.” Before long he even made jokes in a Catonian manner; jokes that were not peculiarly witty, but somewhat gruff and boorish, yet significant of a resigned and self-contented mind. One morning he ordered Ratcliffe to take an ironclad ship of war and attack the Sioux in Montana, seeing that he was in charge of the army and navy and Indians at once, and Jack of all trades; and again he told a naval officer who wanted a court-martial that he had better get Ratcliffe to sit on him for he was a whole court-martial by himself. That Ratcliffe held his chief in no less contempt than before, was probable but not certain, for he kept silence on the subject before the world, and looked solemn whenever the President was mentioned.

Before three days were over, the President, with a little more than his usual abruptness, suddenly asked him what he knew about this fellow Carson, whom the Pennsylvanians were bothering him to put in his Cabinet. Ratcliffe was guarded: he scarcely knew the man; Mr. Carson was not in politics, he believed, but was pretty respectable﻿—for a Pennsylvanian. The President returned to the subject several times; got out his list of Cabinet officers and figured industriously upon it with a rather perplexed face; called Ratcliffe to help him; and at last the “slate” was fairly broken, and Ratcliffe’s eyes gleamed when the President caused his list of nominations to be sent to the Senate on the 5th March, and Josiah B. Carson, of Pennsylvania, was promptly confirmed as Secretary of the Interior.
===Chinese===
实情是，离新内阁能够开张，拉特克利夫眼下恰恰还有十天；在这十天里，他必须把自己对总统的权威立得固若金汤，叫什么力量也撼它不动。他勤勉地行着他的善事。很快，这班朝臣便开始领教他的手劲。商务信函也好、书面呈文也罢，一送到，总统发现批起来轻松省事：“移交财政部长办理。”来访的人不论替自己还是替别人讨情，得到的回复渐渐成了千篇一律：“跟拉特克利夫先生提一声就行；”再不就是，“我看这事拉特克利夫会料理的。”没过多久，他甚至学着加图的口吻说起笑话来；那些笑话算不得如何俏皮，倒有几分粗声粗气、村夫气，却也透着一副听天由命、踌躇满志的心肠。一天早晨，他命令拉特克利夫调一艘铁甲军舰去蒙大拿攻打苏族印第安人，理由是此人一人竟同时管着陆军、海军和印第安人，是个样样都管的万事通；又一回，一名海军军官请求开庭军事法庭，他告诉人家，不如请拉特克利夫坐到他身上去审，因为此人一个人便顶得一整座军事法庭。至于拉特克利夫对自己这位长官的轻蔑，是否一如既往地深，多半如此，却也无从断言；因为他在世人面前对此讳莫如深，而每逢有人提起总统，他总是一脸庄重。

不出三天，总统便带着比平日更甚的粗直，突然问他：这个卡森，宾夕法尼亚人吵着要塞进内阁的这家伙，他知不知道底细。拉特克利夫守着口风：此人他几乎不识；据他看，卡森先生不问政治，倒也相当体面——以一个宾夕法尼亚人而论。总统把这个话头拾起来好几回；把内阁名单翻出来，皱着一张大惑不解的脸，在上面勤勤恳恳地演算；又唤拉特克利夫来帮他盘算；到头来，原先那块“预定名单板”总算彻底砸碎了。三月五日，总统把提名名单送交参议院，宾夕法尼亚的乔赛亚·B. 卡森顺利获得批准，出任内政部长，拉特克利夫的双眼放出了光。

===Original===
But his eyes gleamed still more humorously when, a few days afterwards, the President gave him a long list of some two score names, and asked him to find places for them. He assented good-naturedly, with a remark that it might be necessary to make a few removals to provide for these cases.

“Oh, well,” said the President, “I guess there’s just about as many as that had ought to go out anyway. These are friends of mine; got to be looked after. Just stuff ’em in somewhere.”

Even he felt a little awkward about it, and, to do him justice, this was the last that was heard about the fundamental rule of his administration.
===Chinese===
可几天之后，总统递给他一张长长的单子，约莫四十来个名字，请他给这些人安插位置，他眼里的笑意就更浓了。他和和气气地应承下来，顺口说了一句：要安置这几桩，恐怕得撤掉几个人的差事。

“哦，这个嘛，”总统说，“我估摸着反正差不多也有这么些个该滚蛋的了。这些都是我的朋友；总得有人照应。随便往哪儿塞一个就得。”

连他自己也觉出几分难堪；说句公道话，他那届政府的根本大法，往后就再没有人听人提起过了。

===Original===
Removals were fast and furious, until all Indiana became easy in circumstances. And it was not to be denied that, by one means or another, Ratcliffe’s friends did come into their fair share of the public money. Perhaps the President thought it best to wink at such use of the Treasury patronage for the present, or was already a little overawed by his Secretary.

Ratcliffe’s work was done. The public had, with the help of some clever intrigue, driven its servants into the traces. Even an Indiana stonecutter could be taught that his personal prejudices must yield to the public service. What mischief the selfishness, the ambition, or the ignorance of these men might do, was another matter. As the affair stood, the President was the victim of his own schemes. It remained to be seen whether, at some future day, Mr. Ratcliffe would think it worth his while to strangle his chief by some quiet Eastern intrigue, but the time had gone by when the President could make use of either the bowstring or the axe upon him.
===Chinese===
撤职免任如疾风骤雨，直到整个印第安纳都家境宽裕起来。而且无可否认，凭这样那样的门道，拉特克利夫的朋友们到底分到了公帑里理所当然的一份。也许总统觉得，对财政部门下官缺的这般用法，眼下还是睁一只眼闭一只眼为妙；也许，他已经被他这位部长镇住了几分。

拉特克利夫的活计干完了。公众借着几分巧妙的机谋，把自己的仆役套进了辕轭。就连一个印第安纳石匠，也能被教会这样的道理：他个人的好恶，必须给公众的差事让路。这些人的自私、野心或无知，日后会闯出什么祸，那是另一回事。就眼下的局面而论，总统是自己算计的牺牲品。至于日后某一天，拉特克利夫先生会不会觉得值得费一番手脚，用东方宫廷那种无声无息的机谋掐死他的长官，且留待分晓；只是总统想在他身上动弓弦或是斧钺的时辰，已经一去不返了。

===Original===
All this passed while Mrs. Lee was quietly puzzling her poor little brain about her duty and her responsibility to Ratcliffe, who, meanwhile, rarely failed to find himself on Sunday evenings by her side in her parlour, where his rights were now so well established that no one presumed to contest his seat, unless it were old Jacobi, who from time to time reminded him that he was fallible and mortal. Occasionally, though not often, Mr. Ratcliffe came at other times, as when he persuaded Mrs. Lee to be present at the Inauguration, and to call on the President’s wife. Madeleine and Sybil went to the Capitol and had the best places to see and hear the Inauguration, as well as a cold March wind would allow. Mrs. Lee found fault with the ceremony; it was of the earth, earthy, she said. An elderly western farmer, with silver spectacles, new and glossy evening clothes, bony features, and stiff, thin, gray hair, trying to address a large crowd of people, under the drawbacks of a piercing wind and a cold in his head, was not a hero. Sybil’s mind was lost in wondering whether the President would not soon die of pneumonia. Even this experience, however, was happy when compared with that of the call upon the President’s wife, after which Madeleine decided to leave the new dynasty alone in future. The lady, who was somewhat stout and coarse-featured, and whom Mrs. Lee declared she wouldn’t engage as a cook, showed qualities which, seen under that fierce light which beats upon a throne, seemed ungracious. Her antipathy to Ratcliffe was more violent than her husband’s, and was even more openly expressed, until the President was quite put out of countenance by it. She extended her hostility to everyone who could be supposed to be Ratcliffe’s friend, and the newspapers, as well as private gossip, had marked out Mrs. Lee as one who, by an alliance with Ratcliffe, was aiming at supplanting her own rule over the White House. Hence, when Mrs. Lightfoot Lee was announced, and the two sisters were ushered into the presidential parlour, she put on a coldly patronizing air, and in reply to Madeleine’s hope that she found Washington agreeable, she intimated that there was much in Washington which struck her as awful wicked, especially the women; and, looking at Sybil, she spoke of the style of dress in this city which she said she meant to do what she could to put a stop to. She’d heard tell that people sent to Paris for their gowns, just as though America wasn’t good enough to make one’s clothes! Jacob (all Presidents’ wives speak of their husbands by their first names) had promised her to get a law passed against it. In her town in Indiana, a young woman who was seen on the street in such clothes wouldn’t be spoken to. At these remarks, made with an air and in a temper quite unmistakable, Madeleine became exasperated beyond measure, and said that “Washington would be pleased to see the President do something in regard to dress-reform﻿—or any other reform;” and with this allusion to the President’s ante-election reform speeches, Mrs. Lee turned her back and left the room, followed by Sybil in convulsions of suppressed laughter, which would not have been suppressed had she seen the face of their hostess as the door shut behind them, and the energy with which she shook her head and said: “See if I don’t reform you yet, you﻿—jade!”
===Chinese===
这一切进行的同时，李夫人正安安静静地绞着她那可怜的小脑仁，为着她对拉特克利夫的本分与责任犯难；而拉特克利夫呢，每逢星期天晚上，几乎从不错过坐到她客厅里她身旁的那个位置——他在那里的权利如今已经根深蒂固，没有人敢来争他的座，只有老雅各比算个例外，还不时提醒他一声：他也会出错，也是个凡人。偶尔——虽说不多——拉特克利夫先生也在别的时辰登门，比如他说动李夫人去观礼就职典礼、去拜会总统夫人的那几回。马德琳和西比尔去了国会大厦，占着顶好的位置看和听这场就职典礼——三月的寒风能容许多好，她们便看了多好、听了多好。李夫人对这场典礼颇多挑剔；她说，这玩意儿属土之物，一身泥土气。一个上了年纪的西部农夫，戴着银丝眼镜，穿着崭新发亮的礼服，瘦骨嶙峋的面孔，稀疏硬挺的花白头发，顶着刺骨的寒风、捂着伤风的鼻子，对着一群人使劲演说——这样的人成不了英雄。西比尔的心思，全用在琢磨总统会不会不久便害肺炎死掉上头了。然而就连这份经历，比起拜会总统夫人那一场来，也算得上快活的了；打那以后，马德琳打定主意，往后再不去招惹那个新王朝。那位夫人生得颇为肥硕，面貌粗蠢，照李夫人的话说，雇她当厨娘她都不肯要；她显露出来的种种品性，放在拍打着宝座的那种烈焰般的强光底下看，实在说不上和善。她对拉特克利夫的憎恶比她丈夫的还激烈，表露得还露骨，闹得总统时常为此下不来台。她把敌意撒向凡是可算作拉特克利夫朋友的人；而报纸连同私底下的闲话，早已把李夫人圈了出来，说她正同拉特克利夫结盟，图谋篡夺她对白宫的统治。因此，通报莱特富特·李夫人的名姓、两姐妹被领进总统客厅的时候，她摆出一副冷冰冰、屈尊纡贵的架子；马德琳客套一句，但愿她觉得华盛顿宜人，她却话里有话：华盛顿有许多东西，在她看来坏得吓人，尤其是这里的女人；又拿眼打量西比尔，说起这座城里穿衣裳的派头，说她打算尽自己的一份力禁一禁。她听说这里的人竟打发人上巴黎置办裙衫，说得好像美国的布料配不上做衣裳似的！雅各布（历届总统夫人提起丈夫，都直呼教名）已经答应她，要立一条法律禁绝此事。在印第安纳她那个镇上，年轻女人穿成这样在街上走，就没有人肯同她讲话。这些话说得神气活现、火气分明，马德琳按捺不住心头火起，回敬道：“华盛顿将乐见总统在服装改良上——或任何别的改良上——有所举措；”这话暗暗点着总统竞选前的那几篇改革演说；说罢，李夫人转身便出了房门，西比尔跟在后头，憋笑憋得浑身乱颤。她若是看见了女主人送客关上门时的那副脸色，看见她摇头晃脑、甩出那句“瞧我不把你这——贱蹄子改造过来！”时使的那股劲头，这笑可就憋不住了。

===Original===
Mrs. Lee gave Ratcliffe a lively account of this interview, and he laughed nearly as convulsively as Sybil over it, though he tried to pacify her by saying that the President’s most intimate friends openly declared his wife to be insane, and that he himself was the person most afraid of her. But Mrs. Lee declared that the President was as bad as his wife; that an equally good President and President’s wife could be picked up in any corner-grocery between the Lakes and the Ohio; and that no inducement should ever make her go near that coarse washerwoman again.

Ratcliffe did not attempt to change Mrs. Lee’s opinion. Indeed he knew better than any man how Presidents were made, and he had his own opinions in regard to the process as well as the fabric produced. Nothing Mrs. Lee could say now affected him. He threw off his responsibility and she found it suddenly resting on her own shoulders. When she spoke with indignation of the wholesale removals from office with which the new administration marked its advent to power, he told her the story of the President’s fundamental principle, and asked her what she would have him do. “He meant to tie my hands,” said Ratcliffe, “and to leave his own free, and I accepted the condition. Can I resign now on such a ground as this?” And Madeleine was obliged to agree that he could not. She had no means of knowing how many removals he made in his own interest, or how far he had outwitted the President at his own game. He stood before her a victim and a patriot. Every step he had taken had been taken with her approval. He was now in office to prevent what evil he could, not to be responsible for the evil that was done; and he honestly assured her that much worse men would come in when he went out, as the President would certainly take good care that he did go out when the moment arrived.
===Chinese===
李夫人把这次会面的经过，活灵活现地对拉特克利夫学了一遍；他听了笑得几乎同西比尔一样浑身乱颤，尽管他试着安抚她，说总统最贴身的朋友们公然宣称总统夫人精神失常，还说天底下最怕她的，正是总统本人。可李夫人一口咬定，总统同他老婆是一路货；这一对总统和总统夫人，在五大湖与俄亥俄河之间随便哪家街角杂货铺里，都能原样再拎出一对来；还说任凭什么诱惑，也休想叫她再靠近那个粗俗的洗衣婆子半步。

拉特克利夫并不打算扭转李夫人的看法。说实在的，总统是怎样造出来的，他比天下任何人都清楚；对这道工序，连同它的制成品，他自有主见。如今李夫人说什么，都撼不动他了。他把责任一甩，她却发觉它冷不防落到了自己肩上。当她愤愤然说起新政府上台时那场成批成串的撤职免官，他便把总统那条根本大法的来龙去脉讲给她听，反问她：依她看，他还能怎样？“他原是要捆住我的手，”拉特克利夫说，“却把他自己的手腾空着，这个条件我认下了。如今我能拿这个由头辞职吗？”马德琳只得承认不能。她无从知道，他为自己的私利做成了多少桩撤换；也无从知道，在这场总统自己最拿手的把戏里，他把总统斗败到了什么地步。他立在她面前，一副受难者兼爱国者的模样。他走过的每一步，都是经她点头才走的。他如今居官，为的是能挡多少坏事便挡多少，而不是为做了的坏事担干系；他还一片至诚地向她担保，等他一走，进来的人要坏得多——而且时候一到，总统必定会好生料理，断然送他出门。

===Original===
Mrs. Lee had the chance now to carry out her scheme in coming to Washington, for she was already deep in the mire of politics and could see with every advantage how the great machine floundered about, bespattering with mud even her own pure garments. Ratcliffe himself, since entering the Treasury, had begun to talk with a sneer of the way in which laws were made, and openly said that he wondered how government got on at all. Yet he declared still that this particular government was the highest expression of political thought. Mrs. Lee stared at him and wondered whether he knew what thought was. To her the government seemed to have less thought in it than one of Sybil’s gowns, for if they, like the government, were monstrously costly, they were at least adapted to their purpose, the parts fitted together, and they were neither awkward nor unwieldy.

There was nothing very encouraging in all this, but it was better than New York. At least it gave her something to look at, and to think about. Even Lord Dunbeg preached practical philanthropy to her by the hour. Ratcliffe, too, was compelled to drag himself out of the rut of machine politics, and to justify his right of admission to her house. There Mr. French discoursed at great length, until the fourth of March sent him home to Connecticut; and he brought more than one intelligent member of Congress to Mrs. Lee’s parlour. Underneath the scum floating on the surface of politics, Madeleine felt that there was a sort of healthy ocean current of honest purpose, which swept the scum before it, and kept the mass pure.
===Chinese===
李夫人如今得了机会，来实践她到华盛顿的那番打算，因为她已经深深陷进政治的泥沼，能够占尽一切便宜，看清这台大机器怎样跌跌撞撞地扑腾，连她自己纯净的裙裾都溅上了泥点。拉特克利夫本人自进财政部以来，也带着冷笑谈论起法律是怎样炮制出来的，还公然说，政府居然还能照常运转，真是叫人纳罕。可他照旧宣称，眼前这个政府，是政治思想最高的表达。李夫人瞪着他，心里嘀咕他到底知不知道思想为何物。在她看来，这政府身上的思想，还不如西比尔的一条裙子多：裙子纵然同政府一样贵得骇人，至少还合它的用途，各个部件拼得拢，既不别扭，也不累赘。

这一切都没有多少鼓舞人心之处，但总比纽约强。至少它给了她可看的东西、可想的东西。连邓贝格勋爵都能对着她一连几个钟头，布讲起实用的慈善经。拉特克利夫也不得不把自己从机器政治的车辙里拖出来，为他出入她府上的资格辩护一番。在她客厅里，弗伦奇先生侃侃而谈，长篇大论，直到三月四日把他打发回了康涅狄格；他还带来过不止一位有头脑的国会议员，到李夫人的客厅里坐坐。在漂着浮沫的政治表层之下，马德琳觉出有一股健康的洋流，由诚实的心愿汇成，把浮沫扫在它前头，叫整体保得洁净。

===Original===
This was enough to draw her on. She reconciled herself to accepting the Ratcliffian morals, for she could see no choice. She herself had approved every step she had seen him take. She could not deny that there must be something wrong in a double standard of morality, but where was it? Mr. Ratcliffe seemed to her to be doing good work with as pure means as he had at hand. He ought to be encouraged, not reviled. What was she that she should stand in judgment?

Others watched her progress with less satisfaction. Mr. Nathan Gore was one of these, for he came in one evening, looking much out of temper, and, sitting down by her side he said he had come to bid goodbye and to thank her for the kindness she had shown him; he was to leave Washington the next morning. She too expressed her warm regret, but added that she hoped he was only going in order to take his passage to Madrid.
===Chinese===
这就足以引着她往前走了。她劝服了自己，认可了拉特克利夫式的道德，因为她看不出还有什么别的可选。他走的每一步，都是她亲眼看着、亲口认可的。她无法否认，一种双重标准的道德观里必有些毛病，可毛病在哪儿呢？在她看来，拉特克利夫先生正干着好事，用的手段，也是他手头所能有的顶纯净的手段。这样的人应该受鼓励，不该挨骂。她自己算老几，凭什么坐在那里做法官？

旁人看着她这样走下去，心里就不那么痛快了。内森·戈尔先生便是其中一个。一天晚上他进来，满脸的不痛快，在她身边坐下，说他此来是道别的，也是谢谢她一路待他的好意；他明天早晨就要离开华盛顿了。她也道了热切的惋惜，却又添了一句：但愿他此去，只是为了动身去马德里。

===Original===
He shook his head. “I am going to take my passage,” said he, “but not to Madrid. The fates have cut that thread. The President does not want my services, and I can’t blame him, for if our situations were reversed, I should certainly not want his. He has an Indiana friend, who, I am told, wanted to be postmaster at Indianapolis, but as this did not suit the politicians, he was bought off at the exorbitant price of the Spanish mission. But I should have no chance even if he were out of the way. The President does not approve of me. He objects to the cut of my overcoat which is unfortunately an English one. He also objects to the cut of my hair. I am afraid that his wife objects to me because I am so happy as to be thought a friend of yours.”

Madeleine could only acknowledge that Mr. Gore’s case was a bad one. “But after all,” said she, “why should politicians be expected to love you literary gentlemen who write history. Other criminal classes are not expected to love their judges.”

“No, but they have sense enough to fear them,” replied Gore vindictively; “not one politician living has the brains or the art to defend his own cause. The ocean of history is foul with the carcases of such statesmen, dead and forgotten except when some historian fishes one of them up to gibbet it.”
===Chinese===
他摇了摇头。“我是要去订船票，”他说，“可不是去马德里。命运之神把那根线铰断了。总统不想用我，这也怪不得他，因为倘若我们俩处境对调，我断然也不会要他。他有一位印第安纳朋友，听说本想当印第安纳波利斯的邮政局长，只是政客们觉得不合适，便出了驻西班牙公使这个天价，把他买了下来。可就算此人让了路，我也一样没有指望。总统看不中我。他看不惯我这件大衣的式样，可惜它偏偏是英国货。他还看不惯我头发的式样。至于他的夫人也看不惯我，我怕是因为我有这份福气，被人看作您的一位朋友。”

马德琳只得承认，戈尔先生这桩案子委实没治了。“不过说到底，”她说，“凭什么指望政客们爱你们这班写历史的文人先生呢。旁的罪犯阶层，也没有人指望他们爱自己的法官呀。”

“爱是不成，可他们好歹有那份脑子，懂得怕，”戈尔狠狠地答道；“当今活着的政客，没有一个有那副头脑、那门艺术，能替自己的事业辩护。历史的海洋，让这一类政治家的尸首搅得污浊不堪；他们死了，也早被人忘了个干净，除非哪位史家钓起其中一条来，吊上绞架示众。”

===Original===
Mr. Gore was so much out of temper that after this piece of extravagance he was forced to pause a moment to recover himself. Then he went on:﻿—

“You are perfectly right, and so is the President. I have no business to be meddling in politics. It is not my place. The next time you hear of me, I promise it shall not be as an office-seeker.”

Then he rapidly changed the subject, saying that he hoped Mrs. Lee was soon going northward again, and that they might meet at Newport.
===Chinese===
戈尔先生火气太大，这一番高论发过之后，不得不停一停，缓过神来。然后他接着说道：——

“你说得一点不错，总统也没有错。我本来就不该到政治里头搅和。这不是我的位子。下回你再听到我的消息，我担保，绝不会是又一个跑官要官的。”

随即他麻利地把话头岔开，说盼望李夫人不久再往北边去，两人可以在纽波特见上一面。

===Original===
“I don’t know,” replied Madeleine; “the spring is pleasant here, and we shall stay till the warm weather, I think.”

Mr. Gore looked grave. “And your politics!” said he; “are you satisfied with what you have seen?”

“I have got so far as to lose the distinction between right and wrong. Isn’t that the first step in politics?”
===Chinese===
“说不准，”马德琳答道；“这里春天宜人，我想我们会住到天气热起来再说。”

戈尔先生面色一肃。“还有你的政治！”他说；“你看了这许多，可觉得满意么？”

“我已经进步到分不清是非对错了。这难道不是搞政治的头一步么？”

===Original===
Mr. Gore had no mind even for serious jesting. He broke out into a long lecture which sounded like a chapter of some future history: “But Mrs. Lee, is it possible that you don’t see what a wrong path you are on. If you want to know what the world is really doing to any good purpose, pass a winter at Samarkand, at Timbuktu, but not at Washington. Be a bank-clerk, or a journeyman printer, but not a Congressman. Here you will find nothing but wasted effort and clumsy intrigue.”

“Do you think it a pity for me to learn that?” asked Madeleine when his long essay was ended.

“No!” replied Gore, hesitating; “not if you do learn it. But many people never get so far, or only when too late. I shall be glad to hear that you are mistress of it and have given up reforming politics. The Spaniards have a proverb that smells of the stable, but applies to people like you and me: The man who washes his donkey’s head, loses time and soap.”
===Chinese===
戈尔先生连正色说笑的心思都没有了。他滔滔不绝地发了一篇长论，听上去就像某部未来史书里的一章：“可是李夫人，你难道竟看不出，自己走的是一条多么邪的路？你若想知道这个世界究竟在干些什么正经事，就去撒马尔罕过个冬，去廷巴克图过个冬，千万别在华盛顿。去当个银行办事员，做个印刷工人，也别当国会议员。在这里，你只会找到白费的力气和笨拙的阴谋。”

“你觉得我学到这一课，是件憾事么？”等他那篇宏文告终，马德琳问道。

“不是憾事！”戈尔迟疑着答道；“前提是你当真学到了手。可是许多人一辈子走不到这一步，或者走到时已经太迟。哪天听说你把这一课参透了、把改革政治的心思丢开了，我便高兴。西班牙人有句谚语，一股马厩味儿，用在你我这样的人身上却正合适：给驴子洗头的人，白费工夫又白费胰子。”

===Original===
Gore took his leave before Madeleine had time to grasp all the impudence of this last speech. Not until she was fairly in bed that night did it suddenly flash on her mind that Mr. Gore had dared to caricature her as wasting time and soap on Mr. Ratcliffe. At first she was violently angry and then she laughed in spite of herself; there was truth in the portrait. In secret, too, she was the less offended because she half thought that it had depended only on herself to make of Mr. Gore something more than a friend. If she had overheard his parting words to Carrington, she would have had still more reason to think that a little jealousy of Ratcliffe’s success sharpened the barb of Gore’s enmity.

“Take care of Ratcliffe!” was his farewell; “he is a clever dog. He has set his mark on Mrs. Lee. Look out that he doesn’t walk off with her!”

A little startled by this sudden confidence, Carrington could only ask what he could do to prevent it.

“Cats that go ratting, don’t wear gloves,” replied Gore, who always carried a Spanish proverb in his pocket. Carrington, after painful reflection, could only guess that he wanted Ratcliffe’s enemies to show their claws. But how?
===Chinese===
马德琳还没来得及咂摸出这最后一番话里全部的无礼，戈尔便告辞走了。直到当天夜里她躺上了床，才猛然醒过神来：戈尔先生竟敢把她画成那个给拉特克利夫洗驴头、白费工夫又白费胰子的人。起初她火冒三丈，随后又禁不住笑出声来：这幅漫画像画得着实不差。暗地里，她的气也因而消了几分，因为她隐隐觉得，当初只要她自己愿意，本可以把戈尔先生造就成一位不止于朋友的人。倘若她偷听到了他对卡林顿的临别赠言，她就更有理由认为：对拉特克利夫得手的一星醋意，把戈尔那份敌意的倒钩磨得更尖了。

“留神拉特克利夫！”这便是他的临别赠言；“他是条机灵的狗。他已经在李夫人身上下了记号。当心别让他把她整个叼了走！”

卡林顿被这突如其来的交底弄得微微一惊，只能问他：自己能做点什么来拦住这件事呢。

“捉耗子的猫，可不戴手套，”戈尔答道——他口袋里永远揣着一句西班牙谚语。卡林顿苦苦思量一番之后，只能猜出一点：他是要拉特克利夫的敌人们亮出爪子来。可是，怎么个亮法呢？

===Original===
Mrs. Lee not long afterwards spoke to Ratcliffe of her regret at Gore’s disappointment and hinted at his disgust. Ratcliffe replied that he had done what he could for Gore, and had introduced him to the President, who, after seeing him, had sworn his usual granitic oath that he would sooner send his nigger farmhand Jake to Spain than that man-milliner. “You know how I stand;” added Ratcliffe; “what more could I do?” And Mrs. Lee’s implied reproach was silenced.

If Gore was little pleased with Ratcliffe’s conduct, poor Schneidekoupon was still less so. He turned up again at Washington not long after the Inauguration and had a private interview with the Secretary of the Treasury. What passed at it was known only to themselves, but, whatever it was, Schneidekoupon’s temper was none the better for it. From his conversations with Sybil, it seemed that there was some question about appointments in which his protectionist friends were interested, and he talked very openly about Ratcliffe’s want of good faith, and how he had promised everything to everybody and had failed to keep a single pledge; if Schneidekoupon’s advice had been taken, this wouldn’t have happened. Mrs. Lee told Ratcliffe that Schneidekoupon seemed out of temper, and asked the reason. He only laughed and evaded the question, remarking that cattle of this kind were always complaining unless they were allowed to run the whole government; Schneidekoupon had nothing to grumble about; no one had ever made any promises to him. But nevertheless Schneidekoupon confided to Sybil his antipathy to Ratcliffe and solemnly begged her not to let Mrs. Lee fall into his hands, to which Sybil answered tartly that she only wished Mr. Schneidekoupon would tell her how to help it.
===Chinese===
不久之后，李夫人便对拉特克利夫提起戈尔落空的事，深表惋惜，又隐隐点到他的愤懑。拉特克利夫答道，他为戈尔已经尽了力，还引他去见了总统；总统见过他之后，赌上了他那句惯常的花岗岩咒，说宁肯打发他那个黑鬼农场雇工杰克去西班牙，也绝不打发那个女帽匠式的男人。“你知道我的处境；”拉特克利夫补了一句；“我还能怎样？”李夫人这一番含而不露的责备，就这样被堵了回去。

如果说戈尔对拉特克利夫的行事不甚满意，可怜的施奈德库彭就更是不满了。就职典礼后不久，他又在华盛顿露了面，同财政部长进行了一次密谈。谈了些什么，只有他们自己知道；只是不管谈了什么，施奈德库彭的脾气都没见半点好转。从他与西比尔的交谈里听来，似乎是官位任命上有一桩纠葛，他那些保护主义的朋友正牵涉其中；他毫不遮掩地大谈拉特克利夫如何不讲信义，如何对人人许下一切、却一条诺言也不曾兑现；又说当初若听了他施奈德库彭的主意，断不至如此。李夫人对拉特克利夫说，施奈德库彭像是一肚子不痛快，问是何故。他只是笑，顾左右而言他，说这一路的牛马，若不让他们把整个政府都包办下来，就永远有得抱怨；施奈德库彭没什么可嘟囔的；从没有人对他许过什么愿。话虽如此，施奈德库彭还是向西比尔吐露了他对拉特克利夫的反感，还一本正经地恳求她，千万别叫李夫人落进他的手心；西比尔尖刻地回敬：她只盼施奈德库彭先生肯教教她，这事到底怎么才拦得住。

===Original===
The reformer French had also been one of Ratcliffe’s backers in the fight over the Treasury. He remained in Washington a few days after the Inauguration, and then disappeared, leaving cards with P.P.C. in the corner, at Mrs. Lee’s door. Rumour said that he too was disappointed, but he kept his own counsel, and, if he really wanted the mission to Belgium, he contented himself with waiting for it. A respectable stagecoach proprietor from Oregon got the place.

As for Jacobi, who was not disappointed, and who had nothing to ask for, he was bitterest of all. He formally offered his congratulations to Ratcliffe on his appointment. This little scene occurred in Mrs. Lee’s parlour. The old Baron, with his most suave manner, and his most Voltairean leer, said that in all his experience, and he had seen a great many court intrigues, he had never seen anything better managed than that about the Treasury. Ratcliffe was furiously angry, and told the Baron outright that foreign ministers who insulted the governments to which they were accredited ran a risk of being sent home.
===Chinese===
改革家弗伦奇，在争夺财政部的那场交锋里，原本也是拉特克利夫的后盾之一。就职典礼后，他在华盛顿又盘桓了几天，随后便消失了，只在李夫人门前留下名片，一角印着 P.P.C.（告辞之意）。传言说他也落了空，但他守口如瓶；倘若他当真想要驻比利时公使那个位子，他也只安于静候。结果那顶位子，落到俄勒冈一位体面的驿车车主手里。

至于雅各比，他既没有落空之憾，也无事所求，却数他说话最刻毒。他为拉特克利夫的任命，郑重其事地道了贺。这出小小的戏，就发生在李夫人的客厅里。老男爵拿出他最温文的手腕、最伏尔泰式的斜睨，说道：以他一生的阅历——他见过的宫廷阴谋着实不少——他还从没见过哪一桩，办得比财政部这一桩更漂亮。拉特克利夫勃然大怒，直截了当地警告男爵：外交使节若出言侮辱驻在国政府，可是冒着被遣送回国的风险的。

===Original===
“*Ce serait toujours un pis aller*,” said Jacobi, seating himself with calmness in Ratcliffe’s favourite chair by Mrs. Lee’s side.

Madeleine, alarmed as she was, could not help interposing, and hastily asked whether that remark was translatable.

“Ah!” said the Baron; “I can do nothing with your language. You would only say that it was a choice of evils, to go, or to stay.”

“We might translate it by saying: ‘One may go farther and fare worse,’ ” rejoined Madeleine; and so the storm blew over for the time, and Ratcliffe sulkily let the subject drop. Nevertheless the two men never met in Mrs. Lee’s parlour without her dreading a personal altercation. Little by little, what with Jacobi’s sarcasms and Ratcliffe’s roughness, they nearly ceased to speak, and glared at each other like quarrelsome dogs. Madeleine was driven to all kinds of expedients to keep the peace, yet at the same time she could not but be greatly amused by their behaviour, and as their hatred of each other only stimulated their devotion to her, she was content to hold an even balance between them.
===Chinese===
“*Ce serait toujours un pis aller*，”雅各比说罢，从容不迫地在拉特克利夫最中意的那把椅子——李夫人身旁的椅子——上坐了下来。

马德琳尽管心惊，也忍不住出来打圆场，急急地问：那句话可翻译得过来么。

“啊！”男爵说；“我对贵国的语言无能为力。你们至多会说：无非两害相权，走，还是留。”

“我们倒可以这样翻译：‘只怕走得越远，反倒越糟，’”马德琳应道；这一场风波总算暂时平息，拉特克利夫绷着脸，把话头撂下了。然而此后这两人在李夫人客厅里狭路相逢，没有一回不叫她提心吊胆，生怕闹出一场失体面的争执。渐渐地，一个凭着雅各比的讥诮，一个凭着拉特克利夫的粗鲁，两人几乎不再交谈，只是像一对好斗的狗似的，你瞪我、我瞪你。马德琳被逼得使出百般招数来维护太平；可与此同时，他们的做派又实在叫她暗暗好笑；而两人彼此的仇恨既然只会给他们对她的殷勤添柴加火，她也就乐得在他们之间持平端着这碗水。

===Original===
Nor were these all the awkward consequences of Ratcliffe’s attentions. Now that he was distinctly recognized as an intimate friend of Mrs. Lee’s, and possibly her future husband, no one ventured any longer to attack him in her presence, but nevertheless she was conscious in a thousand ways that the atmosphere became more and more dense under the shadow of the Secretary of the Treasury. In spite of herself she sometimes felt uneasy, as though there were conspiracy in the air. One March afternoon she was sitting by her fire, with an English Review in her hand, trying to read the last Symposium on the sympathies of Eternal Punishment, when her servant brought in a card, and Mrs. Lee had barely time to read the name of Mrs. Samuel Baker when that lady followed the servant into the room, forcing the countersign in so effective style that for once Madeleine was fairly disconcerted. Her manner when thus intruded upon, was cool, but in this case, on Carrington’s account, she tried to smile courteously and asked her visitor to sit down, which Mrs. Baker was doing without an invitation, very soon putting her hostess entirely at her ease. She was, when seen without her veil, a showy woman verging on forty, decidedly large, tall, overdressed even in mourning, and with a complexion rather fresher than nature had made it. There was a geniality in her address, savouring of easy Washington ways, a fruitiness of smile, and a rich southern accent, that explained on the spot her success in the lobby. She looked about her with fine self-possession, and approved Mrs. Lee’s surroundings with a cordiality so different from the northern stinginess of praise, that Madeleine was rather pleased than offended. Yet when her eye rested on the Corot, Madeleine’s only pride, she was evidently perplexed, and resorted to eyeglasses, in order, as it seemed, to gain time for reflection. But she was not to be disconcerted even by Corot’s masterpiece:
===Chinese===
拉特克利夫这份殷勤带来的别扭后患，还不止这些。如今他已被公认为李夫人的密友，说不定还是她未来的丈夫，再没有人敢当着她的面攻击他了；可与此同时，她也千百样地觉出，在财政部长的阴影底下，空气正变得一日浓似一日。她有时不由自主地感到不安，仿佛空气里浮动着阴谋。三月里的一个下午，她正坐在炉火旁，手里捧着一份英国的评论杂志，努力去读最新一期讨论“永恒惩罚之同情”的笔谈专栏，仆人送进来一张名片；李夫人刚来得及看清塞缪尔·贝克夫人的名字，那位太太已经跟着仆人进了屋，强闯口令的架势如此利落有效，马德琳破天荒头一遭乱了方寸。逢到这样不速闯入，她素来是冷淡的；但这一回，看在卡林顿的分上，她勉强堆出和气的笑，请来客落座——其实贝克夫人不等相让已经坐下了，而且不多时便让女主人彻底自在起来。她揭下面纱一看，是个艳光四射的女人，年将四十，块头着实不小，身量也高，即便一身丧服也穿得过分讲究，脸上的肤色也比天然生成的更鲜润几分。她的言谈里透着一股亲热劲儿，带着华盛顿那种随和做派的气味；笑容丰腴饱满，一口浓郁的南方口音——她在院外活动这一行里的成功，当场便得到了解释。她四下打量，仪态从容之至，对李夫人的居所赞不绝口，那份热络，同北方人夸起人来的一毛不拔相比，实在判然有别，马德琳受用之余，倒也不以为忤。然而当她的目光落到那幅柯罗——马德琳唯一的骄傲——上时，她分明犯了难，赶紧掏出夹鼻眼镜，似乎好争取一点斟酌的工夫。可即便是柯罗的杰作，也休想让她乱了阵脚：

===Original===
“How pretty! Japanese, isn’t it? Seaweeds seen through a fog. I went to an auction yesterday, and do you know I bought a teapot with a picture just like that.”

Madeleine inquired with extreme interest about the auction, but after learning all that Mrs. Baker had to tell, she was on the point of being reduced to silence, when she bethought herself to mention Carrington. Mrs. Baker brightened up at once, if she could be said to brighten where there was no sign of dimness:

“Dear Mr. Carrington! Isn’t he sweet? I think he’s a delicious man. I don’t know what I should do without him. Since poor Mr. Baker left me, we have been together all the time. You know my poor husband left directions that all his papers should be burned, and though I would not say so unless you were such a friend of Mr. Carrington’s, I reckon it’s just as well for some people that he did. I never could tell you what quantities of papers Mr. Carrington and I have put in the fire; and we read them all too.”
===Chinese===
“真好看！日本货，是不是？隔着雾看见的海草。昨天我去了趟拍卖行，你猜怎么着，我买回来一把茶壶，上面的画就跟这幅一模一样。”

马德琳怀着极大的兴趣，打听起那场拍卖会；可是把贝克夫人肚里的货色听了个够之后，她几乎要哑口无言了，幸而想起该提一提卡林顿。贝克夫人顿时亮堂起来——如果在她那张本无半点暗色的脸上，还谈得上更亮一点的话：

“亲爱的卡林顿先生！他多讨人喜欢呀！我看他这人真叫人疼。没有他，我真不知道可怎么办。自从可怜的贝克先生丢下我走了，我们就一直在一起。你知道，我可怜的丈夫留下过话，所有的文件一律烧掉；这话要不是看你同卡林顿先生这么要好，我是不肯说的——我琢磨着，他这么吩咐，对某些人来说倒是万幸。卡林顿先生和我往火里塞了多少文件，我一辈子也说不完；而且我们还是一份一份都读过来的。”

===Original===
Madeleine asked whether this was not dull work.

“Oh, dear, no! You see I know all about it, and told Mr. Carrington the story of every paper as we went on. It was quite amusing, I assure you.”

Mrs. Lee then boldly said she had got from Mr. Carrington an idea that Mrs. Baker was a very skilful diplomatist.
===Chinese===
马德琳问，这差事岂不闷得慌。

“哎哟，亲爱的，才不闷呢！你不知道，这里头的事我全门儿清，我们一边烧，我一边把每份文件的来历讲给卡林顿先生听。我向你担保，有趣得很哩。”

李夫人于是放大了胆子说，她从卡林顿先生那里得了一个印象：贝克夫人是位手段十分了得的外交家。

===Original===
“Diplomatist!” echoed the widow with her genial laugh; “Well! it was as much that as anything, but there’s not many diplomatists’ wives in this city ever did as much work as I used to do. Why, I knew half the members of Congress intimately, and all of them by sight. I knew where they came from and what they liked best. I could get round the greater part of them, sooner or later.”

Mrs. Lee asked what she did with all this knowledge. Mrs. Baker shook her pink-and-white countenance, and almost paralysed her opposite neighbour by a sort of Grande Duchesse wink:

“Oh, my dear! you are new here. If you had seen Washington in war-times and for a few years afterwards, you wouldn’t ask that. We had more congressional business than all the other agents put together. Everyone came to us then, to get his bill through, or his appropriation watched. We were hard at work all the time. You see, one can’t keep the run of three hundred men without some trouble. My husband used to make lists of them in books with a history of each man and all he could learn about him, but I carried it all in my head.”
===Chinese===
“外交家！”寡妇应声笑道，笑声一团和气；“嘿！说是这个也未尝不可，不过这座城里，外交官的太太们赶得上我当年那副辛劳的，可没有几个。这么跟你说吧，国会议员，一半跟我过从甚密，其余的也个个混了个脸熟。他们打哪儿来、最爱哪一口，我全知道。他们里头的一多半，我迟早总有法子绕得过去。”

李夫人问，这一肚子学问，她都派了什么用场。贝克夫人摇晃着那张粉白娇嫩的脸盘，冲着对面的人丢了个“大公夫人”式的眼色，险些把她当场镇瘫：

“哎哟，亲爱的！你到这儿还没几天。你要是见过打仗那些年的华盛顿，还有战后那么几年的华盛顿，就不会问这个了。国会里的买卖，经我们手的，比其余各家经纪人加在一起还多。那时候人人来找我们，不是要推他的议案过关，就是要替他的拨款盯梢。我们成天忙得脚不沾地。你想，三百号人，要把每个人的来龙去脉都记在心里，岂是轻省活计。我丈夫从前把他们开成名单，记在本子上，每人一段履历，凡他打听得来的都记上；我呢，全装在脑子里。”

===Original===
“Do you mean that you could get them all to vote as you pleased?” asked Madeleine.

“Well! we got our bills through,” replied Mrs. Baker.

“But how did you do it? did they take bribes?”

“Some of them did. Some of them liked suppers and cards and theatres and all sorts of things. Some of them could be led, and some had to be driven like Paddy’s pig who thought he was going the other way. Some of them had wives who could talk to them, and some﻿—hadn’t,” said Mrs. Baker, with a queer intonation in her abrupt ending.
===Chinese===
“你的意思是，他们的票，你想叫怎么投就怎么投？”马德琳问。

“嘿！反正我们的议案都过了关，”贝克夫人答道。

“可你们是怎么弄的？他们收受贿赂么？”

“有的收。有的爱吃宵夜、爱打牌、爱上戏园子，各有所好。有的牵一牵就跟着走，有的却得赶——就像帕迪那头猪，自以为朝另一头走，却被人赶着去了。有的家里有个能说话的太太，有的么——没有，”贝克夫人说道，收尾收得突兀，语调古怪。

===Original===
“But surely,” said Mrs. Lee, “many of them must have been above﻿—I mean, they must have had nothing to get hold of, so that you could manage them.”

Mrs. Baker laughed cheerfully and remarked that they were very much of a muchness.

“But I can’t understand how you did it,” urged Madeleine; “now, how would you have gone to work to get a respectable senator’s vote﻿—a man like Mr. Ratcliffe, for instance?”

“Ratcliffe!” repeated Mrs. Baker with a slight elevation of voice that gave way to a patronising laugh. “Oh, my dear! don’t mention names. I should get into trouble. Senator Ratcliffe was a good friend of my husband’s. I guess Mr. Carrington could have told you that. But you see, what we generally wanted was all right enough. We had to know where our bills were, and jog people’s elbows to get them reported in time. Sometimes we had to convince them that our bill was a proper one, and they ought to vote for it. Only now and then, when there was a great deal of money and the vote was close, we had to find out what votes were worth. It was mostly dining and talking, calling them out into the lobby or asking them to supper. I wish I could tell you things I have seen, but I don’t dare. It wouldn’t be safe. I’ve told you already more than I ever said to anyone else; but then you are so intimate with Mr. Carrington, that I always think of you as an old friend.”
===Chinese===
“可是，”李夫人说，“他们中间总该有许多人操守是干净的——我是说，总该有些人身上没有一点可抓的把柄，叫你们无从摆布吧。”

贝克夫人欢欢喜喜地笑了，说他们都是半斤八两，一路货色。

“可我还是闹不明白你们是怎么弄的，”马德琳追问；“这么说吧，比方像拉特克利夫先生这样一位体面的参议员，你想弄到他那一票，会怎么下手呢？”

“拉特克利夫！”贝克夫人把名字重了一遍，嗓门微微一扬，随即化成一阵居高临下的轻笑。“哎哟，亲爱的！别提名道姓的。我要惹麻烦的。拉特克利夫参议员是我丈夫的一位好朋友。这事卡林顿先生本来就能告诉你。不过你瞧，我们平素要办的事，本来就都堂堂正正。我们得知道我们的议案到了哪一步，得碰碰人家的胳膊肘，催他们及时把它报告出来。有时候，我们得叫人相信我们的议案是正当的，他们理应投一票。只是偶尔，牵涉的银钱数目大了，票数又咬得紧，我们才需要摸清一张票值多少价钱。多半不过是请吃饭、套交情，把他们叫到走廊里说几句，或是请来吃顿夜宵。我多想把我见过的事讲给你听，可我不敢。不安全。我对你说的，已经比我对谁说过的都多了；可谁叫你同卡林顿先生那么亲厚呢，我一向就把你当老朋友看待。”

===Original===
Thus Mrs. Baker rippled on, while Mrs. Lee listened with more and more doubt and disgust. The woman was showy, handsome in a coarse style, and perfectly presentable. Mrs. Lee had seen Duchesses as vulgar. She knew more about the practical working of government than Mrs. Lee could ever expect or hope to know. Why then draw back from this interesting lobbyist with such babyish repulsion?

When, after a long, and, as she declared, a most charming call, Mrs. Baker wended her way elsewhere and Madeleine had given the strictest order that she should never be admitted again, Carrington entered, and Madeleine showed him Mrs. Baker’s card and gave a lively account of the interview.

“What shall I do with the woman?” she asked; “must I return her card?” But Carrington declined to offer advice on this interesting point. “And she says that Mr. Ratcliffe was a friend of her husband’s and that you could tell me about that.”
===Chinese===
贝克夫人就这样滔滔汩汩地讲下去，李夫人越听越疑，越听越厌。这女人艳丽招摇，有一种粗俗一路的美，而且拿得出去、见得了人。比她更俗的公爵夫人，李夫人也见识过。她对政府实际运作的了解，远非李夫人所能指望、所能企及。那么，何必带着这般孩子气的嫌恶，从这位有意思的院外说客跟前缩回来呢？

这场拜访拖了很久，而且照贝克夫人自己的说法，愉快之至；待她移驾别处之后，马德琳立刻下了最严格的命令：此人永不放行。卡林顿随即进来，马德琳把贝克夫人的名片给他看，又把会面的经过活灵活现学了一遍。

“你说我拿这女人怎么办？”她问；“总得回拜吧，还她这张名片？”可在这个有趣的问题上，卡林顿不肯赐教。“她还说什么拉特克利夫先生是她丈夫的朋友，说这件事你可以讲给我听。”

===Original===
“Did she say so?” remarked Carrington vaguely.

“Yes! and that she knew everyone’s weak points and could get all their votes.”

Carrington expressed no surprise, and so evidently preferred to change the subject, that Mrs. Lee desisted and said no more.
===Chinese===
“她是这么说的？”卡林顿含含糊糊地应了一声。

“是啊！还说她知道每个人的软处，谁的票她都能弄到手。”

卡林顿毫无讶色，而且那份想把话头岔开的心思明明白白写在脸上，李夫人只得作罢，不再提了。

===Original===
But she determined to try the same experiment on Mr. Ratcliffe, and chose the very next chance that offered. In her most indifferent manner she remarked that Mrs. Sam Baker had called upon her and had initiated her into the mysteries of the lobby till she had become quite ambitious to start on that career.

“She said you were a friend of her husband’s,” added Madeleine softly.

Ratcliffe’s face betrayed no sign.

“If you believe what those people tell you,” said he drily, “you will be wiser than the Queen of Sheba.”
===Chinese===
但她打定主意，要在拉特克利夫先生身上把这同一个试验再做一遍，并且就挑了下一个现成的机会。她拿出最漫不经心的神气，说萨姆·贝克夫人来拜望过她，把她引进了院外活动的种种奥妙，说得她自己都跃跃欲试，想改行入这一道了。

“她说你是她丈夫的朋友，”马德琳轻轻补了一句。

拉特克利夫的脸上看不出一丝动静。

“你要是信得过那种人嘴里的货，”他干巴巴地说，“你可就比示巴女王还要高明了。”

## IX / 九

===Original===
Whenever a man reaches the top of the political ladder, his enemies unite to pull him down. His friends become critical and exacting. Among the many dangers of this sort which now threatened Ratcliffe, there was one that, had he known it, might have made him more uneasy than any of those which were the work of senators and congressmen. Carrington entered into an alliance, offensive and defensive, with Sybil. It came about in this wise. Sybil was fond of riding and occasionally, when Carrington could spare the time, he went as her guide and protector in these country excursions; for every Virginian, however out at elbows, has a horse, as he has shoes or a shirt. In a thoughtless moment Carrington had been drawn into a promise that he would take Sybil to Arlington. The promise was one that he did not hurry to keep, for there were reasons which made a visit to Arlington anything but a pleasure to him; but Sybil would listen to no excuses, and so it came about that, one lovely March morning, when the shrubs and the trees in the square before the house were just beginning, under the warmer sun, to show signs of their coming wantonness, Sybil stood at the open window waiting for him, while her new Kentucky horse before the door showed what he thought of the delay by curving his neck, tossing his head, and pawing the pavement. Carrington was late and kept her waiting so long, that the mignonette and geraniums, which adorned the window, suffered for his slowness, and the curtain tassels showed signs of wilful damage. Nevertheless he arrived at length, and they set out together, choosing the streets least enlivened by horsecars and provision-carts, until they had crept through the great metropolis of Georgetown and come upon the bridge which crosses the noble river just where its bold banks open out to clasp the city of Washington in their easy embrace. Then reaching the Virginia side they cantered gaily up the laurel-margined road, with glimpses of woody defiles, each carrying its trickling stream and rich in promise of summer flowers, while from point to point they caught glorious glimpses of the distant city and river. They passed the small military station on the heights, still dignified by the name of fort, though Sybil silently wondered how a fort was possible without fortifications, and complained that there was nothing more warlike than a “nursery of telegraph poles.” The day was blue and gold; everything smiled and sparkled in the crisp freshness of the morning. Sybil was in bounding spirits and not at all pleased to find that her companion became moody and abstracted as they went on. “Poor Mr. Carrington!” thought she to herself, “he is so nice; but when he puts on that solemn air, one might as well go to sleep. I am quite certain no nice woman will ever marry him if he looks like that;” and her practical mind ran off among all the girls of her acquaintance, in search of one who would put up with Carrington’s melancholy face. She knew his devotion to her sister, but had long ago rejected this as a hopeless chance. There was a simplicity about Sybil’s way of dealing with life, which had its own charm. She never troubled herself about the impossible or the unthinkable. She had feelings, and was rather quick in her sympathies and sorrows, but she was equally quick in getting over them, and she expected other people to do likewise. Madeleine dissected her own feelings and was always wondering whether they were real or not; she had a habit of taking off her mental clothing, as she might take off a dress, and looking at it as though it belonged to someone else, and as though sensations were manufactured like clothes. This seems to be one of the easier ways of deadening sorrow, as though the mind could teach itself to lop off its feelers. Sybil particularly disliked this self-inspection. In the first place she did not understand it, and in the second her mind was all feelers, and amputation was death. She could no more analyse a feeling than doubt its existence, both which were habits of her sister.

===Chinese===
一个人但凡爬到了政治梯子的顶端，他的敌人便会合力把他往下拽，他的朋友也会变得挑剔苛求。如今威胁着拉特克利夫的这许多同类危险里，有一桩，他若得知，只怕会比参议员们、众议员们炮制出来的任何一桩都更叫他不安——卡林顿同西比尔结成了攻守同盟。事情的缘起是这样的。西比尔爱骑马，卡林顿偶尔抽得出空，便充任她乡间出游的向导兼保护人；因为每个弗吉尼亚人，哪怕穷得袖口磨穿，也有一匹马，正如他有鞋穿、有衬衫穿。卡林顿一时没留神，被绕了进去，许下诺言要带西比尔去阿灵顿。这个诺言他不急着兑现，因为种种缘故，去阿灵顿于他绝非一件乐事；可西比尔什么借口都不肯听，于是便有了这么一个明媚的三月早晨：宅前广场上的灌木和树木，正在转暖的日头底下初初显出即将放肆的苗头，西比尔站在敞开的窗前等他，门前她那匹新到的肯塔基马，弯着脖颈，扬着脑袋，蹄子刨着路面，用它自己的法子表示它对这番耽搁的意见。卡林顿迟到了，叫她等了那么久，连装点窗子的木樨草和天竺葵都为他的怠慢遭了殃，窗帘穗子也显出被蓄意作践的痕迹。不管怎样，他总算到了，两人一同出发，专拣马拉街车和运货菜车最少、最不热闹的街走，一路蹭过乔治敦这座堂堂大都会，来到那座桥前——大桥横跨那条壮丽的大河，恰在两岸陡然张开、把华盛顿城松松拥入怀中的地方。到了弗吉尼亚一侧，他们欢快地策马小跑，驰上一条月桂镶边的路，林木蓊郁的隘谷不时在眼前闪过，每一条都载着一脉细流，蕴满夏日繁花的许诺；一路行去，远处的城市与河流，又不时在眼前放出壮丽的容光。他们经过高地上那座小小的军站——至今还顶着“要塞”的尊号，尽管西比尔暗自纳闷，一座没有工事的要塞如何能够成立，还抱怨说这里比“电报线杆苗圃”更像样的武备一样也没有。这一日天色蓝金交映，万物在清晨爽利的鲜洁里含笑闪光。西比尔兴致飞扬，偏发觉同伴越走越郁郁出神，心里老大不痛快。“可怜的卡林顿先生！”她暗自想道，“他这个人多好呀；可他一端起那副正经面孔，还不如睡觉去呢。我敢保真，他若总摆着那副长相，哪个好女人也不肯嫁他；”于是她那讲求实际的头脑，把认得的姑娘挨个兜了一遍，要找出一个肯容忍卡林顿这张愁容的。她知道他对她姐姐的一片痴心，但早就把这条线索断作没指望，撂开了。西比尔应付生活的路数里自有一种质朴，这质朴别有动人之处。凡是不可能的事、想不通的事，她从不自寻烦恼。她有感情，同情与哀愁来得还挺快，可过去得也一样快，并且她指望旁人也这样。马德琳却惯于解剖自己的感情，总在琢磨它们是真是假；她有个习惯，会把心事像脱衣裳似的脱下来，拿在眼前打量，仿佛那是别人的东西，又仿佛种种感受原是像衣裳一样缝制出来的。这似乎是麻掉悲哀的比较省力的路子之一，仿佛头脑能教会自己，把触须一根根削斫了去。西比尔顶讨厌这种自我省视。一来她不懂；二来她的头脑通体是触须，一斫便是死。剖析一种感情，她不会；怀疑一种感情的存在，她也不会——而这两样，恰是她姐姐的惯技。

===Original===
How was Sybil to know what was passing in Carrington’s mind? He was thinking of nothing in which she supposed herself interested. He was troubled with memories of civil war and of associations still earlier, belonging to an age already vanishing or vanished; but what could she know about civil war who had been almost an infant at the time? At this moment, she happened to be interested in the battle of Waterloo, for she was reading *Vanity Fair*, and had cried as she ought for poor little Emmy, when her husband, George Osborne, lay dead on the field there, with a bullet through his heart. But how was she to know that here, only a few rods before her, lay scores and hundreds of George Osbornes, or his betters, and in their graves the love and hope of many Emmys, not creatures of the imagination, but flesh and blood, like herself? To her, there was no more in those associations which made Carrington groan in the silence of his thoughts, than if he had been old Kaspar, and she the little Wilhelmine. What was a skull more or less to her? What concern had she in the famous victory?

===Chinese===
西比尔又如何知道卡林顿心里翻腾的是什么呢？他想的事，没有一件是她自以为会感兴趣的。他被内战的记忆纠缠着，还有更早的旧忆，属于一个正在消逝或已然消逝的时代；可内战那阵子她差不多还在襁褓之中，她能知道内战什么呢？偏巧这会儿，她正对滑铁卢战役大感兴趣，因为她正在读《名利场》，还该哭的地方哭了，为可怜的小爱米哭——她的丈夫乔治·奥斯本横尸在那片战场上，一颗子弹穿过了他的心。可她哪里知道，就在眼前，离她不过几步远的地方，躺着成十上百的乔治·奥斯本，还有比他更出色的人，他们的坟墓里埋着许多个爱米的爱情与指望——不是凭空想象的人物，而是同她一样的血肉之躯？那些旧事引得卡林顿在无声的思绪里呻吟，而她读出的东西，并不比他若是个老卡斯帕、她若是个小威廉明时更多。多一块骷髅少一块骷髅，与她何干？那场赫赫有名的胜仗，又与她什么相干？

===Original===
Yet even Sybil was startled as she rode through the gate and found herself suddenly met by the long white ranks of headstones, stretching up and down the hillsides by thousands, in order of battle; as though Cadmus had reversed his myth, and had sown living men, to come up dragons’ teeth. She drew in her horse with a shiver and a sudden impulse to cry. Here was something new to her. This was war﻿—wounds, disease, death. She dropped her voice and with a look almost as serious as Carrington’s, asked what all these graves meant. When Carrington told her, she began for the first time to catch some dim notion why his face was not quite as gay as her own. Even now this idea was not very precise, for he said little about himself, but at least she grappled with the fact that he had actually, year after year, carried arms against these men who lay at her feet and who had given their lives for her cause. It suddenly occurred to her as a new thought that perhaps he himself might have killed one of them with his own hand. There was a strange shock in this idea. She felt that Carrington was further from her. He gained dignity in his rebel isolation. She wanted to ask him how he could have been a traitor, and she did not dare. Carrington a traitor! Carrington killing her friends! The idea was too large to grasp. She fell back on the simpler task of wondering how he had looked in his rebel uniform.

===Chinese===
然而就连西比尔，骑马进了大门，迎面撞见那一排排长长的白色墓碑——成千上万，顺着山坡上下列成战阵——也不由得吃了一惊；仿佛卡德摩斯把他的神话倒了个个儿，种下去的是活人，冒出来的是龙牙。她打了个寒噤，心头猛地涌上一阵要哭的冲动，勒住了马。这于她是桩新鲜事。这就是战争——创伤、疾病、死亡。她放低了声音，带着一副几乎同卡林顿一样郑重的脸色，问这一座座坟都是什么来历。卡林顿讲给她听之后，她头一回隐约摸着一点影子：为什么他的脸不似她自己的那样欢快。就是此刻，这念头也还不甚分明，因为关于他自己，他说得很少；但至少她抓住了这样一个事实：年复一年，他当真扛着枪，同这些躺在她脚下、为她那份事业舍了性命的人作对。一个新念头冷不丁冒出来：说不定其中哪一个，就是他亲手结果的呢。这念头里有种异样的震骇。她觉得卡林顿离她更远了。他在叛逆者的孤绝里平添了尊严。她想问问他是怎么能做叛徒的，又不敢。卡林顿，一个叛徒！卡林顿，杀她的朋友们！这念头太大，抓不过来。她退而去做一件简单些的事：琢磨他穿上叛军制服，是个什么模样。

===Original===
They rode slowly round to the door of the house and dismounted, after he had with some difficulty found a man to hold their horses. From the heavy brick porch they looked across the superb river to the raw and incoherent ugliness of the city, idealised into dreamy beauty by the atmosphere, and the soft background of purple hills behind. Opposite them, with its crude “thus saith the law” stamped on white dome and fortress-like walls, rose the Capitol.

===Chinese===
两人缓缓绕到宅子门前下马——他费了些周折才寻着一个人替他们看马。站在厚重的砖砌门廊上，他们隔河眺望：那座城原本粗陋散乱、丑得没了章法，却被这里的空气，连同背后紫色山峦的柔和衬底，点化成一种朦胧的美。他们的正对面，国会大厦耸立着，白色穹顶和堡垒般的墙壁上，烙着它那句生硬的“律法如此说”。

===Original===
Carrington stood with her a short time while they looked at the view; then said he would rather not go into the house himself, and sat down on the steps while she strolled alone through the rooms. These were bare and gaunt, so that she, with her feminine sense of fitness, of course considered what she would do to make them habitable. She had a neat fancy for furniture, and distributed her tones and half tones and bits of colour freely about the walls and ceilings, with a high-backed chair here, a spindle-legged sofa there, and a claw-footed table in the centre, until her eye was caught by a very dirty deal desk, on which stood an open book, with an inkstand and some pens. On the leaf she read the last entry: “Eli M. Grow and lady, Thermopyle Centre.” Not even the graves outside had brought the horrors of war so near.

===Chinese===
卡林顿陪她站了一小会儿，一同领略眼前这番景致，然后说，他自己宁可不进屋里去，便在台阶上坐下，由她一个人在各间屋子里信步。屋子空空落落、瘦硬嶙峋，于是她凭着女人对合宜与否的直觉，自然盘算起要怎样收拾才住得进人。她于家具有一手雅致的设想，把她的调子、半调子和点点色彩，慷慨地铺陈到四壁和天花板上——这里一把高背椅，那里一张细腿沙发，正中一张爪脚桌子——直到她的目光被一张脏极了的松木书桌勾住：桌上摊着一本翻开的书，旁边一座墨水台、几支笔。她在摊开的那页上读到末一条题名：“伊莱·M. 格罗携太太，瑟莫派尔中心”。连门外的坟场，都不曾把战争的惨状带得这般近。

===Original===
What a scourge it was! This respectable family turned out of such a lovely house, and all the pretty old furniture swept away before a horde of coarse invaders “with ladies.” Did the hosts of Attila write their names on visiting books in the temple of Vesta and the house of Sallust? What a new terror they would have added to the name of the scourge of God! Sybil returned to the portico and sat down by Carrington on the steps.

“How awfully sad it is!” said she; “I suppose the house was prettily furnished when the Lees lived here? Did you ever see it then?”

===Chinese===
这真是天降的一条鞭子！这样一户体面人家，被从这样一座可爱的宅子里赶了出去，全部雅致的旧家具，也在这帮“携眷”而来的粗野入侵者面前扫地以尽。阿提拉的大军，可曾在维斯塔神庙和萨卢斯特宅的留名簿上题名？果真题了，“上帝之鞭”这个名号，又该平添多少新的恐怖！西比尔回到门廊，挨着卡林顿在台阶上坐下。

“多惨啊！”她说；“李家住在这儿的时候，屋里想必布置得很漂亮吧？那时候你见过吗？”

===Original===
Sybil was not very profound, but she had sympathy, and at this moment Carrington felt sorely in need of comfort. He wanted someone to share his feelings, and he turned towards her hungry for companionship.

“The Lees were old family friends of mine,” said he. “I used to stay here when I was a boy, even as late as the spring of 1861. The last time I sat here, it was with them. We were wild about disunion and talked of nothing else. I have been trying to recall what was said then. We never thought there would be war, and as for coercion, it was nonsense. Coercion, indeed! The idea was ridiculous. I thought so, too, though I was a Union man and did not want the State to go out. But though I felt sure that Virginia must suffer, I never thought we could be beaten. Yet now I am sitting here a pardoned rebel, and the poor Lees are driven away and their place is a graveyard.”

===Chinese===
西比尔算不上多么深刻，但她有同情心，而此刻卡林顿正苦于需要安慰。他盼着有人分担他的心绪，便转向她，如饥似渴地要个伴儿。

“李家同我家是多年的老世交，”他说。“我小时候常来这里小住，甚至晚到1861年春天还来过。我最后一次坐在这里，就是同他们一起。那时我们满脑子热昏的都是脱离联邦，别的一概不谈。我一直想回忆起当年都说了些什么。我们从没想到真会打起来；至于武力强留，那是昏话。武力强留！亏他们想得出。这念头可笑，我也这么看，虽然我拥护联邦，不愿本州退出。可我也明知弗吉尼亚必得吃苦头，却从没想过我们会被打败。然而如今，我这个得了赦免的叛军坐在这里，可怜的李家人被赶走了，他们的家园成了坟场。”

===Original===
Sybil became at once absorbed in the Lees and asked many questions, all which Carrington gladly answered. He told her how he had admired and followed General Lee through the war. “We thought he was to be our Washington, you know; and perhaps he had some such idea himself;” and then, when Sybil wanted to hear about the battles and the fighting, he drew a rough map on the gravel path to show her how the two lines had run, only a few miles away; then he told her how he had carried his musket day after day over all this country, and where he had seen his battles. Sybil had everything to learn; the story came to her with all the animation of real life, for here under her eyes were the graves of her own champions, and by her side was a rebel who had stood under our fire at Malvern Hill and at South Mountain, and who was telling her how men looked and what they thought in face of death. She listened with breathless interest, and at last summoned courage to ask in an awestruck tone whether Carrington had ever killed anyone himself. She was relieved, although a little disappointed, when he said that he believed not; he hoped not; though no private who has discharged a musket in battle can be quite sure where the bullet went. “I never tried to kill anyone,” said he, “though they tried to kill me incessantly.” Then Sybil begged to know how they had tried to kill him, and he told her one or two of those experiences, such as most soldiers have had, when he had been fired upon and the balls had torn his clothes or drawn blood. Poor Sybil was quite overcome, and found a deadly fascination in the horror. As they sat together on the steps with the glorious view spread before them, her attention was so closely fixed on his story that she saw neither the view nor even the carriages of tourists who drove up, looked about, and departed, envying Carrington his occupation with the lovely girl. She was in imagination rushing with him down the valley of Virginia on the heels of our flying army, or gloomily toiling back to the Potomac after the bloody days at Gettysburg, or watching the last grand debacle on the road from Richmond to Appomattox. They would have sat there till sunset if Carrington had not at length insisted that they must go, and then she rose slowly with a deep sigh and undisguised regret.

===Chinese===
西比尔顿时一头扎进李家的事里，问个不住，卡林顿都乐于一一作答。他告诉她，整个战争期间他怎样倾慕李将军、追随着他。“我们都当他是我们的华盛顿，你知道；说不定他自己心里也存着几分这样的念头；”随后，西比尔想听听打仗和厮杀，他便在砾石小径上画了张简图，指给她看两军战线当年怎样排布——离这里不过几英里；又告诉她，他怎样日复一日背着步枪走遍这一片乡野，在哪些地方亲眼见过他那些仗。西比尔样样都得从头学起；这故事带着真实人生的全部生气涌到她面前，因为她眼皮底下就是她自家勇士们的坟茔，身边坐着的是一个叛军，一个在马尔文山、在南山挨过我们炮火的人，正在讲给她听：人到了死神面前是什么模样、心里想的是什么。她屏息凝神地听着，末了鼓起勇气，用一种敬畏的声气问卡林顿，他自己到底杀没杀过人。他说他信是没有；但愿没有；听了这话她松了口气，虽也有点失望——话虽如此，任何一个在战场上放过枪的列兵，谁也说不准自己的子弹究竟飞到了哪里。“我从没想杀过谁，”他说，“他们倒是无时无刻不想杀我。”西比尔又央着要听人家怎么想杀他，他便讲了一两桩大多数军人都有的那类经历：怎样挨过枪击，弹丸怎样撕破他的衣裳、带出血来。可怜的西比尔整个人都被慑住了，在那恐怖里觉出一种致命的魔力。两人一同坐在台阶上，壮丽的景色在眼前铺展，她的心思却全拴在他的故事上，风景固然看不见，连游客的马车驶到、张望一回、又驶走了，都没有瞧见——那些游客还艳羡着卡林顿，说他有这位可爱的人儿作伴。她在想象里同他一道，追着我们溃逃的大军冲下弗吉尼亚河谷；或在葛底斯堡那些流血的日子之后，郁郁地一路苦撑，退回波托马克河；或望着里士满通阿波马托克斯的大路上那最后一场总崩溃。若不是卡林顿末了执意说该走了，他们会一直坐到日头落山；她这才缓缓起身，深深叹了口气，毫不掩饰她的怅惘。

===Original===
As they rode away, Carrington, whose thoughts were not devoted to his companion so entirely as they should have been, ventured to say that he wished her sister had come with them, but he found that his hint was not well received.

===Chinese===
归途上，卡林顿的心思没能全放在同伴身上——本来是应该全放在的——便大着胆子说了一句，可惜她姐姐没同他们一道来，却发现这个话头不甚受欢迎。

===Original===
Sybil emphatically rejected the idea: “I’m very glad she didn’t come. If she had, you would have talked with her all the time, and I should have been left to amuse myself. You would have been discussing things, and I hate discussions. She would have been hunting for first principles, and you would have been running about, trying to catch some for her. Besides, she is coming herself some Sunday with that tiresome Mr. Ratcliffe. I don’t see what she finds in that man to amuse her. Her taste is getting to be demoralised in Washington.
